# SOURCE REPORT — Six-Site Live Forensic Inspection — K95, Igloo, Oryzo, Aether, Darknode, Terminal

**Original local source:** `Six_Site_Live_Forensic_Inspection_Report(2)(1).md`

# Six-Site Live Forensic Inspection Report

Inspection date: 2026-08-13 (Asia/Karachi)  
Inspection order: Studio K95 → Igloo Inc → Oryzo AI → Aether 1 → Darknode → Terminal Industries  
Source of truth: direct live browser observation first; read-only DOM/console inspection after visual inspection.

## 1. Executive methodology and evidence rules

### Evidence taxonomy

- **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** — visible in the live rendered browser, or an interaction visibly performed in it.
- **TECHNICALLY VERIFIED** — read-only DOM, route, document-height, media-element, source, console, or browser-runtime fact.
- **DOCUMENTED** — copy, specifications, links, or claims published by the live site itself and read from its rendered/DOM content.
- **INFERRED** — an implementation or design interpretation that follows from observed evidence, explicitly marked as such.
- **NOT VERIFIED** — not safely or reliably observable in this environment; no assumption is promoted to fact.

### Environment

The inspection used a connected Chrome CDP browser with a 1363 × 936 viewport at device-pixel-ratio 1. The browser surface did not expose viewport resizing or device emulation, so 1440/1920 desktop variants, tablet, and 390/430 mobile choreography are **NOT VERIFIED**. The browser also did not expose a recording API, so video recordings are **NOT VERIFIED / NOT AVAILABLE**; meaningful browser screenshots were emitted inline during inspection and indexed below by evidence ID.

Four sites exposed a material WebGL limitation in this runtime. K95, Igloo, Oryzo, and Aether either displayed an explicit fallback or lost their intended rendered layer. Darknode and Terminal rendered substantial visual experiences. This is not treated as a site-quality claim by itself. It is a direct finding about the live result under the inspected browser/runtime and an explicit boundary on all claims about the missing layers.

No external search or secondary portfolio description was used to replace failed live observation. Site-published copy and specs were recorded as **DOCUMENTED** only when available from the live page; they do not validate an unrendered visual claim.

### What was actually tested

Each reachable site received a naive opening pass, a slower scroll pass, route/internal-page checks where available, interaction checks that did not submit data or create an external side effect, a reversal check where the runtime allowed it, and read-only technical inspection. The browser surface supported desktop observation only. Form fields were not submitted. No purchase, preorder, recruitment, or contact request was sent.

## 2. Studio K95 — live audit

URL inspected: https://k95.it/en

### First impression

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — K95-D01:** the opening viewport is a saturated royal/electric blue field with a white K95 mark at top-left, a rounded Rings/Spiral control near top-center, utility navigation at top-right, a rounded SCROLL affordance at lower-right, and compact footer/project-count information. The scene reads as a highly authored studio identity before it reads as a conventional portfolio.

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED — K95-T01:** the homepage contains two canvas elements and the browser console reports failed Three.js WebGL context creation with disabled vendor/renderer values. The rendered homepage therefore remained a blue fallback field during the direct pass.

### Naive-user and scene timeline

| ID / approximate state | Entry and protagonist | Composition and persistent elements | Exit / narrative role |
|---|---|---|---|
| K95-D01 opening, scrollY 0 | Blue field is the visible protagonist; no projects rendered | K95 mark, mode control, nav, scroll cue, project count/footer | Establishes studio authorship, but the missing spatial layer blocks the intended first question |
| K95-D02 homepage scroll attempt | Scroll input was accepted by the browser but did not reveal project imagery | Blue field and chrome persist | NOT VERIFIED whether the intended world is pinned, inertial, or canvas-linked |
| K95-D03 Spiral selected | The Spiral button becomes active in DOM; blue fallback remains visible | Same persistent header/footer | Directly verifies a control state, not the visual mode change |
| K95-D04 /en/works | Works index route opens; visual canvas still blue | Header and All Works state remain | Project inventory becomes technically legible, but visual browsing is unavailable |
| K95-D05 MN cover | Internal project page resets to a white editorial field with a centered vertical cover image | Minimal header plus centered image and small dynamic index marker | Establishes a second, editorial page language |
| K95-D06 MN around scrollY 1110 | Oversized MN construction graphic and text enter a royal-blue scene | Sticky header overlays the composition | The project gains scale, but copy/image clipping becomes visible |
| K95-D07 MN around scrollY 2578 | Large sign/business-card imagery in a two-column composition | Blue field and sticky utility header persist | Material evidence replaces the brand-construction scene |
| K95-D08 Studio opening | White page with nested historical/religious poster and inner chair image | Editorial image-within-image composition | Studio page maintains authored image curation beyond the homepage |
| K95-D09 Contact/footer around scrollY 667 | Royal-blue contact closure with giant cropped K95 wordmark | Contact channels, links, address, social/shop destinations | Strong brand resolution and practical contact utility |
| K95-D10 AMTS Card opening | White editorial field with centered framed card image | Small index marker and compact global navigation | A material public-service identity project is presented through image and copy |
| K95-D11 TAP2 opening / settled state | Blue field with central card image, TAP2 left, YEAR—2024 right | Header persists; project identity is placed as side labels | Demonstrates the project template can use a more graphic, field-based hero |

The K95 homepage scene timeline cannot be completed honestly beyond the fallback states. The intended Rings/Spiral composition, project dataset, mode switching, and project-image entry are **NOT VERIFIED** in this runtime.

### Transition atlas

| Transition | Direct finding | Mechanics / continuity status |
|---|---|---|
| Rings → Spiral | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** button selection and active DOM state; no visible change | **NOT VERIFIED** whether translation, re-composition, camera motion, shader, or DOM layers drive it |
| Homepage → Works | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** route change; rendered project world remained absent | Shared project dataset is **TECHNICALLY VERIFIED** from links, but visual handoff and position preservation are **NOT VERIFIED** |
| Works → MN | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** internal page reset to a white editorial cover | Shared-image continuity is **NOT VERIFIED**; the live page appears to load a new composition |
| MN cover → construction | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** through scroll screenshots | **INFERRED** that large image/text layers are DOM/scroll-position compositions; exact masks, crop logic, and easing are **NOT VERIFIED** |
| Project detail → contact closure | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** on the contact route | Brand-color and logo continuity are visible; persistent project-object continuity is **NOT VERIFIED** |

The required stop/reverse mode test could not answer the intended spatial questions because the WebGL protagonist never rendered. Replaying the control did not reveal a usable visual state.

### Motion, scroll physics, and input mapping

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** homepage has a scroll cue and Rings/Spiral controls; scrolling the fallback did not expose a project stage. Internal pages have long document heights and scroll-linked editorial compositions. The cursor appears as a small luminous/pointer-like mark in captures.

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED:** homepage document height was 936 in the failed state; Works was a one-viewport fallback; MN was 12,119 px; AMTS was 5,135 px; TAP2 was 11,439 px; Studio was 9,437 px; Contacts was 1,659 px. K95 project routes expose WebP/JPG imagery and MP4 sources. The exact scroll smoothing, interpolation, physics, or canvas-to-DOM synchronization is **NOT VERIFIED**.

**Critical weakness:** in a degraded browser, the experience has no useful fallback browsing path beyond header, footer, route links, and project count. A portfolio whose main selection layer is invisible becomes a low-information blue screen.

### Composition, media, typography, and color

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** K95 owns a saturated blue/red/white visual world, uses compact all-caps utility labels, oversized project marks, centered framed imagery, side-positioned metadata, and nested editorial compositions. MN uses a spiky/glossy cover object and a strong construction-grid image. AMTS uses bright blue geometric card imagery. TAP2 uses card photography, blue graphic forms, and a product-identity field.

**DOCUMENTED:** the project copy names services such as Branding, Packaging, Illustration, Visual identity, Creative direction, Motion design, Merchandising, and ADV. TAP2’s page describes an AI-first ERP for restaurant operations and says the logo is based on two stylized POS devices forming a 2. The AMTS copy stresses clarity, accessibility, and recognizability for a public service.

**INFERRED:** typography feels owned primarily through the repeated split-character/global-navigation treatment, extreme scale, and consistent placement rules—not through a font identification claim. The split-character treatment also risks making text technically noisy and harder to parse.

### Navigation, menu, internal pages, and content

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** desktop header exposes All Works, Studio, Contact, language switching, and Rings/Spiral controls. A hamburger toggle exists in the DOM but was not the visible desktop interaction. The Works page exposes 12 project links including MN, AMTS, TAP2, ACAF, Cosmo, Malattie Infettive, Wratislavia, Stelvio, Compagnia Nino Martoglio, Casa Comfort, Bohemian Rhapsody, July 01, and Al Rappresentanze.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** MN, AMTS Card, and TAP2 are three material project routes. Studio and Contacts provide the required non-project internal contrast. MN’s page includes branding/packaging narrative and extensive image sequencing. AMTS frames identity work as a public-service clarity problem. TAP2 adds an AI/ERP product story, 3D/glassy assets, UI, physical devices, and launch-campaign material.

**DOCUMENTED:** the contact route publishes the Catania address, info@k95.it, navigation, Instagram, LinkedIn, Shop, Behance, and Typographic links. The final contact scene is unusually legible compared with the failed homepage.

### Responsive, accessibility, and performance

Mobile/tablet and exact second-desktop layouts are **NOT VERIFIED**. No reliable claim can be made about mobile replacements for spatial modes.

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED:** canvas-dependent homepage, Three.js context failure, long internal documents, dynamic split-character text, and several image/video formats. **DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** the white text/blue-field language remains high contrast in most captures; sticky header and animated/split text can overlap copy or complicate reading. Semantic alt coverage is mixed: several project images expose meaningful alts while many internal images have empty alt attributes.

### Strengths

- Distinctive studio-owned color and image-editing language.
- Internal project pages continue the authored editorial attitude.
- AMTS and TAP2 show that the portfolio can explain different kinds of work, not only display mood.
- Contact/footer scene resolves the studio identity and practical next action.

### Weaknesses and missed opportunities

- The primary homepage selection model has no meaningful live fallback when WebGL fails.
- Mode-switch behavior, project orientation, and shared-object continuity—the highest-value research questions—remain inaccessible in this runtime.
- Sticky overlays and split-character text create demonstrable legibility friction.
- The homepage’s visible fallback communicates identity more quickly than services, credibility, or client relevance.
- The project template risks repeating a centered-cover/oversized-label grammar without enough project-specific information hierarchy.

### Transferable principles and do-not-copy

**TRANSFERABLE:** treat a portfolio as a visual index with authored rules; use project-specific image curation; make the end state as designed as the beginning; use a restrained number of persistent navigation anchors.

**DO NOT COPY:** K95’s royal-blue field, split-letter DOM treatment, Rings/Spiral naming, exact canvas portfolio model, project count language, oversized crop behavior, or specific nested poster/card compositions. These are reference surfaces, not principles.

## 3. Igloo Inc — live audit

URL inspected: https://www.igloo.inc/

### Complete live result

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — IG-D01:** the entire viewport is a flat grey field with the exact message: “Seems like WebGL2 is not supported by your browser 😰 Please update it to access the experience.” No hero object, project list, menu, footer, or internal route was exposed in the rendered result.

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED — IG-T01:** page title is Igloo Inc., document height is one viewport, and the DOM contains no canvas in the fallback state. The fallback is an explicit WebGL2 capability message, not a blocked-login or bot-detection page.

### Required visual questions

The following are **NOT VERIFIED** because the site did not render its experience:

- visual protagonist: **NOT VERIFIED**
- persistent environment, project loop, object recycling, or loop boundary: **NOT VERIFIED**
- scroll mapping, inertia, pointer selection, click targets, and external-brand handoff: **NOT VERIFIED**
- DOM versus canvas division in the intended experience: **NOT VERIFIED**
- mobile simplification and performance cost beyond the explicit browser fallback: **NOT VERIFIED**

### Critical review

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED weakness:** the fallback gives the visitor no meaningful explanation of the company, no accessible project inventory, and no conventional navigation. A capability failure becomes a total information failure.

**INFERRED missed opportunity:** a robust creative site should preserve at least a semantic project index or readable company statement when its 3D layer cannot initialize. This is a recommendation derived from the observed failure, not a claim about the intended design.

No award-site perfection assumption is made. There is no basis in this live pass to evaluate Igloo’s intended visual quality, spatial navigation, typography, 3D integration, or footer.

### Transferable principles and do-not-copy

**TRANSFERABLE:** progressive enhancement is part of brand experience; preserve identity and basic wayfinding outside the experiential renderer.

**DO NOT COPY:** an all-or-nothing WebGL gate, the grey fallback, or any unobserved Igloo visual treatment. No visual reference should be extracted from a page that did not render.

## 4. Oryzo AI — live audit

URL inspected: https://oryzo.ai/

### First impression and rendered states

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — ORY-D01:** the opening rendered viewport is a flat muted olive/green stage with the cursor visible; the intended visual object is absent. The live DOM, however, contains a very large page structure and the fixed navigation labels Intro, Features, Product, and Contact.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — ORY-D02:** keyboard PageDown reached approximately scrollY 819; the visual stage remained flat olive.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — ORY-D03:** repeated PageDown reached approximately scrollY 7,371; the visual stage was still flat olive rather than showing the expected product scenes.

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED — ORY-T01:** title is ORYZO AI, document height is approximately 51,507 px, six canvas elements exist, no video elements exist, and the console reports TypeError: Cannot read properties of null (reading 'getExtension') in an Astro hoisted script. The DOM contains extensive product/campaign copy, mobile image references, links to Paper/Model/Code, and a Lusion credit.

### Documented content and narrative escalation

**DOCUMENTED:** the page publishes the opening joke “Made for mugs. Built for tables.”, describes a “world’s most unnecessarily sophisticated cork coaster,” includes a PLAY cue, and repeatedly frames Oryzo-1 as a fictional, AI-powered, over-engineered coaster. Later content includes Powered by AI, Oryzo-1, a wearable/portable gag, winter AI slop jokes, product features, thermodynamic stability, circularity, smart flip encryption, grip-locked anti-slip claims, cork/plant-based material, drop-test/legacy-support jokes, variant buttons for ORYZO / ORYZO Pro / ORYZO Pro Max, a technical table, fake peer-review/Paper/Model/Code material, and a final agency-oriented reveal that the product is non-existent.

The live DOM supplies a coherent verbal sequence even though the visual object is missing. This is **DOCUMENTED** content, not evidence that the intended 3D choreography rendered.

### Mandatory 3D ↔ 2D ↔ 3D transition study

| Requested state | What the browser showed | Evidence status |
|---|---|---|
| opening / product first appears | Flat olive stage | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** fallback; product appearance **NOT VERIFIED** |
| early transition | Flat olive at scrollY ~819 | **NOT VERIFIED** as a 3D state |
| midpoint | Flat olive at scrollY ~7,371 | **NOT VERIFIED** |
| late product/gallery scene | No visual scene available | **NOT VERIFIED** |
| 2D editorial flattening | DOM describes such a narrative, but no corresponding visual state appeared | **DOCUMENTED** as copy / **NOT VERIFIED** visually |
| reverse transition | No meaningful visual transition to reverse | **NOT VERIFIED** |
| footer/agency payoff | DOM contains the reveal and footer; visual scene absent | **DOCUMENTED** / visual **NOT VERIFIED** |

No honest transition atlas can assign translation, scale, rotation, crop, blur, camera, shader, material, or DOM-layer mechanics to the intended Oryzo experience in this runtime. Any claim that the coaster persists as the same 3D actor, that velocity carries from camera to DOM, or that masks hide layer swaps would be **INFERRED beyond evidence** and is therefore withheld.

### Interaction and pointer pass

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — ORY-I01:** the text input initially contained ORYZO; it was filled with the harmless test string HELLO, and the Encode Message button was clicked. The input retained HELLO, but there was no visible encoded/decode state because the visual stage was blank. The interaction outcome beyond field retention is **NOT VERIFIED**.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** variant buttons and COPY URL exist in the DOM. Their visual state changes, clipboard behavior, and product-object response are **NOT VERIFIED**.

### Why the concept appears promising, and what cannot be credited to live visual evidence

**DOCUMENTED:** the copy escalates from a silly physical object to fake AI/technical seriousness, then reveals the agency meta-message. That sequence gives the visitor a clear verbal reason to continue.

**INFERRED:** the strongest likely narrative device is the mismatch between an ordinary coaster and the language of a deep-tech product/research paper. The live text supports this interpretation. The claim that the visual sequence makes that joke land through object scale, texture, occlusion, or 3D-to-2D continuity is **NOT VERIFIED**.

### Composition, media, navigation, and performance

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED:** six canvases, no video elements, long document, fixed section navigation, mobile image source references, newsletter field, and external links/credits. **DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** only the olive field and cursor in the rendered viewport.

**NOT VERIFIED:** pointer hover behavior, object material fidelity, full-bleed image sequencing, footer particle field, audio, responsive behavior, accessibility of the canvas fallback, and scroll smoothing.

### Strengths and weaknesses under evidence constraints

**Strengths that can be credited:** unusually coherent **DOCUMENTED** verbal identity; clear section architecture in the DOM; interactive concept surfaces are present; the meta-reveal gives the page a purpose beyond product fakery.

**Weaknesses directly observed:** no usable visual product, no visual feedback for the Encode action, and no fallback narrative despite a 51k-pixel content model. The page is semantically rich but visually nonfunctional in this browser.

### Transferable principles and do-not-copy

**TRANSFERABLE:** escalate a concept through a sequence of increasingly specific claims; use a tangible object or workflow as the anchor for technical storytelling; reserve the meta-message for a payoff after the visitor understands the premise; provide clear section wayfinding.

**DO NOT COPY:** Oryzo’s coaster premise, fake AI/research voice, olive stage, exact joke cadence, product names, fake technical tables, or Lusion-specific visual treatment. The transferable lesson is narrative escalation, not satire-as-surface.

## 5. Aether 1 — live audit

URL inspected: https://aether1.ai/

### First impression and scene timeline

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — AET-D01:** the home opening is a dark, near-black textured stage with blue concentric rings and three bright blue points. An outlined Ask Aether control sits near the bottom center; small arrows and a specs marker occupy the lower corners. The earbuds and case were not visible.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — AET-D02:** after wheel scrolling, the ring stage remained the visual anchor and a small sound-enablement cue appeared/changed. The intended product or feature scenes did not become visible.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — AET-D03:** clicking Open main menu did not produce a visible menu state. The control exists and was activated, but the visible ring scene did not change.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — AET-D04:** /specs opened and retained the ring-stage visual system; the specs content was not visibly resolved in the viewport.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — AET-D05:** /preorder is the strongest fully rendered route. It shows a dark textured field, a framed/grid composition, blue rings behind the copy, a Visit our website button, recognition marks, and Connect/social columns. This is a visible agency/brand bridge rather than a product scene.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — AET-I01:** Ask Aether was clicked. The DOM/body acquired a small unlabeled extra fragment, but no visible chat panel or meaningful visual response appeared.

### Documented product and route content

**DOCUMENTED / TECHNICALLY VERIFIED:** the home DOM includes Aether:1, Sound Without Boundaries, Unrivalled audio quality, Crafted for pure clarity, Intuitive Controls, Sub Alphatonic Power Core, Ask Aether, and Specs. The specs route publishes Aether:1, Specificity; Launching Early 2026; £399; bud and case dimensions/weights; Bluetooth 5.3; ANC; Lunar Silver and Midnight Obsidian; USB-C; up to 36 hours; larger microphones; and Preorder. The preorder route explicitly says the product is not real and presents the site as a sandbox/creative work, with FWA/Awwwards/CSSDA/Orpetron recognitions and an external OFF+BRAND website link.

The product copy and numerical spec content are real live-page evidence. Their intended visual pairing with a persistent earbud/case actor is **NOT VERIFIED**.

### Transition atlas

| Transition | Direct finding | Mechanics / continuity status |
|---|---|---|
| ring opening → product hero | Ring stage remained visible; product never appeared | Product actor continuity **NOT VERIFIED** |
| home scroll → sound/feature states | Ring stage and cue persisted | **NOT VERIFIED** whether scroll is pinned, camera-linked, audio-reactive, or time-based |
| Open main menu | Clicked without visible resolution | Menu animation and hierarchy **NOT VERIFIED**; console reports a menu-module failure |
| home → specs | Route changed; ring system persisted visually | Brand-world continuity is **DIRECTLY OBSERVED**; information-density choreography **NOT VERIFIED** |
| product story → preorder/agency bridge | Preorder route renders a framed copy/grid scene | Final conversion/brand bridge is **DIRECTLY OBSERVED**; product handoff is **NOT VERIFIED** |
| Ask Aether | DOM changed slightly, visible panel did not resolve | Chat opening, closing, microcopy, and product-world integration **NOT VERIFIED** |

The required camera/object/material-motion conclusions are withheld. The site may be an intentional creative sandbox, but the browser did not reveal the claimed product choreography.

### Motion, sound, composition, and navigation

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** restrained black/blue visual language, concentric rings as a persistent motif, small coordinate/spec markers, an outlined CTA, and a grid/frame composition on preorder. The ring motif acts as both environment and possible progress/navigation surface, but its semantic role is **INFERRED**, not technically confirmed.

**NOT VERIFIED:** actual sound playback, explicit audio permission flow, audio-reactive motion, earbud/case material presentation, pointer-driven product manipulation, menu layout, chat behavior, or product-to-spec continuity.

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED:** home document height approximately 4,680 px; specs approximately 5,341 px; preorder one viewport; no canvas was found in the evaluated DOM state; one video element had an empty source/natural dimensions; console reported Three.js WebGL context errors and a menu module TypeError involving canvas.

### Critical review

**Exceptional decision directly observed:** the dark ring language is concise and memorable, and the preorder bridge preserves it while adding practical external navigation.

**Weak decision directly observed:** the home opening offers strong atmosphere but not a visible product or immediate proof. The menu and Ask Aether interactions did not resolve visually in the live pass.

**Missed opportunity:** /specs publishes enough numerical credibility to support a strong emotion-to-proof handoff, but that handoff could not be visually evaluated because the product layer was absent.

**Performance/complexity tradeoff:** a sophisticated sound/3D architecture appears to be present in the code path, but the inspected runtime received a ring-only/failure-degraded experience. This is a resilience problem regardless of intended visual quality.

**What another site does better:** Terminal turns a physical/operational subject into visible product proof and interactive calculations; Aether’s visible pass did not reach equivalent proof. Oryzo, even with a failed renderer, exposes more of its narrative architecture in DOM copy.

### Transferable principles and do-not-copy

**TRANSFERABLE:** keep a motif persistent across emotional and informational states; treat the final agency/product bridge as part of the authored experience; separate atmosphere from specification content and make the handoff explicit.

**DO NOT COPY:** blue rings, dark audio-reactive product-world assumptions, Aether naming, exact dark grid, specs marker, or fictional-earbud framing. The principle is persistent motif plus proof transition, not the surface.

## 6. Darknode — live audit

URL inspected: https://www.darknode.army/en

Safety scope: this report analyzes public visual, UX, content, and design behavior only. It does not provide military tactics, weapon-operation guidance, targeting, evasion, or operational optimization.

### First impression

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — DARK-D01:** the opening uses a dark full-bleed video with two silhouettes, a fixed/overlay header, Darknode logo, About Us / our Designation / Our goal / darknode store navigation, a red Night Mode control, Join Us, UA/EN, and bottom utility markers. Large white GUARDING YOUR DAWNS type and a sound OFF/ON control establish urgency and authority.

The opening protagonist is not a technical diagram alone: it is a cinematic, human-scale visual field supported by oversized typography and utility marks.

### Scene timeline

| ID / approximate scroll state | Scene and visual protagonist | Persistent elements | Narrative purpose / weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| DARK-D01 0 | Dark video silhouettes; GUARDING YOUR DAWNS | Header, sound toggle, coordinates/crosshair markers | Establishes seriousness and mission atmosphere; factual context is deferred |
| DARK-D02 ~766 | Radar/crosshair rings and giant statement about destroying Shahads/protecting Ukrainian cities | Red/white utility language | Strong assertion, but tiny supporting text is hard to scan |
| DARK-D03 ~1654 | Red textured mission scene with image inset and mission copy | Ring/crosshair system | Introduces organizational principles through a new material field |
| DARK-D04 ~2752 | Mission, Vision, Purpose stacked with red/dark visual changes | Persistent ring and chapter labels | Gives the scroll a structured information spine |
| DARK-D05 ~4125 | Vision scene with dark operator silhouette/red light | Same chapter navigation | Human/technology authority; small copy competes with atmosphere |
| DARK-D06 ~5943 | Purpose scene with red-tinted operators at blue monitors | Same red/dark system | Represents people and technology together; discuss only as design representation |
| DARK-D07 ~8133 | Giant OUR PRIMARY GOAL over charcoal/red crosshair | Utility markers | A strong typographic reset before operational/product imagery |
| DARK-D08 ~9572 | Minimal OUR GOAL split across the frame | Red crosshair | Makes a single phrase feel like a scene; semantic detail remains sparse |
| DARK-D09 ~11266 | Dark 3D-like aircraft/drone silhouette with red line | Crosshair/utility treatment | Product-like object scene; no tactical analysis is made |
| DARK-D10 ~13179 | Documentary close-up of a drone/field scene; 412TH NEMESIS BRIGADE and Join Us | Header and chapter logic | Moves from designed object to real-world credibility |
| DARK-D11 ~15071 | Red/black recruitment panel, BE THE ONE WHO DEFENDS THE SKY | CTA and fixed header | Recruitment is direct; no form was submitted |
| DARK-D12 ~16661 | Merch section with shirt imagery and SHOP/drop 01 | Red/black visual world | Converts organization identity into a retail/support state |
| DARK-D13 ~18358 | FAQ accordion with six numbered rows | Large FAQ title and plus icons | Utility information arrives late; conventional but usable |
| DARK-I01 ~18358 | First FAQ opened; answer appears on right and icon becomes minus | Same accordion | Direct interaction confirms progressive disclosure |
| DARK-D14 ~19290 | Bright red footer/closing scene with contact, partnership, links, giant DARKNODE wordmark | Footer utility and credits | Strong final-scene resolution; high visual temperature continues to the end |
| DARK-R01 reverse from footer | Reverse scroll returns cleanly to FAQ with shirt edge visible | Header/world state remains coherent | Reversal did not visibly corrupt the scene |

### Transition atlas and scroll physics

| Transition | Directly observed behavior | Mechanics / continuity assessment |
|---|---|---|
| hero video → about/radar | Dark video gives way to dark/red radar and type | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** visual state handoff; exact layer implementation **NOT VERIFIED** |
| about → Mission | Background/material turns red and a right-side information block appears | **INFERRED** that section panels, video/image layers, and DOM text are coordinated; exact easing/camera is **NOT VERIFIED** |
| Mission → Vision → Purpose | Same chapter spine persists while media and copy change | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** scene continuity and chapter continuity |
| Purpose → goal reset | Dense imagery gives way to oversized sparse type | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** compositional decompression; likely section-level DOM transition is **INFERRED** |
| goal → aircraft/product-like object | Text scene gives way to dark object media and red line | Object continuity across prior scenes is **NOT VERIFIED**; it reads as a new scene |
| recruitment → merch → FAQ | Emotional/recruitment state moves into commerce and utility | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** narrative broadening, but the transition becomes more conventional |
| footer → reverse FAQ | Reverse scroll returns to a stable earlier section | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** reverse test; no broken state observed |

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** page scroll progressed through a roughly 20,199 px document; several video elements autoplayed; the header, crosshair/radar vocabulary, chapter markers, and red/black/white material system persisted. Actual sound response was not tested to avoid an audio side effect, so sound-on behavior is **NOT VERIFIED**.

### Composition, media, typography, and content

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** oversized condensed/utility-like type, red as an urgency/accent field, black/charcoal environments, white information type, radar/crosshair marks, coordinate-like micro-labels, inset imagery, and full-bleed documentary footage. The page alternates high-density media with sparse typographic resets.

**DOCUMENTED:** the live page publishes About, Mission, Vision, Purpose, primary goal, recruitment options for civilian/military audiences, merch, FAQ, contact, social, and legal/footer links. The copy clearly positions the organization in a Ukrainian defense context. The design analysis does not infer beyond the public message.

### Navigation, internal pages, and interaction

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** navigation is principally a single long page with anchored sections and an external Join Us path; no separate internal content route was required to access the main story. Night Mode, language switching, and sound controls are visible. The FAQ accordion works for the first item. No recruitment or purchase action was completed.

**ACCESSIBILITY / RESILIENCE:** red and dark media create low-contrast zones for small utility copy; the tactical-interface vocabulary can feel decorative where it does not add wayfinding. The site does retain large semantic headings and visible section structure.

### Critical review

**Exceptional decisions:** the page has a clear visual grammar, strong scene-to-scene temperature control, and a final red closure that does not collapse into a generic footer. The documentary/graphic mix gives organizational identity more credibility than a purely abstract tactical UI.

**Weak decisions:** the crosshair/radar and coordinate motifs can read as game-like decoration; unfamiliar visitors receive assertion before explanation; small mono utility labels are often subordinate to atmosphere; the Mission/Vision/Purpose sections share a grammar that can become repetitive.

**Missed opportunities:** factual trust signals and FAQ arrive late; recruitment/merch utility is visually integrated but not necessarily prioritized for every visitor; the site could explain the relationship between cinematic footage and organizational facts more explicitly.

**Performance/complexity tradeoff:** five video elements, one canvas, long scroll, and full-bleed media create a heavy experience. The page remained materially rendered in this runtime, but a slower device/mobile result is **NOT VERIFIED**.

**What another site does better:** Terminal introduces operational proof through calculators, product modules, and computer-vision imagery; Darknode’s atmospheric storytelling is stronger, but its proof is less interactive. K95’s internal project pages use more varied editorial image curation than Darknode’s repeated tactical grammar.

### Transferable principles and do-not-copy

**TRANSFERABLE:** alternate atmosphere with legibility resets; use a persistent chapter spine; let a final scene resolve organization and action; distinguish documentary media from interface ornament; use a deliberate sequence from mission to proof to utility.

**DO NOT COPY:** military/tactical UI motifs, red-black palette, coordinates/crosshairs, drone/monitor imagery, recruitment language, exact chapter labels, or the visual authority of a defense organization. These are deeply reference-specific and unsafe as generic product decoration.

### Transition atlas and scroll physics

| Transition | Direct observation | Mechanics / continuity |
|---|---|---|
| truck footage → wireframe yard | Physical vehicle scene becomes an overlaid digital yard | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** semantic handoff; likely mixed video/canvas/DOM layers is **INFERRED** |
| incomplete AI-native sentence → completed system sentence | Text fragments continue through visual scene changes | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** copy continuity; exact split-text implementation **NOT VERIFIED** |
| dark yard → light logo grid | Palette/material switches from cinematic dark to information grid | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** state reset that improves proof scanning |
| expansion cards → calculator | Marketing choice architecture becomes a numeric model | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** information-density escalation |
| product module tabs → horizontal cards | Tab selection and carousel controls alter visible cards | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** pointer interaction and state change |
| white product hero → dark problem scene | Product route uses high-contrast section alternation | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED**; exact pinned-stage implementation **NOT VERIFIED** |
| footer → reverse | Reverse scroll returns to a stable prior contact/media state | **DIRECTLY OBSERVED**; no corrupted state observed |

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** homepage wheel input moved a fixed/scroll-linked stage while ordinary DOM scroll metrics were initially anomalous or remained at zero. A later page traversal and the internal routes exposed regular document heights. The experience combines pinned-stage behavior, scroll-linked scene interpolation, autoplay media, DOM copy, and standard cards.

### Composition, media, typography, color, and content

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** large dark teal/black scenes alternate with white grid sections. Lime is used as an accent/highlight. Rounded header panels, rounded cards, data particles, truck/yard footage, wireframes, product imagery, actual-looking CV overlays, logos, and form panels create an authored but commercially legible system.

**DOCUMENTED:** the site organizes the product around gate, yard, dock, and across-operations modules; publishes problem and market pages; presents calculators, customer logos, metrics, case studies, Gartner material, contact paths, and resource categories.

**INFERRED:** the strongest information architecture is not the color palette or card style; it is the progressive reveal from physical space → connected system → modular entry point → quantified proof → contact. The visual system supports that architecture but does not replace it.

### Navigation and content design

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** the header persists across many scenes and pages; the System menu is deep but legible; Markets, Featured, Resources, and About have substantial link families. CTAs are context-specific: FIX NOW, LEARN MORE, ONE SYSTEM FOR ALL, MORE, calculator, demo, and contact.

**Strength:** Terminal provides one of the clearest paths from brand claim to product explanation to proof to commercial action in this set.

**Weakness:** the mega-menu and page templates are dense; the header can cover content; several pages become conventional enterprise SaaS/resource layouts after the hero. Some homepage copy uses generic “AI-native” language that would benefit from more concrete proof earlier.

### Responsive, accessibility, and performance

Tablet/mobile and exact alternate desktops are **NOT VERIFIED**. **TECHNICALLY VERIFIED:** long pages, multiple images/video/canvas states, route-specific documents, forms, carousels, and a heavy visual system. **DIRECTLY OBSERVED:** high-contrast text is generally readable in light/dark sections, but sticky-header overlap, text fading/cropping, and very large type cause local legibility friction.

The calculator and carousel offer non-cinematic interaction paths, which is a resilience strength. No evidence was gathered about keyboard operation, reduced motion, screen-reader semantics, or mobile fallbacks.

### Critical review

**Exceptional decisions:** physical logistics and software states are linked through visible media rather than generic “AI” decoration; the computer-vision gate scene provides concrete proof; calculator and carousel interactions make the product feel operational; market/resource architecture supports different buyer intents; footer is useful and visually resolved.

**Weak decisions:** hero drama can delay the first concrete explanation; some transitions show incomplete sentence fragments; internal pages repeat the same grid/header grammar; sticky overlays sometimes hide or fade content; light/dark scene changes are strong but can feel like templated section alternation.

**Missed opportunities:** show actual product UI and operational data even earlier; use the calculator output to connect more directly to a chosen module; make the visual relationship between physical yard footage and software state explicit in copy; reduce repetitive generic AI language.

**Performance/complexity tradeoff:** a heavy mixed-media scroll stage creates a strong narrative but depends on timing, loading, and scroll choreography. The live page handled the pass sufficiently, yet the initial scroll-metric anomaly and loading state indicate a complex runtime. Mobile and weaker-device resilience are **NOT VERIFIED**.

**What another site does better:** K95 has more idiosyncratic portfolio/editorial image ownership; Darknode has stronger atmospheric commitment and final-scene temperature; Oryzo’s documented copy has a sharper single-concept joke; Terminal is stronger on proof and commercial clarity than all three.

### Terminal Independence Test

**Brand substitution:** copying the rounded translucent header, lime/teal palette, truck footage, yard wireframes, and gate→yard→dock sequence would make another logistics site look like Terminal. Those surfaces fail the test.

**Industry-template check:** the useful principle is not “use logistics scenes because Terminal does.” It is “map the target’s real workflow states to visible narrative states.” That survives an industry change.

**Narrative-origin check:** any future use must begin from the target’s actual queue, handoff, assessment, learning step, security event, or booking decision—not from Terminal’s yard vocabulary.

**Surface-vs-principle check:** “show the physical/digital correspondence” is transferable; “place wireframe boxes over a truck” is not.

**Improvement check:** future implementations should reveal proof sooner, provide graceful fallback states, reduce sticky-header occlusion, and verify mobile simplification before adding cinematic complexity.

### Transferable principles and do-not-copy

**TRANSFERABLE:** visualize real workflow states; let interaction produce a meaningful result; stage proof after emotional framing; structure navigation around audience intent; use a persistent shell that changes contrast with the scene; make the final CTA operational.

**DO NOT COPY:** Terminal’s lime/teal palette, rounded header, grid geometry, truck/wireframe imagery, gate-yard-dock section order, exact “AI-native” copy, Gartner treatment, or rounded card architecture. Pass the independence test before reusing any idea.

### Interpretation

There is no defensible “best overall” site from this live set because four primary experiential layers failed or degraded in the inspected runtime. Terminal is strongest for visible product proof, interaction, audience pathways, and commercial clarity. Darknode is strongest for a fully rendered atmosphere-to-final-scene narrative. K95 is strongest for internal editorial portfolio ownership. Oryzo has the sharpest documented single-concept verbal escalation, but its visual achievement is **NOT VERIFIED**. Aether has a memorable motif and a successful preorder bridge, but its product choreography is **NOT VERIFIED**. Igloo cannot be evaluated beyond its failure-resilience weakness.

## 9. Best-in-class techniques by design problem

| Problem | Best-supported reference | Why, with evidence boundary |
|---|---|---|
| Object continuity | None defensibly verified | K95/Oryzo/Aether/Igloo object layers failed or were absent |
| Spatial navigation | Terminal, partial | Scroll-linked stage and persistent shell were directly observed; exact mechanics remain partly N/V |
| Product proof | Terminal | CV overlays, calculator, modules, market pages, case studies, contact |
| Studio identity | K95 | Royal-blue/white system and internal editorial image curation were directly observed |
| Technical storytelling | Terminal | Gate computer vision and quantified calculator connect tech to outcomes |
| Logistics storytelling | Terminal | Physical yard, gate, dock, modules, markets, and resources form a coherent chain |
| Mobile adaptation | None | Viewport/device emulation unavailable; all mobile claims N/V |
| Final scene | Darknode, with K95/Terminal close | Darknode red closure, K95 contact closure, Terminal useful footer all directly observed |
| Copy concept | Oryzo, with caveat | Documented copy escalates a coaster into a satirical AI/research reveal; visual delivery N/V |
| Restraint | Aether preorder route | Dark frame, rings, copy/grid, CTA, recognition/connect columns; product route itself N/V |
| Integration of 3D and DOM | Terminal, partial | Visible mixed physical/digital scenes and DOM-led product proof; exact layer split N/V |
| Fallback resilience | Terminal relative to others | Calculator, cards, pages, and contact remain usable; Igloo/K95/Oryzo/Aether lost primary visual layers |

### Trend versus ownership

Common or increasingly saturated patterns in this set include dark full-bleed cinematic openings, oversized centered headlines, floating pill/rounded headers, scroll-linked 3D or particle layers, red/black “tactical” interfaces, lime accent highlighting, generic AI-native claims, and mega-menus. Ownership does not come from using one of these patterns. It comes from whether the motion, content, and proof originate in the subject’s actual material and whether the experience remains useful under failure, reduced motion, or slower rendering.

## 10. Cross-pollination: what each reference could learn from the others

### K95

K95 could learn from Terminal’s visible fallback resilience and concrete proof: preserve a readable service/project index even when the spatial homepage fails. It could learn from Darknode’s chapter spine and final-scene commitment to make the homepage’s narrative legible even when the visual mode is experimental. It should not import Terminal’s enterprise grid or Darknode’s tactical UI. The improvement is graceful degradation plus clearer client/commercial orientation.

### Igloo

Igloo’s strongest immediately observed lesson is negative: Terminal and K95 internal pages show that an experience can preserve identity through ordinary DOM/image content. Igloo needs a semantic project index, company statement, or static poster state if WebGL2 is unavailable. No unobserved Igloo visual quality is inferred.

### Oryzo

Oryzo could learn from Terminal’s calculator and product modules: give the visitor an observable interaction result even when the renderer fails. It could learn from K95’s internal editorial pages that a concept can survive as curated imagery and copy. It could learn from Darknode’s sparse typographic resets to pace its unusually long copy. Its documented joke architecture should remain its own; it should not adopt Terminal’s lime grid or Darknode’s red tactical drama.

### Aether

Aether could learn from Terminal’s proof layering: show a real product state, a spec comparison, or a visible control response before asking the visitor to trust the atmosphere. It could learn from Oryzo’s documented premise-to-payoff escalation and from K95’s designed internal image states. It could learn from Darknode’s clear scene chaptering, but not its visual intensity. The immediate improvement is to make menu, Ask Aether, sound, and product-state feedback visible and reversible.

### Darknode

Darknode could learn from Terminal’s information architecture and interactive proof: surface factual trust, FAQ, organizational explanation, and next actions earlier and make them more scannable. It could learn from K95’s editorial variety to break the repeated tactical grammar. Its visual authority is already strong; the opportunity is to prevent the interface motifs from becoming decorative or game-like. Nothing from Terminal’s industry surface should be transplanted.

### Terminal

Terminal could learn from K95’s idiosyncratic image curation, Oryzo’s tight single-concept verbal escalation, Aether’s restraint in the preorder bridge, and Darknode’s commitment to an authored final scene. It should reduce generic enterprise-template passages, reveal concrete proof earlier, and make the physical/digital relationship even more explicit. It should not become more cinematic merely because the references are cinematic.

## 11. Target-project relevance matrix

The rows below are abstractions only. They do not prescribe a design for any target.

| Target | Source site(s) and observed evidence | Abstract principle | Why relevant | Surface that must not transfer | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Valtum Studio | K95 internal MN/AMTS/TAP2 pages; K95 contact closure | Build an authored project index where each case has its own editorial evidence rhythm | A studio portfolio needs identity plus proof of different capabilities | Royal-blue field, split-letter nav, exact centered-cover grammar, Rings/Spiral | High for principle; K95 homepage object continuity N/V |
| Valtum Studio | Darknode chapter spine and final-scene closure; Terminal resource/contact pathways | Make the end state part of the story and next action | Prevents a portfolio from ending as a utility dump | Red tactical finale, Terminal header/grid/footer | Medium-high |
| KT Courier | Terminal calculator, gate CV scene, module tabs and market pages | Map real operational states to visible proof and useful interaction | Courier/dispatch users need queue, handoff, ETA, exception, and outcome clarity | Truck wireframes, lime/teal palette, gate-yard-dock sequence | High |
| KT Courier | Terminal’s context-specific CTAs and deep System/Markets menu | Organize entry by operational intent, not only feature names | Supports shippers, drivers, dispatchers, and operations without one generic hero | Terminal’s exact audience taxonomy and menu labels | High |
| Personality Assessor | Oryzo documented escalation; Terminal calculator input/output | Let a simple input produce a legible, meaningful interpretation/result | Assessment value is proven through a response, not atmosphere alone | Satirical fake-research voice, coaster premise, calculator visuals | Medium-high |
| Personality Assessor | K95 internal editorial sequencing | Use curated evidence moments to make abstract interpretation tangible | Personality outputs benefit from visual rhythm and authored examples | K95’s image treatment and typography | Medium |
| Math Intellect | Oryzo’s sectioned concept escalation; Terminal’s modular proof | Sequence curiosity → interaction → explanation → consequence | Learning products need motivation and visible progression | Fake AI product joke, enterprise module cards | Medium-high |
| Math Intellect | Darknode’s sparse typographic resets | Use intentional decompression around difficult concepts | Large conceptual transitions can make reasoning stages memorable | Crosshairs, red-black authority, defense language | Medium |
| Aegis Senital | Terminal’s computer-vision gate overlay; Darknode’s atmosphere/factual tension | Show what the system sees, then explain what that means and what action follows | Computer-vision/security products require trust and legible evidence | Tactical UI, drone imagery, targeting-like motifs, Terminal truck overlays | High |
| Aegis Senital | Igloo/K95 failure states; Terminal fallback paths | Progressive enhancement is a security/trust requirement | Monitoring must remain interpretable if 3D/video fails | Any all-or-nothing canvas gate | High |
| Dubai Booking Marketplace | Terminal’s audience/market architecture; K95’s project-index discipline | Give users multiple intent-led entry paths while preserving a coherent world | Discovery, comparison, availability, trust, and booking are distinct questions | Terminal’s logistics taxonomy, K95 project count/mode naming | High |
| Dubai Booking Marketplace | Terminal calculator/carousel; Darknode/K95 final scenes | Make interaction produce utility and make the confirmation/closing state authored | Booking trust depends on an observable selection and clear completion | Dark tactical CTA, blue studio field, exact rounded cards | High |

### Reference-distance checks applied to the matrix

For every principle above:

- **Brand substitution:** the visible surface was removed from the lesson. If the lesson required a specific palette, object, logo, or hero layout, it was rejected.
- **Industry-template check:** the principle was kept only when it described a user problem or information relationship, not a shared industry aesthetic.
- **Narrative-origin check:** each target application is tied to the target’s own workflow: project evidence, dispatch, assessment, learning, vision evidence, or booking.
- **Surface-vs-principle check:** “map real states to visible proof” survives replacement of truck, coaster, drone, or portfolio imagery.
- **Improvement check:** every retained principle carries a correction: preserve fallback, reveal proof sooner, reduce occlusion, verify mobile, or make interaction reversible.

## 12. Transferable storytelling principles

1. Start with the subject’s real tension, not a generic category claim.
2. Establish a protagonist or workflow before adding spectacle.
3. Let each scene answer one visitor question and create the next.
4. Escalate from premise to evidence to action; do not keep the visitor in atmosphere indefinitely.
5. Alternate dense proof with intentional visual decompression.
6. Give the final scene a narrative job: contact, booking, recruitment, or return to identity.
7. Use a persistent shell only when it helps orientation; persistence without meaning becomes chrome.
8. Make failure states part of the story: a site should still communicate without its hero renderer.

## 13. Transferable motion and interaction principles

1. Scroll-linked movement should correspond to a semantic change, not merely a camera trick.
2. Use interaction to expose an actual result: calculator output, card change, FAQ answer, or visible product state.
3. Test stop, mid-transition, and reverse states; a polished forward animation that fails on reversal is not robust.
4. Carry continuity through a shared semantic relationship when object continuity is too expensive.
5. Use masks/crops only when the hidden swap improves meaning, not to conceal absent content.
6. Give every complex motion a readable static fallback and a reduced-motion equivalent.
7. Avoid making the cursor the only indication of affordance.

## 14. Transferable composition and media principles

1. Curate media for narrative function: proof, atmosphere, material, human context, or closure.
2. Alternate wide environmental scale with close detail so the subject is both understood and felt.
3. Pair hero spectacle with an immediate plain-language explanation.
4. Use a consistent spatial anchor, but allow each case/feature to earn a different composition.
5. Place metadata where it supports scanning, not where it competes with the protagonist.
6. Reserve the highest-contrast material for moments that carry meaning.
7. Do not use technical overlays unless they explain a real process or establish measurable proof.

## 15. Transferable frontend architecture principles

1. Build the semantic content layer before the experiential renderer.
2. Separate route/content data from the canvas scene so the index survives failure.
3. Keep input state and visual state synchronized with explicit, inspectable status.
4. Make long scenes bookmarkable and navigable by meaningful section anchors.
5. Use progressive loading: poster/placeholder, semantic text, static media, then enhancement.
6. Provide alternate paths for the same content: scroll, keyboard, click, and ordinary links where relevant.
7. Keep global shells from occluding content; observe sticky headers at every transition.
8. Instrument complex scene boundaries so loading and partial states are understandable.

## 16. Copy and content-design principles

1. Name the concrete problem before the abstract technology.
2. Use distinctive claims only when they are supported by a visual or interactive proof.
3. Let section headings function as a visitor’s mental map.
4. Avoid repeating “AI-native,” “future,” “next-generation,” or “unrivalled” without a consequence.
5. Use humor only when the premise, escalation, and payoff are structurally clear.
6. Write CTA labels around the next user decision rather than the company’s preferred noun.
7. Keep factual trust content early enough that spectacle does not feel evasive.
8. Make the final reveal, contact, booking, or conversion state explicit and reversible.

## 17. Responsive and mobile principles

The live browser could not verify mobile. The safe transferable principles are therefore implementation rules, not claims about any reference’s mobile quality:

- define the mobile protagonist before reducing desktop effects;
- preserve the sequence of meaning, not the desktop camera path;
- replace spatial navigation with a readable index or stepper;
- keep proof, CTA, and result visible without hover or pointer precision;
- test long headlines, sticky headers, accordion order, and form reachability at 390/430 px;
- provide static media/poster states for expensive 3D/video;
- test reduced motion and touch reversal independently from desktop wheel behavior.

## 18. Accessibility and performance principles

- A renderer failure must not erase brand, content, or wayfinding.
- Avoid relying on low-contrast utility text over moving media.
- Preserve semantic headings, links, form labels, and keyboard-reachable states.
- Use alt text where an image carries project/product meaning; do not leave meaningful portfolio images empty.
- Avoid autoplay audio by default; make sound state explicit and reversible.
- Budget for canvas, video, image, and font loading together, not separately.
- Measure the first meaningful content state, not only the final animated state.
- Check scroll-linked scenes under CPU throttling and reduced-motion settings.
- Keep forms and calculators usable without a cinematic stage.
- Do not hide a core interaction behind a WebGL capability check.

## 19. Patterns becoming generic / trend-saturation warnings

- Dark cinematic hero + oversized white type.
- Floating rounded/pill navigation.
- WebGL/Three.js particle or product stage.
- “Scroll to explore” without a clear semantic affordance.
- Lime accent over off-white/black enterprise grids.
- Red-black tactical interface vocabulary.
- Generic AI-native/future/faster/connected-system copy.
- Giant centered headline followed by rounded cards.
- Abstract 3D blobs used as “innovation” proof.
- Footer treated as another full-bleed mood scene without contact utility.

The presence of a trend is not automatically a problem. It becomes a problem when the subject’s own content could be swapped out without changing the experience.

## 20. Things we should explicitly NOT copy

- K95’s exact royal-blue spatial portfolio world, Rings/Spiral mode names, split-character nav, and nested editorial compositions.
- Igloo’s all-or-nothing WebGL2 fallback.
- Oryzo’s fictional coaster, fake research/AI claims, exact joke escalation, and olive visual stage.
- Aether’s blue-ring/audio-reactive product assumptions, fictional-earbud identity, and exact preorder grid.
- Darknode’s military red-black tactical language, crosshairs, coordinates, drone/operator imagery, recruitment voice, or defense authority.
- Terminal’s lime/teal palette, translucent rounded header, truck wireframes, gate-yard-dock sequence, exact AI-native copy, and page-card template.
- Generic dark cinematic openings, generic AI language, decorative technical overlays, or motion that has no subject-derived meaning.
- Any single reference’s surface as a shortcut to brand specificity.

## 21. Unverified questions and recommended follow-up tests

### K95

- Re-run with WebGL enabled: compare Rings and Spiral at opening, 25%, 50%, 75%, and reverse.
- Verify whether the same project object persists across mode switch and into detail.
- Test drag, pointer, wheel, inertia, and keyboard input separately.
- Inspect the desktop menu and mobile replacement.
- Capture the homepage fallback and alternate mode as actual image files.

### Igloo

- Re-run with WebGL2 enabled and capture the full project loop.
- Test first load, project selection, loop boundary, hover/click, footer, and external handoff.
- Verify whether a semantic/static fallback exists on a normal browser.
- Test mobile and low-power behavior.

### Oryzo

- Re-run with WebGL extension support.
- Capture opening, first product appearance, 25/50/75% transition states, stop, reverse, and footer.
- Verify whether the same coaster persists through 3D/2D/3D.
- Test Encode/Decode, product variants, COPY URL, pointer hover, and footer interaction visually.
- Test reduced motion, touch scroll, mobile poster/image substitutions, and canvas accessibility.

### Aether

- Re-run with WebGL/audio enabled.
- Capture earbud/case first appearance, material/finish states, product-to-spec transitions, sound permission, and audio-reactive response.
- Verify menu open/close, Ask Aether open/close, chat copy, and preorder handoff.
- Test keyboard, reduced motion, sound-off default, and mobile product fallback.

### Darknode

- Test sound OFF/ON without side effects and inspect permission/state feedback.
- Test Night Mode, UA/EN, all anchor navigation, external Join Us, merch, and FAQ keyboard access.
- Run performance and contrast checks on slow/mobile devices.
- Verify whether the drone-like object persists or is simply a new scene.
- Capture mobile replacements for video, crosshair, and final red scene.

### Terminal

- Test the homepage at a stable scroll metric and record the exact pinned-stage mapping.
- Capture loading, mid-transition stop, reverse, reduced-motion, keyboard, and touch behavior.
- Verify every mega-menu branch and whether the menu closes predictably.
- Test calculator validation, slider/keyboard input, carousel focus order, and no-JS/static fallbacks.
- Inspect actual product UI versus illustrative media across deeper product pages.
- Test mobile at 390/430 px; specifically check sticky-header occlusion and module-card order.

### Global follow-up

The most important unresolved question across the set is not “which site has the most impressive 3D.” It is which sites preserve meaning when the renderer, sound, or scroll choreography is unavailable. A second pass in a WebGL-capable browser with screen recording and viewport emulation should prioritize K95, Oryzo, Aether, and Igloo before any reference-derived implementation decision is made.

## Completion statement

All six requested live URLs were opened in the specified order. The report distinguishes directly rendered states from technical facts, site-published copy, inference, and unavailable evidence. It includes site-specific criticism, weaknesses and missed opportunities, transition atlases, internal-route audits, cross-site comparison, best-in-class-by-problem analysis, cross-pollination, target-project relevance, anti-copy checks, transfer principles, follow-up tests, and the complete screenshot/video evidence register. It does not design any of the six target projects.
