# SOURCE REPORT — Four-Site Live Forensic Inspection — Outfit, Inversa, Jasmine, Osmo

**Original local source:** `live-forensic-inspection-report.md`

# Four-Site Live Forensic Inspection

Inspection order: [Outfit](https://outfit.hellohello.is/) → [Inversa](https://inversa.com/) → [Jasmine Gunarto](https://jasminegunarto.com/) → [Osmo](https://www.osmo.supply/)

Inspection date: 2026-08-13, Asia/Karachi

## Evidence legend

- **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** — seen in the rendered browser or during a real user interaction.
- **TECHNICALLY VERIFIED** — read from the live DOM, computed styles, browser media state, loaded asset state, or runtime URL.
- **DOCUMENTED** — stated by the site itself in visible copy, labels, metadata, or content structure.
- **INFERRED** — a design or implementation interpretation derived from the observations.
- **NOT VERIFIED** — not established during this run; it is not a failure claim.

## 1. Method and operating conditions

The browser viewport was approximately 1363 × 936. Each site was opened in a cloud Chromium session. The run began with Outfit’s first cold-load view, before refresh or revisit, because the loader was a high-priority research target. For every site, the sequence was: initial load capture; patient settling period; naive navigation and scroll; slow forensic inspection; hover, click, stress, and reversal states where available; internal pages; technical inspection; critical review; cross-site synthesis.

The connection was deliberately treated as slow. Blank frames, transitional fades, partially populated media surfaces, and low-opacity states were not diagnosed as failures while assets or animation were still progressing. Osmo’s showcase, showcase detail, Vault, and resource preview each required materially longer waits after the DOM was complete; those pages eventually settled and are recorded as **DIRECTLY OBSERVED** slow transitions, not broken pages.

No checkout, payment, newsletter submission, account creation, login, external social post, or other irreversible action was attempted. Outfit’s add-to-bag, quantity, and remove-item controls were used because they are local, reversible cart interactions.

Responsive resizing, mobile behavior, keyboard accessibility, network waterfall timings, Lighthouse metrics, and authenticated/member-only states are **NOT VERIFIED**.

## 2. Executive findings

**INFERRED:** The four sites demonstrate four different kinds of authorship:

| Site | Primary authored experience | Strongest lesson | Readiness after patient loading |
|---|---|---|---|
| Outfit | A brand world that behaves like a shop | Make the loader, product system, hover language, and cart feel like one identity | READY |
| Inversa | An operational ecology narrative | Turn a complex service into a sequence of legible states and proof | READY for home/core; Origin’s intermediate mission visuals only partly verified |
| Jasmine Gunarto | An editorial motion-design portfolio | Let typography, pacing, project metadata, and work artifacts carry the identity | READY |
| Osmo | A productized interaction library | Show the capability, then organize it into a navigable, monetizable system | READY after extended settling waits |

**INFERRED:** Outfit is the most complete brand-to-commerce loop. Inversa is the clearest example of service logic becoming visual narrative. Jasmine is the most deliberately typographic and portfolio-editorial. Osmo is the most explicit about turning interaction craft into a reusable product.

## 3. Outfit

Site: [outfit.hellohello.is](https://outfit.hellohello.is/)

### 3.1 First cold-load loader — priority target

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-01:** The first capture, taken about 80 ms after navigation, showed a full black screen with a small white `000` loader mark. The body was still loading. No refresh or revisit occurred before this capture.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-02:** At roughly 780 ms, the black loader remained, while the homepage’s text and product content were already present in the DOM. The browser reported `readyState: complete`, but the page still carried a loader/transition state. This is a deliberate visual gate rather than evidence that the page had failed to load.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-03:** After the loader released, the homepage became a cream field with red `++` branding, a huge red `OUTFIT®` wordmark, a red horizontal rule, fixed navigation, theme dots, and the product grid. The first cold-load transition was therefore: black `000` state → branded reveal → full shop.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-04:** On a later cached reload, the first frame again showed black with `000`; after the page content was ready, a stacked-image transition appeared behind a white `OUTFIT` wordmark with a `091` counter before the cream homepage returned. The cached choreography differed from the first cold capture. The exact state-selection logic is **NOT VERIFIED**, but the loader is clearly stateful and not a generic browser spinner.

### 3.2 Naive-user pass and information architecture

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-05:** The homepage immediately communicates a shop, not an agency case study: the large wordmark occupies the top, the fixed nav exposes `Shop` and `Bag (0)`, and product cards begin below the intro.

**DOCUMENTED — OUT-06:** The intro states that the store and signature collection were created by the `++hellohello` team and were designed as a celebration of collective creativity and apparel. It exposes `VISIT ++ WEBSITE`, `SHIPPING & RETURNS`, the year, and the collection.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-07:** The product grid contains products such as `Off by Design`, `Kerned Confidence`, `Specimen No. HH01`, `Grid System Go`, `Neutral Grotesk`, `Red Dot Not Award`, `Gridlocked`, `Hello Week 001/002`, `Monochrome Manifest`, `Positive Space`, `Whitespace Matters`, and `Command + K`. The composition changes from a regular opening row into asymmetrical rows with large editorial panels and whitespace.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-08:** The closing section repeats the line `Made to be worn. Or judged. Or both.` in oversized red type, pairs it with `©26`, then ends with address, privacy, social, and studio navigation. The commercial experience closes as a brand statement rather than a generic footer.

### 3.3 Hover, motion, and stress behavior

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-09:** Hovering the first product card revealed a second campaign image through a circular red `VIEW MORE` cursor treatment. On leaving, the card returned to its original image. The alternate image was initially bright/contrast-treated and scaled; the reveal was animated rather than a hard image swap.

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED — OUT-10:** The product card uses a custom cursor state, a second image, a clip-path reveal, a scale transform, a high-brightness/high-contrast treatment, and a roughly 500 ms cubic-bezier transition. The site uses a fixed custom cursor, Lenis-related classes, fixed navigation, and a fixed menu layer. The homepage contains zero canvas elements and zero video elements at inspection time.

**INFERRED — OUT-11:** Outfit’s hover language is a compact version of the brand thesis: the product is both merchandise and a graphic artifact, and the cursor behaves like an editorial annotation.

### 3.4 Product and cart paths

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-12:** `/product/off-by-design` opens with a short red transition containing the white `OUTFIT.` wordmark, then settles into a split product page: large product media on the left and product details on the right. The detail includes `Return to Shop`, price, copy, color, size chips, quantity, and `Add to Bag`.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-13:** Selecting size `M` and color `Black` activated the add control. Clicking `Add to Bag` changed the state to `Adding to Bag…`, then updated the fixed nav to `Bag (1)`.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-14:** `/bag` opens with a red transition and settles into a large `YOUR BAG` page. The populated state shows the item, color, size, quantity controls, `Delete`, subtotal, tax/shipping note, and `Checkout ↗`. Checkout was not entered.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-15:** Removing the item produced a transient animated quantity-counter state, then the empty bag displayed `Not even one thing? That’s sad.` with a screensaver image and the footer. The local cart interaction reversed cleanly.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-16:** `/product/kerned-confidence-1` showed a cap product with alternate media and the same commerce grammar. `/product/gridlocked` showed a tote product with the same split layout and a direct `Sold out` state.

**NOT VERIFIED — OUT-17:** The product page showed `$36.50` for Off by Design, while the populated bag later displayed `$34` for the selected item. This may be variant, tax, discount, or API state; the cause was not established and should not be called a confirmed pricing bug without a fresh commerce-data check.

### 3.5 Technical and critical review

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED — OUT-18:** The live page loaded Next.js-style chunks, Google Tag Manager assets, Neue Haas Grotesk Text Pro regular/medium/bold font files, Shopify CDN product media, and six `/preloader/image-01.jpg` through `/preloader/image-06.jpg` assets. Below-fold images were lazy-loaded. The page used fixed overlays and no canvas/video on the inspected homepage.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OUT-19:** Early below-fold alternate images were still arriving during the first scroll, but continued loading and settled. This matched the slow-network condition and was not treated as a site failure.

**INFERRED — OUT-20:** The loader is the most strategically valuable element to study. It establishes a controlled black-to-cream threshold, makes the brand name the transition object, and turns waiting into a signature moment. The strongest part is not the `000` itself; it is the continuity between loader, wordmark, cursor, product imagery, and cart.

**INFERRED — OUT-21:** Main risks are overusing a blocking preloader, keeping custom cursor meaning on touch devices, and allowing price/variant state to feel ambiguous. The current desktop experience is coherent and complete after assets settle.

## 4. Inversa

Site: [inversa.com](https://inversa.com/)

### 4.1 Load, hero, and narrative structure

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-01:** The first roughly 120 ms was blank white. Around 1.2 s, a dark grid/loading screen appeared with `LOADING…`, a horizontal rule, `PHASE 0%`, and `FREQ 16HZ`. After the extended wait, the page settled into the hero.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-02:** The hero is a full-bleed aerial landscape/river image with fixed `INVERSA` at top-left and `Menu` at top-right. The large white claim is `Invasions move fast. Be faster.` with a bright yellow `EXPLORE ORIGIN` action.

**DOCUMENTED — INV-03:** The page explains a service that combines economics, ecology, and efficiency around invasive-species management. The visible information architecture includes `Damaged Ecosystems`, `One Click Invasive Management`, `Restoration`, the three-part Economics/Ecology/Efficiency system, workforce tools, a quote slider, contact, connect, subscribe, and footer.

### 4.2 Custom scroll and state choreography

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED — INV-04:** The body is hidden behind a custom `.scroll.lenis` container with an internal scroll height of roughly 11,647 px. The main wrapper is sticky, the header and cursor are fixed, and the page does not use CSS scroll snap. The site loads Nuxt-style `/_nuxt/` assets and an Inversa style bundle; exact framework internals beyond the observed asset structure are **NOT VERIFIED**.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-05:** The efficiency section combines an angular-clipped body image, an environmental image, and copy describing field specialists using Origin’s digital and physical tools in real time.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-06:** The workforce section begins with the large claim `Creating the modern invasive management workforce`, followed by a dark line-art scene of field workers and a circular `SCROLL` cue.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-07:** The `001 · BODY CAMS` state shows a field specialist, a `MODELZ77` card, an `AI CONFIRMATION 99% CERTAINTY` card, and a scan/verification visual. The copy explains documenting fieldwork and verifying removals for transparency and safety.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-08:** The `002 · PREDICTIVE HEATMAPS` state shows a bright yellow invasive-hotspots map, a field specialist with a tablet, and a data-source card. The copy explains assigning missions using habitat and predicted-density models.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-09:** Scrolling backward from the heatmap state returned to the Body Cams state with a coherent reverse transition. This is direct evidence that the process sequence is reversible, not merely a collection of unrelated scroll illustrations.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-10:** The `003 · FIELD APP` state shows a specialist with a phone and an activity/notification card containing a named field operator, active status, removals, and CPUE data. The copy explains priority zones, capture logging, and instant operations sync.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-11:** The evidence/quote area displays a lime counter such as `001/003`, large partnership quotes, and a later `002/003` quote state. The footer resolves into black CONTACT, CONNECT, and SUBSCRIBE panels, followed by a large lime/black `INVERSA` close.

**INFERRED — INV-12:** Inversa’s main innovation is not simply dark mode or line art. It is the conversion of a complex operational system into a few named, reversible field states: detect, predict, verify, act, report.

### 4.3 Internal pages

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-13:** `/origin` opens slowly into a dark topographic/contour-map atmosphere with the heading `Environmental intelligence for invasive species management.` It documents `Field Integration`, `001 Detect`, `002 Deploy`, `003 Deliver`, `One click Invasive management`, `Invasive Hotspot`, `Initiating a Mission`, `Mission in Progress`, `Removals`, `Econ Metrics + ROI`, `ROI & Safety`, and Origin product variants.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-14:** The Origin page’s aerial Field Integration scene was visible after internal scrolling, with copy anchored beside the image. A large jump to the footer initially produced a transitional blank state, then settled after an additional wait into the same contact/connect/subscribe treatment as the homepage.

**NOT VERIFIED — INV-15:** Because the custom scroll container’s control was inconsistent during the slow run, the intermediate Origin mission visualizations were not all independently observed in the rendered browser. Their headings and content structure are present in the DOM; their full visual choreography remains not verified.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-16:** `/case/lionfish-management-program` settles into a dark case-study opening with the title `Lionfish Management Program`, the claim `Turning invasive into impact through inversa’s integrated lionfish management program`, location metadata, a narrow editorial body column, quote, partner/context copy, and outcome figures: `$2.1M+`, `40k+`, `14–20X`, and `267` fishers.

**DOCUMENTED — INV-17:** The case-study copy names Belize and Colombia, describes the lionfish problem, and references NOAA, ORRAA, Conservation International, permits, fishers, supply chain, and the economic/ecological alignment of the program.

### 4.4 Technical and critical review

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED — INV-18:** The homepage loads a Nuxt-style bundle, Storyblok-hosted images, SVG illustrations, an invasive-hotspot PNG, and an autoplaying muted loop video at `https://inversa.com/video/body-cam.mp4`. No canvas was observed.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — INV-19:** The loader and pinned process sections remained visually active for an extended period but settled; no persistent site runtime failure was observed.

**INFERRED — INV-20:** Inversa has the best service-explanation model of the four. Its product is difficult to understand abstractly, so the site gives the visitor a controlled operational journey and repeatedly ties technology to field outcomes.

**INFERRED — INV-21:** The main risk is interaction fragility: custom scrolling, sticky scenes, and long transitions create more opportunities for a user to misread a temporary frame as a broken state. The site needs especially clear fallback behavior and motion-reduction handling, both **NOT VERIFIED** here.

## 5. Jasmine Gunarto

Site: [jasminegunarto.com](https://jasminegunarto.com/)

### 5.1 Load and home experience

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-01:** The first frame was blank white, followed by a dark/blank preloader period. After waiting, the homepage settled into a pale-gray editorial canvas with location/time/coordinate metadata, fixed navigation, and a huge condensed `JASMINE GUNARTO` wordmark.

**DOCUMENTED — JAS-02:** The home page states `MOTION DESIGN 2026`, `A VISUAL DESIGNER`, and `PURPOSEFUL DESIGN CREATING MOTION NARRATIVE THROUGH ANIMATION WITH MEANING`.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-03:** The hero uses enormous black display type, small utility metadata, a centered project-media panel, and split descriptors such as `A VISUAL` and `DESIGNER`. The visual system is typographic first; imagery and video enter as framed editorial artifacts.

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED — JAS-04:** The body is not the active scroller. A `.main-content-wrapper.lenis` container holds roughly 10,022 px of content while the viewport remains fixed. The custom scroller moved through distinct scenes while the header remained fixed.

### 5.2 Scroll chapters and interaction

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-05:** The home scroller passed through the hero, an image-led transition, the dark-brown statement `CREATING MOTION WITH MEANING`, the Featured Works area, the black `BREAK` section, and the footer/`LET’S TALK` close.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-06:** The Featured Works area exposes Arizona Diamondbacks, Home in a Hot Pot, PreCollege ’25, and PINC with category tags such as Design, Motion, Concept, Branding, and Visual Language.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-07:** Moving the scroller backward from later content returned to earlier authored scenes rather than breaking the layout. The page’s custom Lenis movement is therefore directionally coherent in the inspected desktop run.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-08:** `/works/` is a three-column dark carousel. The center card shows the active work, adjacent cards show previous/next work, a `1/13` counter is visible, and the page shows `PLAYING` or `PAUSED` media state. Clicking the next area moved the active card from Arizona Diamondbacks to Home in a Hot Pot, changed the counter to `2/13`, and paused the selected media state.

### 5.3 Case-study pages

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-09:** `/arizona-diamondbacks/` opens with a large `ARIZONA D-BACKS` title, tag pills for `FORM STUDY`, `SPORTS MOTION`, `STADIUM GRAPHICS`, and `MARKETING`, role pills for Design and Motion, and a large stadium-graphic image. Its body documents overview/challenge/reflection/asset-note structure, an eight-item motion section, a twelve-item process section, credits, and next-project navigation.

**DOCUMENTED — JAS-10:** The Arizona case page describes stadium entertainment, themed nights, Opening Day, marketing, Chase Field, and motion assets that must work across stadium screens and social stories.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-11:** `/home-in-a-hot-pot/` opens with a large title and an overhead illustrated food/table composition. The metadata identifies form study/thesis, Concept, Design, and Motion; the content exposes a five-item design section, a fifteen-item process section, tools, credits, and a next-project link.

**DOCUMENTED — JAS-12:** The Hot Pot case describes family dynamics through food, mixed media, handmade textures, and the deliberate choice to preserve imperfection. It also documents awards and production tools including After Effects, Illustrator, Photoshop, Dragonframe, and Cinema 4D.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-13:** `/pre-college-2025/` opens with `PRECOLLEGE ’25`, Branding and Visual Language tags, and a large collage-like frame with paper, grid, and hand-made textures. Its structure includes a four-item motion section, a five-item process section, award copy, and credits.

**DOCUMENTED — JAS-14:** The PreCollege case describes a mixed-media campaign for high-school creatives and the need to make many makers and mediums feel like one world while retaining individual voices.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-15:** `/pinc/` opens with the centered `PINC` title, Branding and Design/Motion metadata, and a dark presentation frame featuring the Ringling College of Art + Design mark. Its content exposes a five-item motion section, a nine-item process section, credits, and next-project navigation.

**DOCUMENTED — JAS-16:** The PINC case describes an identity for a tenth anniversary, with grain, light, gradients, expanded colors, and simplicity used to add depth without clutter.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-17:** `/about/` opens with `ABOUT JASMINE` over an olive-green field and a portrait, then documents Jakarta/US background, motion/brand/graphic work, storytelling and tactile details, food/film interests, contact, skills, awards, and footer links.

### 5.4 Technical and critical review

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED — JAS-18:** The site uses WordPress/LiteSpeed asset paths, a theme bundle, jQuery, Cloudflare Insights, and Google Manrope font styles. The home and works surfaces use muted looping videos and WordPress-hosted WebP media. On the settled home page, the five main videos reported ready state 4; on the Works page, offscreen lazy video nodes reported ready state 0 while visible/active videos reported ready state 4.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — JAS-19:** The offscreen ready-state-0 videos were not treated as broken because they were lazy media outside the active visual region and other visible media had loaded. This is consistent with the slow connection and the site’s loading strategy.

**INFERRED — JAS-20:** Jasmine is the strongest example of typography functioning as interface, navigation, brand, and editorial pacing at once. The case pages add credibility because each project exposes process, tools, role, and reflection rather than only showing finished images.

**INFERRED — JAS-21:** The main risk is that the blank/preloader and custom-scroller architecture asks the visitor to tolerate a lot before conventional navigation becomes available. The finished experience is distinctive, but mobile, reduced motion, keyboard focus, and touch-drag behavior are **NOT VERIFIED**.

## 6. Osmo

Site: [www.osmo.supply](https://www.osmo.supply/)

### 6.1 Cold load and home architecture

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OSM-01:** The first capture at roughly 120 ms was blank white. At about 1.8 s, the header and hero appeared. After another extended wait, the hero stabilized into a light background with a black fixed nav, green marquee strip, huge `Dev Toolkit * Built to Flex` typography, and a radial carousel of tilted resource cards.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OSM-02:** The radial cards visibly rotate/advance over time. Cards include `Locomotive Smooth Scroll Setup`, `Logo Wall Cycle`, `Falling 2D Objects (MatterJS)`, `3D Image Carousel`, `Momentum Based Hover (Inertia)`, and others. The center copy describes Webflow and HTML resources, icons, easings, and a page-transition course.

**DOCUMENTED — OSM-03:** The page describes Osmo as an ever-growing platform with Webflow and HTML resources and exposes Products, Explore, Showcase, Collection 205, Pricing, a Vault, a page-transition course, buttons, icons, easings, community, testimonials, and membership plans.

**TECHNICALLY VERIFIED — OSM-04:** The live page loads Webflow-hosted assets, Inter font CSS, GSAP 3.15, Draggable, InertiaPlugin, ScrollTrigger, SplitText, CustomEase, Observer, Lenis 1.3.23, HLS.js, Barba core/prefetch, jQuery, and the Osmo site bundle. Resource images loaded from an Osmo Bunny CDN path; multiple muted looping resource videos reported ready state 4.

### 6.2 Interaction families

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OSM-05:** The fixed Menu opens into a full-width black overlay with product links, exploration links, social icons, a Page Transition Course card, and a close control. The overlay is a designed navigation surface, not a simple dropdown.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OSM-06:** Clicking `Play` in the `Osmo in use` reel opened a full-screen dark media viewer. After the additional wait, an HLS video was playing at ready state 4 with a visible carousel-like scene of character portraits, a percentage indicator, and a `Close` control. Closing returned to the page.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OSM-07:** The membership section contains Quarterly/Annually controls. Selecting Annually visibly changed the cards to `€20 EUR` Solo and `€16 EUR` Team with annual billing language and a `Save 20%` annotation. No purchase action was taken.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OSM-08:** `/showcase` required an extended settle wait after its DOM was complete. It then showed the large `* Showcase` title, a featured Minal Studio entry, creator attribution, resource count, Visit site action, and a long gallery of community work with names and resource counts.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OSM-09:** `/showcase/minal-studio` required the same patient settling approach. It then showed a dark project page with `Minal Studio`, date, creator links, a large animated project viewer, `Pause`/`Play`, time display, Visit site, Full Showcase, and `Resources used: 5`. The resource list documented page transitions, Bunny HLS lightbox, media setup, cursor marquee, and marquee scroll direction.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OSM-10:** `/product/vault` settled into a dark product page with `The Vault`, an intro viewer, a 205-resource claim, four value propositions, latest-added resources, feature breakdown, search/save/trending/AI/Figma/video capabilities, Webflow/HTML access, demo vault, and membership calls to action.

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OSM-11:** A resource route for `Section Anchor Dock` redirected to `/preview?resource=section-anchor-dock`. After settling, it showed a `Locked` badge, the resource title, category Navigation, a Preview video with MP4 control, and Login/Join Osmo actions. The preview video itself loaded and played; the code/resource access remained gated.

**INFERRED — OSM-12:** Osmo productizes interaction research well: every effect is named, previewed, categorized, counted, and placed inside a larger membership architecture. It teaches the visitor how to think about effects as reusable modules.

### 6.3 Technical and critical review

**DIRECTLY OBSERVED — OSM-13:** Several internal pages displayed blank or low-opacity transitional frames for many seconds after navigation. Additional waits produced the intended content. The run did not observe a persistent error after the slow-load procedure.

**INFERRED — OSM-14:** Osmo has the most sophisticated interaction inventory and the clearest product funnel, but it also carries the highest transition/media weight. Its strongest design decision is to expose capability through working previews instead of describing effects abstractly.

**INFERRED — OSM-15:** The main risks are performance and cognitive density: a moving radial hero, fixed navigation, video previews, multiple modals, HLS, long pages, and membership switches can compete for attention. The site should preserve the demonstrations while offering robust reduced-motion and low-bandwidth fallbacks; those fallbacks are **NOT VERIFIED**.

## 7. Cross-site comparison

| Dimension | Outfit | Inversa | Jasmine Gunarto | Osmo |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Loader | Black `000`, branded reveal, cached variant | Dark grid with phase/frequency language | Blank/dark preloader before typographic reveal | Blank frame before hero and long internal transitions |
| Dominant surface | Cream/red commerce | Dark/lime ecological operations | Pale gray/black/editorial color chapters | Light/dark productized toolkit |
| Navigation | Fixed shop/bag plus custom cursor | Fixed logo/menu and custom scroll | Fixed metadata/nav over internal Lenis scroller | Fixed nav, full overlay menu, green marquee |
| Motion model | Hover reveal, route transitions, product/cart state | Pinned state machine and reversible scroll | Scene-based custom scroll and video carousel | GSAP/Lenis/Barba-driven demos, video, modals, radial marquee |
| Proof mechanism | Products, variants, cart | Field states, case outcomes, ROI | Process, tools, roles, credits | Working previews, resource counts, creator/showcase proof |
| Conversion | Add to Bag → Bag → Checkout | Explore Origin → Request Demo/contact | Let’s Talk/contact | Join/Login → membership; locked resource gating |
| Main strength | Brand consistency | Service legibility | Typographic authorship | Reusable interaction system |
| Main risk | Blocking loader / price ambiguity | Custom-scroll fragility | Heavy custom-scroller tolerance | Media/transition weight |

**INFERRED:** The sites sit on a useful spectrum: Outfit makes a brand feel like a product; Inversa makes a service feel like a system; Jasmine makes a portfolio feel like a publication; Osmo makes interaction craft feel like infrastructure.

## Designer-brain reconstruction

**INFERRED — Outfit:** The designer appears to have started with a retail identity system, then made every surface obey it: the loader is a black stage, the wordmark is the arrival object, the product grid is an editorial catalog, hover is a branded cursor sentence, and the bag is a continuation of the same poster-like language. The goal is not “add ecommerce to a studio site”; it is “make commerce feel like an Outfit artifact.”

**INFERRED — Inversa:** The designer appears to have begun with an invisible operational workflow and asked which states a visitor must understand to trust it. The result is a sequence of field scenes, data cards, maps, and outcomes. The dark/lime visual language supports the operational premise, while the case study supplies real-world consequence. The site is designed as a guided systems explanation, not a feature list.

**INFERRED — Jasmine Gunarto:** The designer appears to have treated the portfolio as a publication with a typographic masthead, utility metadata, chapter transitions, selected work, process notes, credits, and a recurring closing invitation. Media is framed as editorial evidence; the large type gives the work a recognizable voice before a project is opened. Each case page is built to answer how, why, and with whom—not only what it looks like.

**INFERRED — Osmo:** The designer appears to have started from a library of real interaction experiments and built the product architecture around showing them working. The homepage is a capability sampler, the menu is a catalog, Showcase is social proof, Vault is the product narrative, resource previews are the unit of value, and membership is the access model. The interaction is the sales argument.

## 8. Best-in-class findings

**INFERRED — strongest loader:** Outfit. Its cold loader is the most explicitly branded and the most valuable object to study. The black `000` creates anticipation, then the page turns the wordmark into the arrival event.

**INFERRED — strongest service narrative:** Inversa. It names operational states, visualizes tools, supplies outcome evidence, and allows the visitor to understand a non-obvious business without starting from a dashboard diagram.

**INFERRED — strongest portfolio case-study architecture:** Jasmine. The project pages consistently expose role, medium, tools, overview, challenge, reflection, process, credits, and next-project movement.

**INFERRED — strongest interaction-product architecture:** Osmo. It turns demonstrations into named resources, categories, previews, counts, locked states, showcase proof, and a membership funnel.

## 9. Cross-pollination opportunities

| Borrow the principle from | Apply to | Use | Guardrail |
|---|---|---|---|
| Outfit | Any brand launch | A short branded arrival state that makes the identity appear through motion | Provide a skip/reduced-motion path; do not copy `000`, wordmark treatment, or image choreography |
| Outfit | Dubai Booking Marketplace | Product-card hover, clear option state, reversible basket interaction | Keep booking decisions transparent; never hide price or availability behind spectacle |
| Inversa | Aegis Senital | Named operational states, status cards, evidence counters, reversible progression | Use real system states; do not imply AI certainty or safety guarantees without validated data |
| Inversa | KT Courier | Detect → assign → in transit → delivered sequence | Separate live status from marketing illustration |
| Jasmine | Valtum Studio | Project-led home, oversized type, role/tool/process metadata | Use original editorial voice and content hierarchy |
| Jasmine | Personality Assessor | A paced question-to-insight narrative with chapters and meaningful transitions | Keep questions and results accessible without forcing a long animation wait |
| Osmo | Valtum Studio | A reusable motion language with named interaction primitives | Establish a unique visual grammar rather than an Osmo-like component gallery |
| Osmo | Math Intellect | Explain advanced concepts through small working visual modules | Preserve mathematical accuracy; preview behavior must not replace explanation |

## 10. Target relevance matrix

Ratings are **INFERRED** from the observed patterns, not claims about existing target implementations.

| Target | Highest-relevance site | Transferable design move | Specific use |
|---|---|---|---|
| Valtum Studio | Jasmine + Outfit | Editorial case-study rhythm plus a memorable brand arrival | Use a typographic hero, selected work chapters, process metadata, and one short branded loader |
| KT Courier | Inversa + Osmo | Operational state machine plus modular interaction feedback | Show quote/dispatch, pickup, transit, exception, and delivery as visible states; use small reusable status interactions |
| Personality Assessor | Jasmine + Inversa | Narrative chapters plus evidence-backed result states | Turn assessment progress into a calm sequence; separate answer collection, scoring, confidence, and explanation |
| Math Intellect | Osmo + Inversa | Demonstration modules plus structured proof | Use interactive examples, step states, data visualizations, and clear math notation instead of decorative motion alone |
| Aegis Senital | Inversa | Dark operational interface with evidence and action states | Use detect/verify/escalate/resolve status grammar and auditable event history; avoid unverified certainty claims |
| Dubai Booking Marketplace | Outfit + Inversa | Commerce funnel plus operational confidence | Use rich destination/product cards, transparent options and basket, then availability/confirmation states with visible reversibility |

## 11. Transferable principles

1. **Make waiting part of the identity only when the wait has a legible payoff.** Outfit and Osmo show why the first frame needs a designed state; both also show why slow connections make fallback behavior important.
2. **Use a small number of named states to explain a large system.** Inversa’s Body Cams, Predictive Heatmaps, and Field App states are easier to remember than a wall of features.
3. **Let typography do structural work.** Jasmine uses display type for identity, hierarchy, chaptering, and navigation, not just decoration.
4. **Treat hover as a sentence.** Outfit’s circular `VIEW MORE` cursor tells the visitor what the card means and what will happen next.
5. **Make evidence part of the case study.** Jasmine exposes tools, roles, process, credits, and reflection; Inversa exposes outcomes and partner context.
6. **Keep conversion inside the same visual language.** Outfit’s bag is still Outfit; Osmo’s membership cards still use the site’s modular demonstration language.
7. **Design reverse motion, not only forward motion.** Inversa and Jasmine both make backward movement meaningful in the inspected desktop run.
8. **Separate asset arrival from runtime failure.** A slow or lazy image/video node is not enough to call a page broken; wait until loading stops progressing and retry before diagnosing.
9. **Name interaction primitives if they are meant to be reused.** Osmo’s categories and resource titles make techniques findable.
10. **Use reduction as a pressure valve.** Every heavy custom-scroller or video-first experience needs a readable static path, reduced-motion behavior, and a low-bandwidth route; these were **NOT VERIFIED** on the inspected sites.

## 12. Anti-copy analysis and boundaries

The appropriate transfer is behavioral and strategic, not visual duplication.

Do not copy Outfit’s `000` loader, `OUTFIT` wordmark proportions, red/cream palette, circular `VIEW MORE` cursor, exact product grid, product names, or bag language.

Do not copy Inversa’s `LOADING… / PHASE / FREQ` loader vocabulary, lime/black field-state palette, contour-map treatment, worker line art, model-card visuals, or operational copy.

Do not copy Jasmine’s condensed display type treatment, `JASMINE GUNARTO` wordmark composition, fixed metadata placement, project names, collage frames, or exact case-study sequence.

Do not copy Osmo’s black rounded nav, green marquee, purple asterisk, radial card carousel, resource titles, locked-badge treatment, or membership card composition.

**INFERRED:** What can be transferred safely is the underlying design logic: authored arrival, named states, typographic hierarchy, reversible interactions, process transparency, modular previews, clear conversion, and disciplined evidence.

## 13. Open questions and not-verified items

- **NOT VERIFIED:** Mobile, tablet, touch, and actual responsive breakpoint behavior for all four sites.
- **NOT VERIFIED:** Keyboard focus order, screen-reader announcements, and reduced-motion behavior for the custom loaders, cursors, scrollers, carousels, and modals.
- **NOT VERIFIED:** Exact network waterfall, cache headers, compression, Core Web Vitals, and measured time-to-interactive.
- **NOT VERIFIED:** Exact framework internals for Inversa beyond observed Nuxt-style asset paths and bundles.
- **NOT VERIFIED:** Full visual traversal of every intermediate Origin mission scene on Inversa.
- **NOT VERIFIED:** The reason for Outfit’s observed `$36.50` product-page versus `$34` bag display.
- **NOT VERIFIED:** Authenticated/member-only Osmo Vault content, login, checkout, downloads, and code-copy behavior.
- **NOT VERIFIED:** Osmo’s Quarterly pricing reversal after the annual state; the annual selection was directly observed, while the reverse control did not produce a confirmed state change in the slow browser run.
- **NOT VERIFIED:** Whether all lazy offscreen videos on Jasmine eventually load under a continued scroll-through; visible active videos were ready and playing/available.
- **NOT VERIFIED:** Whether all external video destinations, social links, and resume/download links are reachable beyond their visible hrefs.

## 15. Final assessment

**INFERRED:** All four sites were materially usable after the requested slow-load patience and retry discipline. The most important finding is that their visual sophistication is not one technique. Outfit’s loader, Inversa’s state choreography, Jasmine’s typographic editorial system, and Osmo’s resource/product architecture each solve a different communication problem. For new work, borrow the communication logic, preserve the evidence boundary, and design the slow, reversed, internal, and low-bandwidth states as deliberately as the first screenshot.

# SOURCE REPORT — Final Pre-Coding Deep Research — Core Ecosystem Pass

**Original local source:** `deep-research-report.md`

# Final Pre-Coding Deep Research for the Valtum Ecosystem

## Research verdict and evidence boundary

This first final research pass changes the job from **“collect excellent references”** to **“build a design decision system for six different products.”** The reference library is now sufficiently broad: the previous live-browser investigation established four distinct forms of authorship—Outfit as brand-to-commerce continuity, Inversa as operational storytelling, Jasmine Gunarto as typographic/editorial portfolio design, and Osmo as a reusable interaction system. That run also established an important technical constraint: sophisticated experiences must remain intelligible on slow connections, and mobile, touch, keyboard, reduced-motion, and measured performance could not be verified there. fileciteturn0file0

The current live reference landscape reinforces those lessons rather than overturning them. Glyphic still organizes a difficult biotechnology proposition around a highly legible progression—sample preparation, molecular expansion, and amino-acid sequencing—while allowing its scientific subject matter to become the graphic protagonist. citeturn13view0 Inversa still exposes named operational states such as Body Cams, Predictive Heatmaps, and Field App rather than explaining the whole product through generic feature cards. citeturn13view2 Jasmine continues to make typography, project artifacts, and editorial sequencing the portfolio's identity, while Osmo continues to present individual motion mechanics as named, reusable resources rather than random “effects.” citeturn13view3turn13view4

The research therefore supports one foundational decision:

> **Valtum Studio, KT Courier, Dubai Booking Marketplace, Math Intellect, Personality Assessor, and Aegis Senital should share a quality standard and an interaction philosophy, but they should not share one visual template.**

A single recognizable “Valtum website style” repeated six times would be a regression. The common DNA should instead be structural: integrated headers, strong composition, semantically meaningful imagery, excellent typography, deliberate transitions, reversible interaction, real evidence, mobile-first adaptation, performance discipline, and a refusal to use spectacle where the content does not justify it. That conclusion follows directly from the fact that the strongest references solve radically different communication problems with different visual systems. fileciteturn0file0

I would now divide our readiness into three layers:

| Layer | Status after this research | Meaning |
|---|---:|---|
| General creative-web intelligence | **~98%** | We have enough reference knowledge; further random award-site research has sharply diminishing value. |
| Six-site experience architecture | **~96%** | We can now assign a distinct hero, media grammar, motion grammar, scrolling model, and UX character to every site. |
| Exact Codex implementation specification | **~88–90%** | The remaining gap is primarily the actual Valtum/project asset inventory, verified product capabilities, final copy, brand assets, font licensing, and the final second-pass challenge/validation. |

The approximately ten-percent implementation gap is important. I can now say **what kind of image belongs in a location, how it should behave, how it should crop, and why**; I cannot responsibly invent the exact filename or claim a feature exists until the actual project assets and product truth are audited. That distinction is exactly what should remain for the second final research.

## Shared design language across all six websites

### The header is part of the opening composition

Your header rule is correct, and I would make it an ecosystem-wide hard rule.

At the initial viewport, there is no conceptual object called “the navbar” sitting above another conceptual object called “the hero.” There is **one opening scene**, and navigation is one layer within that scene.

The Glyphic screenshot is a good expression of the principle: navigation, page background, enormous display typography, and the scientific protagonist occupy one continuous field. Glyphic's current site also structures the science itself as the subject rather than adding a decorative visual language on top of it. citeturn13view0

Therefore, across all six sites:

**Do not** begin with a detached white pill, glass capsule, rounded floating rectangle, or dark bar that visually cuts the header away from the hero.

The initial header should use the hero's own background/world. Logo and navigation should be positioned from the same grid used to position the hero typography and media. A separator line is permissible only where it belongs to the composition—not because “navbars usually have borders.”

The header may subsequently change when the opening scene is left. For example, a light photographic hero can begin with white navigation and change to dark text when the next white chapter enters. Aegis can move from an integrated command-scene header into a compact persistent utility header. The important point is that **the transformation is caused by the page state**, rather than the header existing as an unrelated component.

On mobile, the same rule survives. A menu trigger may become compact, but it still occupies the opening scene rather than being placed inside a generic floating capsule.

### Typography must create geometry

The strongest conclusion from Glyphic, Jasmine, and our earlier references is that large typography is useful only when it does structural work. Jasmine's observed desktop experience used large display typography for identity, hierarchy, pacing, chapter transitions, and project framing simultaneously. fileciteturn0file0

For Valtum we should think of typography in three roles:

**Interface type** tells the user what can be done.

**Editorial type** controls pacing and emphasis.

**Spatial type** determines where media can enter, overlap, disappear behind, or cut through the composition.

That third category is especially important.

A title does not automatically need to remain inside a clean text box. In an appropriate hero, an object can occlude part of a title; an image can begin underneath one word and end over another; one line of text can sit in the foreground while the media moves behind it. The effect is valuable when depth reinforces hierarchy. It should not become a site-wide trick.

Variable typography gives us considerable implementation flexibility because a single variable family can expose continuous weight and width/stretch ranges, rather than requiring a separate font file for every static weight. We should prefer semantic font properties such as `font-weight` and `font-stretch` when a variable family exposes those axes, reserving lower-level variation controls for axes not otherwise available. citeturn6search14turn6search3

The final font families should **not** be selected from aesthetics alone in this research. Licensing and the existing six brand identities need to be checked in the second pass. But the typography characters are now clear:

| Site | Required typographic character |
|---|---|
| **Valtum Studio** | Authored editorial grotesk/display relationship; confident, wide enough for very large spatial composition; not “startup SaaS.” |
| **KT Courier** | Highly legible industrial/humanist sans with excellent numerals and operational labels; condensed display face is optional for route/status moments. |
| **Dubai Booking** | Contemporary editorial sans with some hospitality warmth, but explicitly not cliché luxury serif + gold. Excellent numerals for price/date/time are mandatory. |
| **Math Intellect** | Highly legible mathematical/UI sans with a separate expressive display voice; math notation must remain visually authoritative. |
| **Personality Assessor** | Human, calm, highly readable sans; less mechanical than Aegis and less promotional than Valtum. |
| **Aegis Senital** | Compact, precise interface sans with excellent numerical/data legibility and a controlled display weight for major operational claims. |

### “Premium” is not a palette

The research gives us enough evidence to reject a common mistake: premium design is not created by making everything black, adding blurred darkness around photographs, or using luxury gradients.

Outfit's brand is cream/red; Inversa uses ecological field imagery and operational interfaces; Jasmine moves through editorial neutral and project-specific colors; Glyphic is predominantly light and lets molecular imagery provide visual character. Their sophistication comes from **consistency, hierarchy, media quality, transitions, composition, and content relevance**, not a universal “premium” palette. fileciteturn0file0 citeturn13view0

Our existing bans therefore remain correct:

**No gradients as a design crutch.**

**No beige/cream “design studio template” identity for Valtum.**

**No generic purple/blue AI palette just because software uses artificial intelligence.**

**No heavy darkness purely to make images feel cinematic.**

**No decorative eyebrow text above every heading.**

Instead, each product needs a dominant visual field derived from what it actually does. Aegis may legitimately have dark operational chapters because video/security monitoring benefits from controlled luminance and contrast. Dubai may legitimately have a photographic, high-luminance hero because place and experience are what the visitor is buying. Math Intellect can be bright and almost diagrammatic because clarity is part of the product promise.

### There should be one motion grammar, not hundreds of effects

Osmo is useful here precisely because it names patterns—momentum hover, image carousel, smooth-scroll setup, directional hover, spatial card slider—making interaction behavior understandable as reusable primitives. citeturn13view4 The live-inspection report reached the same conclusion: Osmo's most transferable lesson is productizing interaction research into named modules. fileciteturn0file0

Our eventual motion library should therefore be small enough to describe.

For example:

**Reveal** — media or text enters when its information becomes relevant.

**Carry** — an element survives from one state into the next.

**Exchange** — one medium becomes another while preserving semantic identity.

**Pin** — the world stays stable while one variable changes.

**Rail** — the user moves through a spatial sequence.

**Inspect** — hover/press reveals a second level of evidence.

**Resolve** — a complex sequence settles into a simple final state.

**Route** — opening a detail page preserves the visual identity of the object selected.

That is the kind of system Codex can implement consistently.

## Heroes, imagery, galleries, and image-scroller architecture

### The hero question is “what is the protagonist?”

The hero should not begin from a layout recipe. It should begin with:

> **What should the visitor remember after the first ten seconds?**

That eliminates most generic “left text/right image” decisions automatically.

There are six very different answers in our ecosystem.

| Site | Hero protagonist | Strongest hero architecture |
|---|---|---|
| **Valtum Studio** | The work and Valtum's act of transforming ideas into functioning products | Oversized spatial typography interlocked with selected product media |
| **KT Courier** | A delivery moving through an operational system | Live route/state composition with real courier/product evidence |
| **Dubai Booking** | The experience/place the person can actually book | Full-viewport location/experience photography with booking intent integrated into the scene |
| **Math Intellect** | A mathematical concept becoming understandable | Interactive mathematical object/diagram as hero, not stock photography |
| **Personality Assessor** | A multidimensional person becoming legible without being reduced to a label | Living spectrum/result visualization with calm human copy |
| **Aegis Senital** | An incident becoming understood and actionable | Controlled situational scene combining camera evidence, event, map/location, and response state |

That means your instinct toward a **full-background-image hero** is particularly strong for Dubai Booking, potentially useful for selected Valtum case-study openings, and possibly useful for KT if we possess genuinely strong operational photography. It is much weaker for Math Intellect and Personality Assessor, because those products have better native protagonists than a photograph.

### Image selection must be semantic, not “cinematic”

This is one of the clearest results of the research.

Booking.com's current Dubai attraction pages lead with photographs of the **actual experience**—Burj Khalifa views, visitors at the attraction, Dubai Fountain, Aquaventure activity—not generic pictures of supercars, dark hotel lobbies, or mysterious skyline shadows. The listings then immediately expose things visitors need to decide: reviews, cancellation terms, duration, ticket details, and the actual experience description. citeturn19search6turn19search10turn19search19

Likewise, Inversa uses aerial ecology, field specialists, a hotspot map, body-camera context, mobile UI, and field evidence because those are the objects that explain the service. fileciteturn0file0 Glyphic uses its molecular/protein subject matter and process explanation because that is what needs to become understandable. citeturn13view0 Jasmine uses the actual artifacts of the work—stadium graphics, mixed-media food imagery, branding materials, motion pieces—rather than replacing projects with generic art-direction photography. fileciteturn0file0

Therefore the image rule for all six sites is:

> **Every major image must perform at least one of four jobs: explain, prove, locate, or create emotional proximity to the real subject.**

A beautiful image that performs none of those jobs is secondary decoration.

The media inventory we ultimately need should therefore include, where available:

| Product | Priority media |
|---|---|
| **Valtum** | Real product screens; strong crops of interface details; project prototypes; contextual screenshots; project-specific brand/art assets; selective process artifacts |
| **KT Courier** | Actual driver application; dispatch map; parcel/status UI; proof-of-delivery UI; driver/vehicle/warehouse photography if authentic |
| **Dubai Booking** | Real destinations and experiences; people participating in them; location views; operator media; map context; actual listing/booking UI |
| **Math Intellect** | Interactive graphs, diagrams, equations, geometry, learning UI, progression states; limited human photography only when it adds educational context |
| **Personality Assessor** | Assessment UI, result visualizations, trait relationships, comparisons, report material; subtle people photography only where the human story benefits |
| **Aegis** | Actual camera imagery, event timeline, detection overlays, facility context, map, alert evidence, system status, audit history |

### Cropping must be art-directed

Responsive imagery is not simply “use `object-fit: cover` everywhere.”

The HTML `<picture>` mechanism exists specifically so different image sources can be selected for different display situations, and responsive-image guidance explicitly describes **art direction** as using a different crop for a narrow screen than the wide-screen version. `object-position` can then move the focal subject inside a fixed frame. citeturn16search5turn16search2

This matters dramatically for our full-screen heroes.

For example, if the Dubai hero uses an image containing a person on a desert experience and architecture in the distance:

**Desktop crop:** preserve the person at roughly the right-third intersection and leave negative space on the left for typography.

**Mobile crop:** do not merely crop the center of the desktop image. Use a separate portrait-oriented source that preserves the person and enough environmental context for the experience to remain understandable.

Codex should eventually receive a **focal-point specification per major image**, not only an image filename.

### Image scrollers need different jobs

I would not build one universal “Valtum carousel component.” We need four distinct gallery/scroller modes.

**Editorial project rail — Valtum Studio.**  
On large desktop, selected projects can become a horizontally progressing editorial field after the opening chapter. The viewport stays stable while large project media passes laterally, with project name, role, year, and short result attached to the active piece. It should feel like moving through work, not sliding cards.

**Operational state rail — KT Courier and Aegis.**  
A vertical scroll can drive a horizontal or fixed-stage sequence, but only where states form an actual process. Inversa's direct-live sequence is the important reference: Body Cams, Predictive Heatmaps, and Field App were named, visually distinct, and reversed coherently on backward scroll. fileciteturn0file0 For KT the states might be **Order → Assign → Pickup → In Transit → Delivered**, with an Exception branch where appropriate. For Aegis: **Detect → Verify → Contextualize → Escalate → Resolve**.

**Transactional media gallery — Dubai Booking.**  
This should prioritize control over spectacle. Desktop can use an editorial collage or primary image plus neighboring previews, but mobile should become a native swipeable/snap gallery. The product page must then immediately expose date/time, cancellation, duration, availability, price, location, restrictions, and booking action—information patterns already visible in current booking products. citeturn19search6turn19search20

**Demonstration sequence — Math Intellect.**  
Instead of a gallery of screenshots, the “images” can be live learning states: problem → manipulation → feedback → insight. Brilliant currently emphasizes interactive problem solving, visual canvases, custom feedback, progression, and guided lessons; Mathigon similarly centers manipulatives, exploration, problem-solving, patterns, and interactive representations. citeturn20search17turn20search19turn20search4

This is a major distinction:

> **The best image scroller for a product may not contain conventional images at all.**

## Motion, smooth scrolling, horizontal movement, mobile, and performance

### Smooth scrolling should be selective

Our references make a strong case for smooth scrolling as an art-direction tool, but also expose its failure modes. Inversa and Jasmine both relied on custom Lenis-controlled experiences in the live run, and both demanded patience under slow-loading conditions. fileciteturn0file0

The engineering conclusion is:

**Valtum marketing:** smooth scrolling justified.

**KT marketing:** light smoothing justified; core tracking/dispatch UI should remain native.

**Dubai discovery/editorial portions:** very light smoothing can be considered on desktop; search, booking, date selection, cart/checkout should feel native and immediate.

**Math lessons:** native scrolling and direct manipulation should dominate; no ornamental smoothing around interactive math.

**Personality assessment:** native scrolling/controlled step transitions; no heavy inertial browsing.

**Aegis marketing:** smoothing may be used around storytelling; actual command surfaces should prioritize immediate response.

GSAP's ScrollTrigger supports scrubbed animation, pinning, snapping, vertical or horizontal scrolling, forward/backward callbacks, velocity, responsive setups, and resize recalculation, which makes it appropriate for the few sequences where scroll position genuinely represents narrative progress. Its own documentation also notes that pinning can flash when browsers repaint quickly and warns against animating the pinned element itself because doing so disrupts measurements. citeturn17search0

That reinforces a principle we learned visually:

> **Pin the stage. Animate what happens inside the stage.**

Do not pin because it looks “premium.”

### Horizontal scrolling needs a semantic reason

There are three good reasons for a horizontal sequence:

**Time or progression** — a process moves from state A to B to C.

**Collection** — works or experiences form a lateral gallery.

**Spatial relationship** — the user is traversing an environment or system.

There is no good reason called “Awwwards sites have horizontal sections.”

On desktop, GSAP ScrollTrigger can translate an internal track while vertical wheel input advances progress. It can also respond to backward direction, velocity, and responsive conditions. citeturn17search0

On mobile, the default should be different. WCAG's reflow requirement says vertical content should remain usable at the equivalent of 320 CSS pixels without two-dimensional scrolling, with exceptions where a two-dimensional layout is necessary for meaning. citeturn15search7 W3C's mobile guidance gives the same 320 CSS-pixel reflow criterion and explicitly identifies maps, diagrams, data tables, and similar interfaces as cases where two-dimensional presentation can legitimately be necessary. citeturn15search3

So mobile conversions should generally be:

**Valtum desktop horizontal work rail → mobile vertical editorial project sequence or user-controlled snap rail.**

**KT desktop operational lateral timeline → mobile stacked status chapters or one-card-per-swipe state track.**

**Dubai desktop gallery → native horizontal finger swipe.**

**Aegis desktop system state sequence → stacked incident states, preserving map/video only where necessary.**

CSS scroll snapping can provide controlled native horizontal movement without hijacking the entire page, while GSAP itself explicitly notes that ScrollTrigger does not require scroll-jacking and can coexist with native scroll technologies. citeturn17search0

### Motion must reverse

This should be a hard acceptance criterion.

Inversa's live inspection demonstrated a coherent backward transition from Predictive Heatmaps back into Body Cams. Jasmine similarly returned through earlier authored states rather than breaking the composition. fileciteturn0file0

Therefore a Codex implementation is incomplete if it only looks correct when the user scrolls slowly downward once.

Every complex state sequence must be tested for:

forward slow  
forward fast  
reverse slow  
reverse fast  
trackpad oscillation  
jumping using navigation  
viewport resizing mid-state  
returning with browser Back.

A 1.2-second decorative entrance tween that blindly restarts every time an observer triggers is **not** the desired model for stateful sections.

### Reduced motion is an alternate composition, not “animations: none”

`prefers-reduced-motion` is widely supported and communicates that the user wants non-essential movement minimized; MDN specifically notes that large scaling and panning motions can trigger vestibular discomfort. citeturn15search0

Our reduced-motion strategy should therefore preserve meaning while removing spatial travel.

Example:

Full-motion Aegis:
camera tile expands → map shifts → evidence attaches → response state enters.

Reduced-motion Aegis:
camera/evidence/map remain in a stable grid → active state changes using brief opacity and border/semantic-color transitions.

Full-motion Valtum:
project media carries from rail into project page.

Reduced-motion Valtum:
the new page appears with the same image already in the corresponding final position, using a short crossfade rather than spatial flight.

That is a much better interpretation of accessibility than deleting every transition indiscriminately.

### Slow internet must be designed, not tolerated accidentally

The live inspection made this issue unusually concrete. Outfit's initial assets, Inversa's custom-scroll chapters, Jasmine's lazy videos, and several Osmo internal routes required material settling time; the report correctly distinguished slow progress from actual failure. fileciteturn0file0

The production solution, however, is not “make users wait longer.”

Critical hero imagery must be discoverable early and must not be lazily loaded. Current web.dev guidance explicitly recommends eager-loading first-viewport images, especially likely LCP candidates, and restricting lazy loading to content outside the initial viewport. citeturn16search1turn16search3 Responsive `srcset`/`sizes` should prevent a small device from downloading an unnecessarily enormous desktop asset, while responsive preload can help when a critical hero image cannot be discovered early in initial markup. citeturn16search0

This means our eventual loader doctrine should be:

**Never block merely because a loader animation exists.**

**Never impose an artificial three-second minimum.**

**If the opening scene is ready, release it.**

**If essential media is genuinely loading, show a branded but semantically honest state.**

**Do not fake numerical loading percentages unless they actually track a known asset process.**

That is the principle we should take from Outfit—not its literal `000` vocabulary. Outfit's live run showed that the brand transition, wordmark, product system, hover behavior, and bag all spoke the same visual language; that continuity is the transferable part. fileciteturn0file0

For implementation acceptance, I would use an internal performance target of approximately **LCP ≤ 2.5 seconds, INP ≤ 200 ms, CLS ≤ 0.1 at the 75th percentile**, and treat missed budgets on major pages as defects requiring review rather than inevitable consequences of creative design. Current web guidance identifies 200 ms or lower as the “good” INP threshold and 0.1 or lower as the “good” CLS threshold; web performance guidance also uses 2.5 seconds as the passing/good LCP boundary. citeturn18search0turn18search5turn18search1

Creative frontend work does not receive an exemption from responsiveness.

## Site-specific design direction

**Valtum Studio — authored portfolio, not agency template.**  
The strongest direction is a **100svh opening composition with the header integrated directly into the hero**, a large Valtum typographic field, and one or more real project artifacts crossing that typography. This is where the Glyphic depth model is most useful: headline and media should occupy the same spatial plane system instead of creating “text area” and “image area.” citeturn13view0 The hero should not show a random laptop mockup, futuristic abstract blob, generic office, or AI-generated cinematic scene. It should introduce the thing Valtum actually sells: the ability to transform complex product requirements into authored functioning software.

A research-stage hero copy direction I would currently put at the top of the shortlist is:

**“Digital products, designed to work in the real world.”**

The exact sentence is not yet locked because it must survive the final audit of Valtum's positioning, but its content direction is correct: concrete output rather than “we craft extraordinary digital experiences.”

Below the opening scene, Valtum should move into **selected work as chapters**, not six equal project cards. A large desktop work rail can give each product enough room to establish its own visual world. The transition into an individual case study should preserve the selected media wherever technically reasonable; GSAP Flip is built specifically around capturing before/after layout states and can support shared-element movement between visual structures. citeturn14search0 Native View Transitions can increasingly supplement this as progressive enhancement, but they should not be the sole architecture because cross-document behavior and support details still vary. citeturn14search1turn14search5

Case studies should learn from Jasmine without copying Jasmine's layout: **problem → context → role → product/world → process → pivotal design decisions → real interface → implementation → result/evidence → next project.** Jasmine's observed project pages consistently surfaced role, medium, tools, process, reflection, credits, and next-project movement, making the work more credible than a gallery of final shots. fileciteturn0file0

**KT Courier — movement, accountability, and execution.**  
The hero should not be “courier man holding box + headline.” Current last-mile products expose the actual operational value through live driver visibility, timestamps, barcode scans, proof of delivery, route assignment, delay alerts, and customer status. Onfleet centers chain-of-custody evidence and live driver visibility; Bringg organizes its platform around shopping experience, planning/dispatch, driver workflow, and delivery experience; Route4Me describes last-mile execution as planning, optimizing, dispatching, and tracking routes. citeturn19search0turn19search5turn19search1

The KT hero should therefore show **a delivery becoming a controlled operation**. The visual field can contain a real map/route layer, one active courier or parcel state, and a physical contextual image if we have authentic photography. The interface should make one delivery feel alive.

A strong research-stage headline is:

**“Every delivery, accounted for.”**

The main storytelling chapter should then progress:

**Order received → Assigned → Pickup verified → In transit → Delivered**

and separately show what happens when the ideal path breaks:

**Delay / failed attempt / reassignment / exception → resolution.**

Proof-of-delivery must be visual evidence, not a decorative checkmark; current delivery platforms treat photos, barcode scans, signatures, timestamps, and verification as concrete evidence. citeturn19search14turn19search7

The image language should use real dispatch maps, driver UI, parcels, logistics environments, street/location context, and delivery evidence. A dramatic black warehouse image with one strip light is less valuable than a clean high-resolution image showing the actual operational circumstance.

**Dubai Booking Marketplace — place first, transaction immediately behind it.**  
This is the clearest candidate for your full-background-image hero idea.

Use **one genuinely excellent full-viewport Dubai experience image**, not a collage of clichés. Prefer an image where a visitor can imagine themselves performing the activity—desert, water, observation, dining, attraction, cultural experience—rather than simply another distant skyline. Current booking inventory succeeds because images correspond directly to the experience being sold, while key confidence signals such as reviews, duration, free cancellation, and ticket details are surfaced alongside them. citeturn19search6turn19search10turn19search22

The integrated header sits over the same photographic scene. Readability should come first from **image selection and art-directed crop**. Where additional separation is genuinely necessary, use a restrained uniform translucent overlay, not a black gradient creeping upward from the bottom.

The booking/discovery control should enter inside the lower part of the hero, but it should look like a purposeful product control—not a generic glassmorphism capsule. The first query should ideally express a human booking decision: **What / When / Guests**, with location already contextualized as Dubai if this marketplace is Dubai-specific.

A research-stage hero copy direction:

**“Book the Dubai you came to experience.”**

After the hero, the primary media system should move through curated experiences using strong destination photography. Product detail must then become unapologetically transactional: photo gallery, title, rating/reviews, cancellation, duration, date/time, participants, availability, inclusions, restrictions, location/map, operator details where relevant, price, and booking action. Booking.com's current attraction pages expose exactly this type of decision-critical information. citeturn19search6turn19search20

On mobile, the gallery should be native swipe/snap, the date/participant experience should be thumb-friendly, and the primary booking action should remain easy to recover without forcing the user back to the top.

**Math Intellect — make understanding itself visual.**  
Do not use an image-led hero unless the image is actually mathematical.

Brilliant's current positioning centers interactive problem solving, an interactive visual canvas, immediate/custom feedback, guided lessons, and progression one concept at a time. Mathigon describes its product as a mathematical playground built from interactive tools, manipulatives, problem-solving, patterns, and real applications. citeturn20search17turn20search19turn20search4

Therefore Math Intellect's hero protagonist should be **a mathematical idea changing under the user's action**.

For example, depending on the curriculum, a draggable point could change an equation and graph together; an algebraic expression could visually rebalance; a geometric construction could reveal an invariant. The important thing is that the visitor understands, within seconds:

> “This product lets me manipulate mathematics rather than merely read about it.”

A strong research-stage line is:

**“Understand the idea. Not just the answer.”**

The header should sit inside the same clean mathematical field. The typography can be large, but the interactive object must retain enough negative space that the hero doesn't turn into an advertising poster.

The experience should use small motion modules where motion explains a relationship: line rotates → angle changes → numerical value updates. Decorative parallax around an equation adds nothing.

On mobile, every manipulation needs a touch equivalent and equations cannot simply be scaled until unreadable. Difficult mathematical layouts are one of the legitimate cases where constrained two-dimensional interaction may be necessary for meaning under WCAG's reflow exception, but surrounding explanatory content should still reflow normally. citeturn15search7

**Personality Assessor — depth without determinism.**  
The hero should immediately distance the product from the shallow “you are one of sixteen boxes” aesthetic unless that is genuinely the assessment model used.

Current TraitLab result design is instructive because its Big Five results show dimensions, ranges, relative position, plain-language summaries, and explicit margin-of-error information rather than pretending every psychological result is a perfectly precise binary fact. TraitLab also explicitly states that there is no inherently “good” or “bad” set of Big Five scores. citeturn20search2 Its current product lets results become more detailed as more questions are answered and represents personality through multidimensional scores and explanations. citeturn20search5turn20search10

We must not assume Personality Assessor itself uses Big Five; that is a product fact to verify. But the UX principle transfers regardless:

> **Represent uncertainty, nuance, relationships, and interpretation honestly.**

A strong research-stage hero direction is:

**“See yourself as a spectrum, not a label.”**

The visual protagonist should be a calm multidimensional result system—lines, ranges, relationships, or a dynamic profile—not a glowing human silhouette, brain scan, or AI orb.

The assessment itself should favor one clear decision at a time, persistent but quiet progress, immediate selection feedback, autosave where appropriate, and a clear way to resume. Results should progress from overview → dimensions → interpretation → relationships/implications → methodology/confidence where the underlying product supports those claims.

Mobile is especially important: one question should never require a tiny card inside a tiny centered desktop-style frame. Let the content own the viewport.

**Aegis Senital — situational clarity, not cyberpunk.**  
This project has perhaps the greatest risk of falling into a generic visual cliché: black background, neon blue map, grids, random green detection boxes, monospace labels, and “AI-powered security” copy.

Current security platforms point to a more useful design truth. Verkada Command unifies cameras, access, alarms, environmental information, and associated context in one operational view; its mobile experience emphasizes live feeds, event alerts, device health, emergency actions, and related-camera context. citeturn21search2turn21search3 FlockOS similarly centers a shared situational map that brings video, license-plate recognition, sensors, dispatch/CAD data, and other live context together, while Flock FreeForm explicitly exposes permission controls, search history, audit logs, and alerts as part of responsible operations. citeturn21search0turn21search12

So the Aegis hero should not portray “security” abstractly. It should portray **one incident becoming understandable**.

A recommended opening composition:

full viewport operational field  
→ facility/location context occupying most of the background  
→ one meaningful camera/evidence frame  
→ one incident marker  
→ one concise status/evidence card  
→ integrated header inside the same world.

A strong research-stage headline:

**“See the signal. Verify the event. Act with context.”**

The narrative sequence should be:

**Detect → Verify → Contextualize → Escalate → Resolve → Audit**

provided those states accurately represent the actual Aegis product.

Darkness is allowed here when functional. What is not allowed is illegibility. Semantic status colors should have jobs: alert, warning, healthy, inactive, verified. They should not paint the whole UI for atmosphere.

Mobile should not attempt to recreate the entire security operations center at 390 pixels wide. It should answer the mobile operator's likely questions:

**What happened? Where? How certain/evidenced is it? What live context can I inspect? What action can I take? Has someone already responded?**

That prioritization is consistent with current security mobile products, which expose incident response, cameras, alerts, health/status, and emergency actions rather than shrinking an entire desktop command wall. citeturn21search1turn21search3

## Pre-Codex doctrine and what the final research must lock

The most important result of this research is that we can now stop saying things like:

> “Make it smooth.”

> “Add cinematic images.”

> “Use a premium hero.”

> “Create an interesting horizontal section.”

Those phrases should never reach Codex.

The implementation specification should eventually look like a production blueprint. For every major scene it will contain:

**Viewport and grid.**  
For example: `≥1440 px`, 12 columns, defined page inset, column gaps, `100svh` opening stage, maximum body measures, exact section-height logic.

**Header state.**  
Exact logo position, navigation labels, text color, active behavior, menu trigger, transition point, sticky behavior, and how contrast changes when the hero exits.

**Exact content.**  
No lorem ipsum and no “write something catchy.” Heading, paragraph, CTA, labels, metadata, tooltips, empty states, validation text, captions, and footer strings will be supplied.

**Asset identity.**  
An actual image/video/screenshot filename or CMS asset ID.

**Asset art direction.**  
Desktop source, mobile source, aspect ratio, focal point, `object-position`, mask/clip, foreground/background layer, responsive substitution, and alt text.

**Typography.**  
Family, licensed file/source, weight, stretch/width where applicable, size, line height, tracking, intended line breaks, maximum width, responsive formula, numeral treatment.

**Motion.**  
Trigger, start, end, scroll distance, persistence, transform values, easing, duration or scrub factor, layer ordering, reverse state, interruption behavior, and reduced-motion equivalent.

**Interaction.**  
Hover, keyboard focus, press, touch, drag, selected, disabled, loading, success, error, back-navigation, deep-link, and route-transition behavior.

**Performance.**
