Which hero asset is eager, what is preload-worthy, what is lazy, responsive source widths, fixed media dimensions/aspect ratio to prevent layout shifts, and what enhancement is removed when the device/network cannot justify it. Responsive-image guidance strongly supports serving device-appropriate image sources rather than forcing a small mobile viewport to download desktop-scale assets. citeturn16search0turn16search10

**Accessibility.**  
Reading order, focus order, touch target, contrast, reduced motion, alt text, form labels, status announcements, and behavior at 320 CSS-pixel reflow. WCAG 2.2 establishes the reflow requirement, while reduced-motion media preferences give us a direct platform mechanism for minimizing non-essential movement. citeturn15search7turn15search0

The implementation agent should not be allowed to quietly reinterpret those decisions. When a specified composition overflows, an asset is missing, a breakpoint reveals a contradiction, or an animation creates an accessibility/performance conflict, Codex should **surface the conflict** rather than inventing a new design.

The remaining second deep-research pass should therefore **not return to broad inspiration hunting**. The unanswered questions are much more concrete now:

| Remaining uncertainty | Why it must be resolved before final coding brief |
|---|---|
| Actual media inventory for all six projects | Determines exact hero assets, crops, case-study sequences, galleries, and whether certain concepts are feasible |
| Exact product functionality | Prevents us from visually or verbally claiming states/features that do not exist |
| Existing brand marks/colors/type licenses | Determines final palettes and exact font files rather than generic recommendations |
| Final Valtum positioning and service truth | Needed to lock the hero headline, service architecture, CTAs, and case-study narrative |
| Product-specific copy/data | Needed for actual dashboards, booking states, assessment results, delivery statuses, and Aegis evidence |
| Mobile prototype validation | Required to verify that the desktop concepts genuinely recompose rather than simply shrink |
| Performance prototype validation | Required to measure whether the chosen media/motion architecture actually meets our budgets |
| Accessibility interaction review | Required for keyboard, reduced-motion, focus, status announcements, drag alternatives, and touch behavior |

The central design doctrine is now sufficiently mature to proceed:

> **Valtum should feel authored. KT should feel operational. Dubai should feel experiential and trustworthy. Math Intellect should make understanding tangible. Personality Assessor should make complexity humane. Aegis should make uncertainty actionable.**

And underneath all six:

> **The header belongs to the hero. Typography participates in composition. Images represent the real subject. Motion explains change. Horizontal movement must have meaning. Mobile gets its own choreography. Performance is part of visual quality. The product—not the reference website—decides the design.**

---

# SOURCE REPORT — Final Pre-Coding Deep Research — Follow-up / Expanded Pass

**Original local source:** `deep-research-report (1).md`

# Final Pre-Coding Deep Research for the Valtum Ecosystem

## Executive conclusion

This final research pass changes my conclusion in one important way: **we do not need more broad inspiration research before coding**. The remaining uncertainty is no longer “What does top-tier UI/UX look like?” or “How do award-level websites handle typography, images, horizontal motion, loaders, storytelling, or portfolios?” Those questions are sufficiently answered. What remains is **product truth**: the actual logos, screenshots, videos, feature inventory, brand colors, licensed fonts, real dashboard data, and final claims that belong to the six products. That is an implementation-input problem, not an inspiration problem. The previous deep-research report had already identified exactly those remaining boundaries, and this pass confirms them rather than discovering a missing visual category. fileciteturn0file0

The live-browser investigation is especially important because it prevents this final doctrine from being based on static screenshots. Outfit demonstrated brand continuity from loader through product hover, product detail and bag; Inversa demonstrated named operational states and coherent reverse scrolling; Jasmine Gunarto demonstrated typography as editorial architecture and detailed case-study storytelling; Osmo demonstrated motion as a catalog of reusable interaction primitives. Mobile, keyboard, reduced-motion and measured performance were the major unverified areas in that live run, so this final pass has concentrated particularly heavily on those weaknesses. fileciteturn0file1

My final research-readiness estimate is therefore:

| Layer | Before live inspection | After first final deep research | **After this final deep research** |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Creative-web/UI intelligence | ~71% | ~98% | **~99%** |
| Hero/composition/media intelligence | ~72% | ~97% | **~99%** |
| Motion/scroll/transition intelligence | ~68% | ~96% | **~99%** |
| Product-specific UX architecture | ~65% | ~94% | **~97%** |
| Mobile/accessibility/performance doctrine | ~66% | ~91% | **~97%** |
| Codex design-direction readiness | ~70% | ~90% | **~97%** |
| Exact production specification | — | ~88–90% | **~93–95% until the real assets/product facts are attached** |

That last 5–7% is deliberately not being filled with invention. I will not tell Codex to show “live route optimization,” “99% AI confidence,” a particular personality model, a booking cancellation guarantee, or a specific security response workflow unless the corresponding product really supports it. Current logistics platforms, for example, commonly expose routing, dispatch, driver workflows, exceptions, live tracking and proof of delivery, but that does **not** prove KT Courier implements all of those functions. citeturn21search0turn21search16turn21search20 Likewise, personality-assessment instruments differ materially; IPIP itself contains thousands of public-domain items and hundreds of scales and explicitly warns against referring to one generic measure as “the IPIP.” citeturn20search0turn20search11

So the final rule before Codex touches these websites is:

> **Research is complete. Product verification continues.**

We now know **how** each site should think. We must use the actual project material to determine exactly **what** it says and shows.

The six identities remain deliberately different:

| Website | Experience identity | Visitor should feel |
|---|---|---|
| **Valtum Studio** | Authored editorial product studio | “These people think deeply and can actually build.” |
| **KT Courier** | Operational movement and accountability | “I can see where work is and what happened.” |
| **Dubai Booking Marketplace** | Place, desire, trust and transaction | “I want this experience, and booking it feels safe.” |
| **Math Intellect** | Understanding made manipulable | “I can actually see why this works.” |
| **Personality Assessor** | Humane interpretation of complexity | “This describes dimensions of me rather than putting me in a box.” |
| **Aegis Senital** | Evidence → context → action | “I can understand what happened and know what to do next.” |

That is now the ecosystem-level creative brief.

## Ecosystem-wide design system

### The header and hero are one scene

This is now a **hard design rule across all six websites**.

At page load, there is no independent object called “header” sitting above an object called “hero.” There is **one opening composition**. Logo, navigation, typography, imagery, protagonist, background and opening motion all belong to that composition.

The earlier Glyphic reference is useful because its scientific subject matter, navigation and large visual/type composition occupy one coherent visual field rather than resembling an off-the-shelf navbar added above a hero. More broadly, the best references in our live run made their navigation feel native to their worlds rather than visually interrupting them. fileciteturn0file0

Therefore Codex should ultimately receive a header specification close to this baseline:

| Property | Desktop baseline | Mobile baseline |
|---|---|---|
| Position at first viewport | Absolute/fixed within hero visual field | Same |
| Separate background | **None** | **None** |
| Floating pill/card | **Forbidden** | **Forbidden** |
| Border radius container | **None** | **None** |
| Page inset | approximately 28–40 px depending on composition | approximately 18–22 px |
| Vertical opening offset | approximately 24–32 px | approximately 16–20 px |
| Minimum practical interaction area | target 44–48 px for primary touch controls | target 44–48 px |
| Contrast | Determined by underlying hero state | Determined by underlying hero state |
| Later sticky transformation | Allowed if section state requires it | Allowed if useful |

WCAG 2.2 requires pointer targets to be at least 24×24 CSS pixels or meet defined spacing exceptions; our own 44–48 px design target should be substantially more generous for important touch controls rather than treating the WCAG minimum as an ideal. citeturn24search1

When the hero changes into the next chapter, the header **may transform**. A white wordmark over Dubai photography can become dark text on a white discovery chapter. Aegis may begin embedded in its operational scene and later become a compact persistent utility header.

The transformation must be caused by **the changing composition**, not by a generic rule such as:

> “After 100 px, make navbar white with blur.”

That distinction is important.

And because a persistent header can obscure keyboard-focused controls, the final implementation needs appropriate scroll padding/offset behavior; WCAG's Focus Not Obscured criterion explicitly identifies sticky headers and footers as a common source of this problem. citeturn24search0turn24search4

### Typography is architecture, not decoration

Our strongest references have destroyed the idea that typography is merely:

> H1 72 px  
> H2 48 px  
> Body 18 px.

For these sites, type should have at least four roles:

**Reading typography** communicates information.

**Interface typography** labels actions, states, values and navigation.

**Editorial typography** controls pace and character.

**Spatial typography** participates directly in the composition with media.

Valtum should use that fourth category heavily.

An image may cross a large word.

An object may occlude part of a title.

A title may begin behind one image plane and finish in front of another.

A project frame may occupy the negative space created by a letter.

The critical rule is that this must emerge from the content's geometry—not become an effect that appears in every section.

The type personalities are now sufficiently clear to brief a type-selection process:

| Site | Typographic character | Avoid |
|---|---|---|
| **Valtum** | Authored contemporary display/grotesk relationship with enough width/weight variation for spatial typography | Generic SaaS Inter clone; fake “luxury” serif |
| **KT Courier** | Industrial/humanist clarity, excellent numerals, dense operational labels; optional condensed display companion | Sci-fi logistics type |
| **Dubai Booking** | Contemporary editorial warmth + extremely legible UI/numeral system | Gold luxury serif cliché |
| **Math Intellect** | Calm mathematical/UI face with authoritative notation + expressive display partner | Childish school typography |
| **Personality Assessor** | Human, calm, approachable, non-clinical | Cold dashboard type or wellness-script cliché |
| **Aegis** | Precise, compact interface typography with strong numerals/status labels | Monospace-everywhere “hacker terminal” cliché |

I am **not locking a paid font family without seeing the licensed font assets**. That would be fake precision. At Codex handoff, the exact family, files, weights, variable axes and fallbacks should be specified once we know what Valtum owns or can legally license.

### Color is product-specific

There should not be a shared “Valtum ecosystem palette” applied mechanically to all six properties.

They can share craft without sharing surface treatment.

The live references prove why. Outfit's identity works through cream/red retail graphics; Inversa through ecological material, dark operational surfaces and lime; Jasmine through typography and project-dependent editorial worlds; Osmo through a toolkit/product language. Their sophistication is not caused by one universal premium palette. fileciteturn0file1

Our restrictions remain:

**No gradients used to manufacture sophistication.**

**No beige/cream agency-template system for Valtum.**

**No default purple/blue “AI” visual identity.**

**No darkness simply because somebody has called a design cinematic.**

**No neon cyberpunk treatment simply because Aegis concerns security.**

**No gold-on-black cliché simply because Dubai involves premium experiences.**

The color strategy instead follows information:

Valtum: strong neutral authored field + project colors entering through work.

KT: neutral operational field + constrained status system.

Dubai: imagery supplies much of the chromatic range; interface remains composed enough not to compete with locations.

Math: high-luminance clarity with carefully assigned learning/feedback colors.

Personality: calm multidimensional spectrum without turning scores into a rainbow chart.

Aegis: darker surfaces are legitimate where video/evidence benefits from them, but alert/status colors carry semantic meaning rather than ambient decoration.

### Premium means resolution

The working definition I would now give Codex is:

> **Premium = every visible decision feels resolved.**

Not “make it dark.”

Not “use animation.”

Not “use a huge font.”

It means the crop is intentional. The paragraph width is intentional. A button's selected state is intentional. The previous scene reverses correctly. The mobile image uses an appropriate focal point. A loader releases as soon as it can. A dashboard has real hierarchy. A checkout does not become harder because the marketing page was artistic.

That is the consistent quality standard connecting the six websites.

## Hero and media blueprint

The hero research now gives us a decisive answer: **there is no universal Valtum hero component**.

Each hero requires its own protagonist.

### Valtum Studio

The protagonist is **Valtum's work transforming from idea into functioning product**.

The opening should occupy approximately `100svh` and should not be split into a conventional copy column and image column.

My strongest direction remains:

> **large spatial Valtum typography + actual project artifacts occupying the same depth field.**

Think of the opening composition as three planes:

**Back plane:** restrained background and perhaps one enormous typographic fragment.

**Middle plane:** one or two carefully selected product/interface artifacts.

**Front plane:** essential copy/navigation and perhaps another typographic fragment.

The project media should actually intersect the type. It should not sit inside a generic mockup card with 24 px rounded corners.

The strongest current copy lock candidate remains:

> **Digital products, designed to work in the real world.**

Supporting text should explain Valtum in plain language rather than using agency filler such as “We craft transformative digital experiences at the intersection of design and technology.”

The homepage should then turn the **five portfolio products themselves into the narrative**. The work is proof; therefore it should dominate sooner than an enormous services list.

### KT Courier

The protagonist is **one delivery moving through a controlled system**.

Modern last-mile platforms place real operational importance on planning, routing, dispatch, live order/driver visibility, exception management, driver workflow and proof of delivery. Bringg currently presents those as connected parts of the last-mile system, while Route4Me's current proof workflows include photos, e-signatures, barcode scans, geofenced timestamps and audit-ready activity history. citeturn21search0turn21search19turn21search20

That suggests a hero structure far stronger than:

> delivery person photo | headline | CTA.

Instead:

**background spatial layer:** route/map geometry or authentic location context.

**active delivery:** one parcel/order represented as a stateful object.

**supporting evidence:** courier position, current state, expected progression, perhaps proof/status metadata if the actual product supports it.

**integrated headline:** likely candidate:

> **Every delivery, accounted for.**

The first transition should make the visitor understand that this is not merely a consumer tracking page. It is an operational system.

The narrative state model should be verified against the actual app, but the research-supported ideal is:

> Order → Assign → Pickup → In Transit → Delivered

with exceptions handled as a parallel reality rather than hidden:

> Delay / failed attempt / reassignment → resolved state.

That pattern is grounded in how contemporary delivery products expose route progress, exceptions, status, driver communication and proof rather than pretending every delivery follows a perfect path. citeturn21search0turn21search1

### Dubai Booking Marketplace

This is the **strongest candidate for the full-background-image hero you mentioned**.

And the research is very clear about what that image should be.

Booking products currently use imagery of the actual thing being booked: Aquaventure pages show people at the waterpark, Palm Jumeirah listings show the real setting/views, and GetYourGuide experiences expose the real activity alongside reviews, duration, cancellation policy, date/participant selection and availability. citeturn21search10turn21search17turn21search2turn21search5

Therefore the Dubai hero should use:

> **one exceptional, human-relevant photograph of an actual bookable experience.**

Not a moody black Lamborghini.

Not an empty luxury-hotel corridor.

Not a skyline image chosen solely because it feels expensive.

Not a dark shadow overlay to manufacture drama.

A strong candidate would contain a person actually experiencing the place, with enough environmental context to understand where they are.

The current copy candidate remains:

> **Book the Dubai you came to experience.**

The booking intent can be embedded toward the lower area of the hero:

**What**  
**When**  
**Guests**  
**Search / Explore**

Because the site is Dubai-specific, the visitor should not have to select “Dubai” redundantly unless the product architecture genuinely supports multiple geographies.

Current booking experiences consistently expose decision-critical information such as review scores, cancellation, duration, participants/dates, availability, starting price and activity details close to the selection point. citeturn21search2turn21search4turn21search12

The creative design therefore needs to **collapse into transactional clarity** at the exact moment somebody wants to buy.

That contrast is good design.

### Math Intellect

The protagonist is **a mathematical relationship becoming understandable**.

Mathigon describes its environment around interactive tools, manipulatives, patterns, problem-solving and discovery rather than passive content consumption. citeturn20search1

Therefore a photograph is probably the wrong hero protagonist.

The hero should contain one live mathematical system, for example:

point moves  
→ graph changes  
→ equation changes  
→ geometric relation becomes visible.

Or:

terms move between sides  
→ balance stays equal  
→ algebraic equivalence becomes visual.

This produces the message before the visitor has finished reading it.

Current copy candidate:

> **Understand the idea. Not just the answer.**

Motion here must be explanatory. An equation floating slowly because “motion is premium” should be rejected.

### Personality Assessor

The protagonist is **a multidimensional human profile**.

The biggest design risk is pseudo-scientific certainty.

IPIP's own documentation underscores how many instruments/scales can exist even under a broader personality framework; it explicitly warns that one specific questionnaire should not simply be called “the IPIP.” citeturn20search0turn20search11

Until the actual Personality Assessor model is verified, we should not visually imply:

“five scientifically proven traits”

“six dimensions”

“98% confidence”

or any other invented methodology.

The visual direction should instead communicate:

**dimensions**  
**relative position**  
**relationships**  
**interpretation**  
**nuance**.

Current hero copy candidate:

> **See yourself as a spectrum, not a label.**

Avoid:

brain scans  
glowing heads  
AI orbs  
MBTI-like sixteen-character grids unless the product actually uses such a model  
fake scientific diagrams.

If the instrument is based on IPIP material, IPIP items and scales are public domain, but the specific scale and scoring method still need to be identified accurately rather than represented as one universal IPIP test. citeturn20search0turn20search15

### Aegis Senital

The protagonist is **an event becoming intelligible**.

Current physical-security platforms strongly converge on contextualization: Genetec combines video, access control, intrusion and communication; FlockOS emphasizes one map combining video, sensors and other operational information; Verkada's mobile system surfaces live camera feeds, device/site status and contextual camera information. citeturn22search0turn22search4turn22search5turn22search3

That supports our existing Aegis narrative far more strongly than a generic dark cyber-security page.

Opening world:

**location / site context**

plus

**meaningful camera/evidence surface**

plus

**one event marker**

plus

**one concise incident/status state**

plus

**integrated navigation**

The hero line remains strong:

> **See the signal. Verify the event. Act with context.**

Again, this is only final if the product actually includes the verify/context/action workflow.

Aegis should be allowed to use lower-luminance visual environments because video-surveillance material may genuinely benefit from them.

But:

**dark ≠ premium**  
**neon ≠ intelligent**  
**green boxes ≠ computer vision**  
**monospace ≠ security**.

The narrative, pending product verification, should ideally resemble:

> Detect → Verify → Contextualize → Escalate → Resolve → Audit

because current security platforms explicitly connect event detection, video/evidence, unified context and action. citeturn22search0turn22search5turn22search11

### Image selection and cropping

I am locking one image rule for all six sites:

> **A major image must explain, prove, locate, or create genuine emotional proximity to the subject.**

It can perform more than one.

A beautiful image that does none of those is not hero material.

And image responsiveness will be **art-directed**, not simply:

```css
object-fit: cover;
```

Responsive-image guidance explicitly treats changed crops and even alternate images across viewport sizes as “art direction”; `<picture>` exists for precisely these cases. citeturn25search1

For major images, the eventual Codex specification should therefore have:

| Asset property | Required |
|---|---|
| Exact source file | Yes |
| Subject/focal point | Yes |
| Desktop crop | Yes |
| Mobile crop/source | Yes |
| Aspect ratio | Yes |
| `object-position` | Yes |
| Foreground/midground/background layer | Yes |
| Eager or lazy | Yes |
| Responsive source widths | Yes |
| Alt text | Yes |
| Transition participation | Yes |
| Reduced-motion final state | Where relevant |

For the Dubai example, desktop may position a participant in the right third and leave natural space left for headline text. Mobile should then use a portrait crop/source preserving the person **and** enough of the activity/environment to remain meaningful. It should not merely crop the center 390 pixels out of a desktop photograph.

### Image scrollers

This final research makes me **more convinced that we should not build one shared carousel**.

The right pattern differs by meaning.

**Valtum:** a desktop editorial work rail. Five project worlds can pass through a stable viewport. Media should be large enough to feel like work, not cards. Active project metadata stays tied to the corresponding work. On mobile it becomes a vertical editorial sequence or direct native swipe where appropriate.

**KT Courier:** an operational state rail. It exists because a delivery genuinely moves through states. The scroll direction represents progression, not visual fashion.

**Dubai:** a transactional image gallery. Beautiful, yes, but immediately controllable. On touch it should behave like a natural horizontal gallery rather than a desktop mouse effect forced onto a phone.

**Math:** an interaction sequence rather than an image carousel. Problem → manipulation → feedback → understanding.

**Personality:** probably no large decorative image rail. Result dimensions/insights are the real content.

**Aegis:** an evidence sequence only where an actual incident has several contextual views, not a fashionable carousel of dashboard screenshots.

That is one of the most important anti-template decisions in this entire research.

## Site-specific pre-coding direction

The following is the **near-final experience architecture** I would use as the basis for the Codex specifications. Exact section content can still change when project assets/functionality are audited, but the narrative logic should be very difficult to overturn now.

| Website | Opening | First proof | Main narrative device | Conversion |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Valtum** | Spatial type + project media | Selected real work | Five authored project chapters | Start a project / contact |
| **KT** | One live delivery/system | Actual tracking/dispatch UI | Operational progression | Product/demo/contact depending real site goal |
| **Dubai** | Full-viewport real experience photo | Real experiences + trust facts | Discovery → detail → booking | Book |
| **Math** | Manipulable mathematical relationship | Actual lesson interaction | Concept → action → feedback → insight | Start learning |
| **Personality** | Living multidimensional profile | Actual assessment/result UI | Question → dimension → interpretation | Start assessment |
| **Aegis** | Incident/evidence world | Actual event/security UI | Detect → verify → context → action | Demo/contact/access depending product |

**Valtum Studio.**  
The homepage should be less like a service brochure and more like an authored argument. We should not show ten equal portfolio cards. The five projects are different enough that each deserves its own visual field. The transition from homepage work into a case study should preserve visual identity where practical, because the user selected an object and should feel that the object became the case study rather than that the old page vanished and another unrelated page loaded. Jasmine's live-inspected case-study architecture is especially valuable here because process, roles, tools, reflection and credits turn finished visuals into credible evidence. fileciteturn0file1

Valtum's case-study architecture should be:

> opening result → product problem → constraints/context → Valtum's role → experience concept → pivotal decisions → interfaces/system → implementation → result/evidence → reflection → next project.

This is preferable to:

> hero → screenshots → screenshots → screenshots → next project.

The hero should show the actual work immediately enough that a prospective client understands Valtum is not simply another beautiful-studio landing page.

**KT Courier.**  
The product should make movement legible. Current last-mile systems combine planning/dispatch, real-time visibility, exceptions, driver workflows and delivery evidence; that is why stateful storytelling is a much more appropriate design language than feature cards. citeturn21search0turn21search18turn21search20

Dashboard hierarchy should privilege:

current exceptions  
active deliveries  
routes/couriers  
time/ETA  
proof/status  
historical performance

rather than filling a screen with equal-weight charts.

A delivery should always answer:

> Where is it?  
> What state is it in?  
> What happened last?  
> What is expected next?  
> Is intervention required?

That is the core interface narrative.

**Dubai Booking Marketplace.**  
The emotional opening needs to hand off extremely quickly to confidence. Booking pages currently expose cancellation, duration, rating/reviews, participants/date, availability and price precisely because desire by itself does not complete a transaction. citeturn21search2turn21search5turn21search17

The home architecture should therefore read approximately:

> experience hero → direct discovery/search → curated experiences → categories/context → destination storytelling → trust/proof → conversion.

The detail page:

> media gallery → title/review/essential facts → availability/date/guests → price → inclusions → itinerary/details → map/location → operator/trust → policy → related experiences.

The creative layer should **decrease** as transaction risk increases. Checkout is not the place to prove that we know GSAP.

**Math Intellect.**  
The site should treat interaction as explanation. Mathigon's current product framing explicitly emphasizes interactive tools, manipulatives, problem-solving and discovery, which is a more relevant reference than generic education SaaS dashboards. citeturn20search1

The learning architecture should favor:

> prompt → manipulate → observe consequence → receive feedback → articulate insight → extend.

Progress should represent concept mastery/progression, not just gamification decoration.

The best motion on Math Intellect will often be **small and causal**:

drag point → slope changes  
change coefficient → graph updates  
rotate shape → invariant remains  
move term → balance responds.

That will feel more advanced than a hundred parallax effects because motion itself becomes knowledge.

**Personality Assessor.**  
The assessment must slow the visual language down.

Not boring.

Calm.

One question should own the moment. Progress should be visible without pressuring the user. Selection should have immediate but restrained feedback. Back navigation should be safe. Resume/autosave should be considered if supported by the actual product.

The results architecture should progress:

> summary → dimensions → relationships → interpretation → examples/implications → methodology/limitations.

This is where honesty can itself become premium. The product should not use false precision to appear scientific.

And we need the actual model before writing final results copy because even within IPIP-based work there are many distinct inventories/scales. citeturn20search0turn20search11

**Aegis Senital.**  
The marketing site and the operational application should have related DNA but **different motion tolerances**.

The marketing site can narrate an incident.

The command application must optimize decision speed.

Current physical-security products increasingly centralize multiple signals so operators can understand events in context rather than jumping across unrelated systems. citeturn22search4turn22search5turn22search18

The desktop operational screen should answer:

> What needs my attention now?  
> What is the evidence?  
> Where is it?  
> What else is related?  
> What has already happened?  
> What action is available?  
> Who acted?  
> What is the audit trail?

The mobile screen should **not be a shrunken command center**. Verkada's current mobile approach, for example, prioritizes live camera feeds, incident-relevant context, device/site health and actionable control rather than shrinking an entire wall of desktop information onto a phone. citeturn22search3

That strongly supports a focused mobile incident architecture.

## Motion, interaction and scrolling specification

The most important change after all these references is that I no longer think in terms of:

> “What animation do we add?”

I think in terms of:

> **What state is changing, and what should persist?**

Our ecosystem should use a named motion grammar:

| Primitive | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Reveal** | Information becomes relevant |
| **Carry** | An important object survives into the next state |
| **Exchange** | The same idea changes medium |
| **Pin** | The world remains stable while one variable progresses |
| **Rail** | The user traverses a genuine sequence/collection |
| **Inspect** | Interaction exposes deeper evidence |
| **Resolve** | Complexity settles into a clear final state |
| **Route** | Selected content visually carries into a new page/context |

That is much more useful for Codex than “use GSAP creatively.”

### Smooth scrolling

**Use smooth scrolling where it improves authored narrative. Do not install it ecosystem-wide because premium websites use Lenis.**

Lenis currently runs on native scroll and supports vertical/horizontal configurations, but its documentation also notes specific considerations: anchors are disabled unless explicitly enabled, nested scrolling needs handling, CSS scroll-snap requires Lenis's own snap mechanism, and `syncTouch` has iOS caveats. citeturn23search0turn23search4

My final recommendation:

| Surface | Smooth scrolling |
|---|---|
| Valtum marketing site | **Yes, desktop**, restrained |
| Valtum case studies | Yes where transitions need it; native semantics preserved |
| KT marketing | Light desktop smoothing only |
| KT operational app | **No** |
| Dubai editorial/discovery | Light/optional desktop smoothing |
| Dubai search/detail/booking/checkout | **No ornamental smoothing** |
| Math marketing | Optional |
| Math learning interaction | **Native/direct** |
| Personality marketing | Optional/light |
| Personality assessment | **Native/direct** |
| Aegis marketing | Optional |
| Aegis operations | **Native/direct** |

On touch devices, native behavior should generally dominate. There is no benefit in making a finger feel artificially delayed.

### Horizontal scrolling

Horizontal movement is approved only where it encodes:

**progression**  
**collection**  
**space**.

GSAP ScrollTrigger can natively support scrubbed timelines, pinning, snapping, horizontal behavior, velocity and interruption/fast-scroll handling, so we have sufficient implementation machinery without inventing a custom scroll engine. citeturn23search1

The critical implementation rule is:

> **Pin the stage; animate the content within the stage.**

The pinned section must still behave correctly when the visitor scrolls backward, flings through quickly, changes viewport dimensions or navigates directly.

No one-way animation.

No “it looked right when I recorded it slowly.”

The direct Inversa inspection already proved why reverse choreography matters: its Predictive Heatmaps state coherently returned into Body Cams when the user reversed. fileciteturn0file1

### Mobile horizontal behavior

This is now settled.

A desktop horizontal narrative does **not** automatically remain horizontal on mobile.

WCAG's reflow criterion requires content to work at the equivalent of 320 CSS pixels without two-dimensional scrolling except where the information genuinely requires a two-dimensional layout, such as maps, diagrams, data tables and certain manipulation interfaces. citeturn24search5

Therefore:

> Valtum desktop project rail → mobile editorial vertical project chapters or direct finger-controlled gallery.

> KT process rail → stacked sequential states or controlled swipe states.

> Dubai gallery → native horizontal touch gallery.

> Aegis narrative → stacked incident states.

> Math interactive graph → can preserve necessary two-dimensional manipulation because the diagram itself may require it, while explanatory text still reflows.

### Drag interactions

No essential function will require drag alone.

WCAG 2.2 explicitly requires a non-dragging single-pointer alternative unless dragging itself is essential. citeturn24search2

So an Osmo-style draggable gallery can still exist, but it must also have:

arrow controls  
click/tap selection  
keyboard operation  
or another equivalent.

Math Intellect is a special case: dragging a mathematical point may itself be educational, but we should still give alternative ways to alter the value whenever practical.

### Loader doctrine

Outfit remains the strongest loader reference **for Valtum**, but we are borrowing the logic, not the literal implementation.

The direct inspection showed a black `000` state, subsequent branded transition behavior, and continuity from loader into wordmark/shop/product/cart. fileciteturn0file1

The transferable idea is:

> **Waiting can establish identity when the transition into the real page is continuous.**

The non-transferable elements are:

`000`  
`091`  
Outfit's wordmark  
red/cream system  
image-stack choreography.

For Valtum, a loader should exist **only if real critical media needs enough time that an arrival state improves the experience**.

Never:

> minimum 3-second loader because it looks cool.

Never:

> fake 0–100 counter disconnected from real work.

Never:

> hide a ready page while animation finishes.

If the initial composition can render, release it.

And I would not put full branded preloaders on all five product sites. That would itself become a template signature.

### Timing grammar

Before detailed motion design, I would give Codex these approximate ranges as starting tokens—not absolute laws:

| Interaction scale | Baseline |
|---|---:|
| Tiny state feedback | ~120–200 ms |
| Hover/focus/media inspect | ~180–320 ms |
| UI panel/menu state | ~280–450 ms |
| Larger structural entrance | ~450–750 ms |
| Authored page transition | ~600–900 ms |
| Scroll narrative | Progress-linked rather than fixed-duration |

The defining value is not duration alone; **easing and continuity matter more**.

Scroll-linked changes that express actual progress should generally use direct/progress mapping rather than decorative easing. State transitions can use softer easing.

And interruption must work. GSAP's ScrollTrigger exposes velocity, snapping interruption and fast-scroll behavior specifically because real users do not scroll at cinematic-demo speed. citeturn23search1

## Mobile, accessibility and performance acceptance criteria

This was the largest unverified hole in the Work browser run, so I am making it a first-class production requirement rather than an afterthought. fileciteturn0file1

### Mobile is recomposition, not reduction

The design workflow for all six websites should be:

> desktop concept + mobile concept developed together

not:

> finish 1440 px → stack everything at 390 px.

For each major scene we will explicitly decide:

**What remains?**

**What disappears?**

**What changes medium?**

**What becomes touch-controlled?**

**What stops being pinned?**

**What changes crop?**

**What changes reading order?**

**What becomes more prominent because mobile context differs?**

That produces very different adaptations.

Valtum's overlapping spatial type may simplify but retain one purposeful media/type intersection.

Dubai's wide hero photo gets a separate portrait art direction.

KT's route world might prioritize current delivery status before its wider map.

Aegis mobile prioritizes incident evidence/action over dashboard overview.

Math makes manipulation areas larger rather than shrinking an entire desktop workspace.

Personality gives the question itself more viewport ownership.

### Reduced motion is a separate composition

Reduced motion should not equal:

```css
* {
  animation: none !important;
}
```

That can destroy state relationships.

Instead:

**full-motion Valtum:** selected project image travels/shared-transitions into case study.

**reduced-motion Valtum:** destination page opens with the image already in the corresponding final position and uses a short opacity transition.

**full-motion Aegis:** camera evidence expands, location shifts, contextual state attaches.

**reduced-motion Aegis:** same information remains in a stable grid while emphasis/state changes without large spatial movement.

**full-motion Math:** point/graph relationships animate continuously.

**reduced-motion Math:** direct value/state change remains readable; only nonessential travel is suppressed.

This preserves meaning while reducing vestibular movement.

### Touch and controls

WCAG's formal minimum is 24×24 CSS pixels or sufficient spacing, but our product interfaces should usually target approximately 44–48 px for primary touch controls where density permits. citeturn24search1

No critical hover-only information.

Anything available through Outfit-like hover reveal on desktop must remain directly accessible on touch.

No custom cursor logic on touch.

No tiny carousel arrows.

No “swipe to discover” as the only way to discover content.

### Keyboard

Every custom menu, gallery, modal, booking selector, assessment control and command interface needs a deliberate keyboard path.

Sticky headers cannot fully hide focused elements. citeturn24search0

Drag interactions need simple alternatives. citeturn24search2

Focus should follow visual/modal state rather than remaining invisibly behind overlays.

The visually creative surface is not allowed to create a second-class keyboard site.

### Imagery and slow connections

Your slow-network warning to Work produced an important insight: the correct production solution is **not asking real visitors to be equally patient**.

Above-the-fold critical images should be prioritized. Responsive image sources prevent a phone from downloading an unnecessarily large desktop asset, and current web.dev guidance describes responsive preload for critical images when the resource cannot be discovered early enough in markup. citeturn25search0

For a major photographic hero:

**desktop receives appropriately sized landscape source**

**mobile receives portrait art-directed source**

**LCP image is not lazy-loaded**

**below-fold media is lazy-loaded**

**width/height or aspect ratio is reserved before arrival**

**the page still communicates something useful before every secondary image/video arrives**.

If the image is easily discoverable in initial HTML, web.dev specifically recommends allowing normal preload scanning rather than unnecessarily preloading everything; priority hints/preloads are tools for genuinely critical/discoverability-constrained resources. citeturn25search0

This is crucial for Valtum because one of our most dangerous failure modes would be:

> beautiful 100 MB portfolio that only feels premium on our development machine.

### Performance is a design requirement

I am locking the Core Web Vitals “good” thresholds as our minimum field targets:

**LCP ≤ 2.5 s**  
**INP ≤ 200 ms**  
**CLS ≤ 0.1**  
evaluated at the 75th percentile and separately across device classes where field data is available. citeturn26search3turn26search5

That does not mean we merely pass Lighthouse.

It means design decisions can be challenged.

A 40 MB video hero is not sacred.

A giant WebGL scene is not sacred.

A smooth-scroll library is not sacred.

A 500-frame image sequence is not sacred.

If a simpler medium gives the same narrative result with dramatically better responsiveness, the simpler medium wins.

web.dev's current performance recommendations emphasize making LCP resources discoverable/prioritized, sizing dynamic content to avoid layout shifts, reducing unnecessary JavaScript and avoiding large rendering work. citeturn26search6

And this is precisely where the latest four-reference research strengthens us: Jasmine showed how far high-quality editorial work can go without treating real-time 3D as mandatory, while Osmo showed that individual interaction modules can be selectively employed instead of forcing every page into one giant experiential renderer. fileciteturn0file1

## Final Codex handoff doctrine

The most important result of these two final deep-research passes is that **Codex will not be asked to art-direct these websites**.

It will implement art direction.

When we begin a website, I will eventually describe each major scene in a specification structured like this:

| Specification field | Example of the required precision |
|---|---|
| **Purpose** | What the visitor should understand after the scene |
| **Exact text** | The actual heading/body/CTA, not “add compelling copy” |
| **Grid** | Columns, insets, gaps, max widths |
| **Viewport state** | Exact desktop/tablet/mobile composition |
| **Asset** | Actual filename/CMS reference |
| **Crop** | Aspect ratio, focal point, alternate mobile asset |
| **Type** | Exact family/file/weight/size/line-height/tracking |
| **Layering** | Which media sits above/below type |
| **Motion** | Trigger, start/end, movement, easing/scrub, persistence |
| **Reverse** | Exact appearance when movement reverses |
| **Interaction** | Hover/touch/keyboard/focus/selected/loading/error |
| **Reduced motion** | Alternate final-state composition |
| **Loading** | Eager/lazy/preload/priority behavior |
| **Fallback** | What survives without enhancement |
| **Acceptance** | What constitutes “correct” |

So instead of:

> “Create a premium horizontally scrolling portfolio.”

Codex should get something conceptually like:

> At desktop widths ≥1280 px, Selected Work occupies one narrative rail containing five project chapters. The section becomes a viewport-height stage; vertical scroll advances the internal work track laterally. Do not translate the pinned outer stage. Keep the project index and project metadata synchronized with the active media. The active project should occupy approximately two-thirds of the visual width rather than appearing as a card. Reverse scroll must reconstruct the previous project state continuously. At widths below the final tablet breakpoint, disable the pinned rail and render the same projects in editorial vertical sequence. Do not preserve horizontal scrolling merely for visual consistency.

Instead of:

> “Make the Dubai hero cinematic.”

Codex should receive:

> Use the supplied experience photograph `X` as the first-viewport protagonist. On desktop, preserve subject `Y` in the right third, leaving the naturally quieter left field for the headline. Do not add a bottom black gradient. If contrast is inadequate, use the specified uniform scrim only. Use portrait asset `Z` below the mobile breakpoint rather than center-cropping the desktop source. Set the image as the likely LCP asset; do not lazy-load it. The navigation is rendered directly over the same photographic field without a floating container.

Instead of:

> “Add cool scrolling animations.”

It should receive:

> Pin the stage for this defined scroll interval. Drive only the inner state timeline. State A and State B must coexist during the specified transition window so the user understands that the object is changing state, not disappearing and being replaced. Scroll reversal maps continuously to the same timeline. Reduced-motion users receive the final-state content in normal document flow rather than the pinned transformation.

And instead of:

> “Use the Outfit loader.”

Codex should receive:

> Build a Valtum-specific arrival transition only if the measured critical opening asset requires enough settling time to justify it. The loader must derive from Valtum's identity and must hand a visual object into the opening hero. Do not reproduce Outfit's numeric counter, wordmark motion, palette or image stack. Do not impose an artificial minimum wait. Release the hero immediately once its essential state is ready.

That is how the research will actually translate into code.

The anti-copy test will remain mandatory:

> **If the reference disappeared tomorrow, would this decision still make sense because of Valtum, KT, Dubai, Math, Personality or Aegis itself?**

If not, reject it.

The second test is ecosystem differentiation:

> **Could I swap this hero/section into another one of our six websites by changing the logo and text?**

If yes, the design is probably not specific enough.

The third is semantic motion:

> **If the animation were removed, could I explain why that motion existed?**

If the only answer is “it looked premium,” reject it.

The fourth is imagery:

> **What does this image tell the visitor that a different pretty image would not?**

If there is no answer, replace it.

The fifth is mobile:

> **Did we design a mobile experience, or merely shrink the desktop one?**

The sixth is product truth:

> **Can we prove the claim/state/data shown here exists?**

This is particularly important for security, personality assessment, logistics and booking. Current competitors show what users reasonably expect from those product categories, but competitor capabilities are not evidence of our own products' capabilities. citeturn21search0turn22search5turn20search0

## Final evidence boundary and readiness

The research phase itself is now effectively complete.

There is **no meaningful remaining general UI/UX gap** that I think another week of browsing award sites would solve.

The unresolved inputs are concrete:

| Required input | Why it matters |
|---|---|
| Actual Valtum logo/brand assets | Locks identity and exact header composition |
| Existing colors/brand rules | Locks final palettes |
| Available font licenses/files | Locks exact typography |
| Screenshots for all five portfolio products | Determines real hero/case-study media |
| Product videos/recordings | Determines whether motion can use real product behavior |
| Real project functionality | Prevents fictional feature claims |
| Real dashboard/sample data | Prevents placeholder-looking operational UI |
| Final business/service positioning | Locks Valtum homepage copy |
| Real outcomes/results where publishable | Gives case studies proof |
| Personality model/scoring methodology | Locks assessment language/result visualization |
| Dubai booking/payment/product scope | Locks transactional architecture |
| KT operational scope | Locks actual delivery-state narrative |
| Aegis capability/event model | Locks incident workflow |
| Math curriculum/interactions | Locks which mathematical concept becomes protagonist |

Those were already identified as the remaining boundaries after the first deep-research pass, and this second pass confirms they are the correct ones. fileciteturn0file0

Once those inputs are available, there is no reason for Codex to receive vague art-direction language.

My final readiness assessment is:

> **General UI/UX research readiness: 99%**

> **Valtum Studio creative-direction readiness: 99%**

> **Five portfolio-site creative-direction readiness: 97–98%**

> **Motion/scroll/image-scroller readiness: 99%**

> **Mobile/accessibility/performance doctrine readiness: 97–98%**

> **Exact Codex specification readiness right now: approximately 94%**

> **Expected Codex specification readiness after the actual project assets/functions are mapped: approximately 99%.**

I would not describe the missing one percent as “more inspiration.” Some uncertainty should survive until we put the actual media into real browser compositions, because typography, crop, line breaks, scroll distances, media weight and transition timing have to be tuned against real assets rather than guessed in prose.

The final creative doctrine I will carry into implementation is therefore:

> **Valtum is authored, not templated.**

> **KT makes movement and accountability visible.**

> **Dubai lets the real experience create desire, then makes the transaction effortless.**

> **Math turns abstract relationships into something the learner can manipulate.**

> **Personality turns complexity into humane interpretation without fake certainty.**

> **Aegis turns evidence into context and context into action.**

Across all six:

> **The header belongs to the hero. Typography builds space. Images show the real subject. HD quality is necessary but relevance is more important than artificial “cinematic” darkness. Motion explains change. Horizontal movement earns its existence. Loaders serve readiness and identity rather than ego. Complex scenes reverse. Mobile is recomposed. Touch and keyboard are first-class. Reduced motion preserves meaning. Performance is part of visual quality. And Codex implements explicit decisions rather than inventing design on our behalf.** fileciteturn0file0 fileciteturn0file1

# SOURCE REPORT — Behance + Dribbble Design Intelligence

**Original local source:** `deep-research-report (2).md`

# Behance + Dribbble Design Intelligence for Valtum and Its Product Portfolio

## Research frame and what “top-tier” actually means

I treated this as a **design-intelligence investigation**, not a moodboard exercise. The sampling priority was current **Behance UI/UX curation, Best of Behance, Dribbble Select, Dribbble’s current agency/editorial curation, and representative agency work**, rather than simply sorting by likes. Behance explicitly describes its UI/UX gallery and Best of Behance as work hand-picked by its curation team; Dribbble Select likewise says its selections are intended to go beyond surface aesthetics and emphasize clarity, usability, product thinking, storytelling, and real-world outcomes. Dribbble’s Select agency directory additionally says Select agencies are vetted for portfolio quality and professional work, with an emphasis on real client work over speculative concepts. citeturn20search0turn20search20turn21search3turn21search5

This distinction matters because **Behance and Dribbble are presentation environments**. A beautiful shot can prove visual judgment, composition, typography, illustration, or interaction conception; it does not automatically prove that a product works under keyboard navigation, a 390-pixel viewport, real data, a slow connection, a 200-item list, a failed API response, or six months of production use. Dribbble itself effectively acknowledges this problem by describing its higher-level Select curation in terms of usability, user journeys, product performance and business goals rather than aesthetics alone. citeturn21search0turn21search4

That means this research sits on top of—not in place of—our previous live-browser work. The Outfit/Inversa/Jasmine/Osmo inspection already gave us direct behavioral evidence for loader continuity, reversible scroll narratives, editorial portfolio pacing, and reusable interaction primitives. fileciteturn0file2 Our two previous pre-coding research passes also established the existing Valtum doctrine: **header and hero as one composition, typography as spatial architecture, semantically useful imagery, motion that explains state change, horizontal movement that earns its existence, mobile recomposition, progressive enhancement, and product-specific identities across the six sites.** fileciteturn0file1 fileciteturn0file0

What Behance and Dribbble add is a different layer: **highly refined art direction, case-study presentation, mobile interface composition, visual-system breadth, component treatment, dashboard density, editorial presentation, and a much larger sample of how professional designers package a concept into a coherent visual system.** Behance’s current curated UI/UX stream, for example, spans language learning, finance SaaS, CRM, mobile finance, logistics, construction SaaS, e-commerce and banking rather than presenting one universal visual style. citeturn20search0turn20search1

The most important conclusion is therefore not “this style is trending.” It is:

> **The strongest designers repeatedly derive the visual system from the nature of the product, then use hierarchy, imagery, typography, interaction and component logic to make that idea coherent across every screen.**

That conclusion is consistent with Dribbble’s 2026 agency analysis: its strongest agencies are described not merely as visual stylists but as teams combining usability, product thinking, motion, branding, interaction design, design systems and implementation thinking. citeturn17search3turn21search2

### The Behance/Dribbble layer now added to our design brain

| Layer | What the research changes |
|---|---|
| **Concept development** | Start from a native truth of the product, not a visual effect. |
| **Art direction** | Build a coherent visual world from that truth rather than decorating a generic wireframe. |
| **Hero design** | Choose a protagonist before choosing a layout. |
| **Typography** | Use type to establish geometry, identity, rhythm and information hierarchy—not merely headings. |
| **Imagery** | Use subject-relevant images, UI, illustration, 3D or data depending on what best explains the product. |
| **Color** | Build hierarchy and identity; never use “premium dark,” gradients or muted beige as universal recipes. |
| **Cards** | Treat cards as semantic containers, not as the default shape for every section. |
| **Mobile** | Redesign priority and interaction for the context rather than shrinking desktop. |
| **Motion** | Give movement a role: explain, carry, reveal, connect, demonstrate or orient. |
| **Case studies** | Treat portfolio presentation itself as an authored narrative, while keeping it distinct from the actual product UI. |
| **Production** | Translate all of the above into states, systems and responsive rules rather than isolated polished screens. |

This also reinforces Apple’s newly refreshed design principles: purpose, agency, familiarity, flexibility, simplicity, craft and delight are presented as interlocking concerns, with an explicit warning not to mistake delight for decoration. citeturn18search2

## The agency brain behind the strongest work

The largest pattern across the agency research is that **top studios do not begin by designing sections**. They establish a conceptual relationship between the subject and the digital experience, and then repeatedly express that relationship through composition, typography, media, interaction and product structure. citeturn21search2turn17search8

Consider three radically different examples.

**Phenomenon Studio’s BioForge** takes the precision, sterility, robotics and controlled processes of biotech manufacturing and turns those concepts into structured layouts, sharp typography, cool technical tones, negative space, robotic imagery and restrained microinteractions. The page is not merely “a beautiful biotech landing page”; its visual discipline mirrors the operational discipline it is describing. citeturn17search0turn21search4

**Bearplus’s CargoKite** does something entirely different. The product is a kite-powered vessel, so the ship itself becomes the protagonist; interactive 3D and horizontal movement are used to make traversing the website feel related to traversing an ocean journey. Its horizontal navigation therefore has a semantic reason rather than existing because horizontal scrolling is fashionable. citeturn17search1turn17search4turn21search3

**Unseen Studio’s Illoca** derives its identity from architecture’s beginning on paper: stacked drawings, trace overlays, pencil marks and layered thinking. The agency states that the brand and web system were rooted in precisely this process. That is an exceptionally useful model for us: **find the native material, action or metaphor of the product and allow that to generate the visual language.** citeturn17search2

That is the studio-level thinking I want us to adopt.

### Representative agency intelligence

| Studio / project | What the work is doing | What matters to us |
|---|---|---|
| **Phenomenon Studio — BioForge** | Structured layouts, sharp type, restrained color, large robotics/cleanroom imagery, subtle interaction; operational information follows the real manufacturing subject. citeturn17search0turn21search4 | **Subject → visual system.** Precision is communicated structurally, not through generic futuristic decoration. |
| **Bearplus — CargoKite** | 3D ship protagonist and horizontal storytelling turn an unfamiliar maritime technology into something spatially understandable. citeturn17search1turn21search3 | Interaction is strongest when it represents the product’s actual behavior or story. |
| **Unseen Studio — Illoca** | The brand derives from architectural drawings, tracing paper, layers and manual marks. citeturn17search2 | Build visual identity from a **native domain artifact**, not from design trends. |
| **Arounda — Auralis** | Uses a layered information architecture described as summary first, explanation second, action third; photography, muted greens and serif/sans typography separate it from generic AI products. citeturn22search0 | Information architecture and brand differentiation should be designed simultaneously. |
| **Red Lions** | Dribbble highlights tokens, components, Auto Layout and implementation-oriented design-system thinking rather than isolated screens. citeturn21search2 | Top-tier UI is a **system that survives growth**, not a collection of Figma paintings. |
| **Orix Creative** | Treats branding and UI as one product ecosystem: typography, color, composition and interaction behave as parts of the same identity. citeturn21search2 | Brand must survive inside forms, cards, navigation and product states—not end at the marketing hero. |
| **Agensip / WareTrack** | Operational SaaS is simplified through structured layouts, restrained treatment and workflow organization. citeturn21search2 | Enterprise does not need visual clutter to prove complexity. |
| **Meya Lab / healthcare dashboards** | Modular hierarchy and controlled density make large information sets more quickly scannable. citeturn21search2 | Dashboard polish means prioritization, not more widgets. |
| **Allonsy** | Brand storytelling, strong composition and layered motion are used to move beyond predictable landing-page sequences. citeturn21search2 | Narrative web work can be expressive without losing information structure. |
| **Wowsy.lab** | Dribbble explicitly contrasts its AI work with interchangeable dark dashboards and generic gradients, emphasizing composition, typography and controlled pacing instead. citeturn21search2 | Particularly relevant to our existing **no generic AI aesthetic** rule. |
| **Ryven Global** | Dark-mode enterprise UX is treated as hierarchy, spacing and workflow separation rather than “black + neon.” citeturn21search2 | Highly relevant to Aegis: darkness can be functional without becoming cyberpunk. |

Another agency-level lesson comes from the scope of professional design services themselves. Arounda’s current Behance service descriptions treat serious UI/UX delivery as research and flows, high-fidelity layouts, key app states, interactive prototypes, responsive/adaptive breakpoints, reusable style guides/design systems, accessibility checks and UX audits—not merely polished hero screens. citeturn22search8turn22search12

That is exactly the mentality our eventual Codex specifications need to embody.

### The design process I now regard as “top-tier studio thinking”

The recurring evidence suggests a process closer to this:

> **Product truth**  
> → **user objective**  
> → **business objective**  
> → **category constraints**  
> → **emotional objective**  
> → **native concept/metaphor**  
> → **visual protagonist**  
> → **information architecture**  
> → **composition grammar**  
> → **typography system**  
> → **media system**  
> → **component/system grammar**  
> → **motion grammar**  
> → **mobile/context adaptation**  
> → **states and edge cases**  
> → **proof**  
> → **production refinement**

This is a synthesis from the subject-derived systems in BioForge, CargoKite and Illoca; the brand/product integration emphasized by current Dribbble agency curation; and the scalable product-system approach visible in Red Lions, Agensip and Arounda. citeturn17search0turn17search1turn17search2turn21search2turn22search12

Notice what is absent:

> “Find a cool website and reproduce its section order.”

That is not how we should design Valtum.

## Web visual grammar: heroes, images, typography, colors, cards and scrollers

### The hero is increasingly a thesis, not a template

The strongest examples repeatedly avoid beginning with the abstract layout decision:

> text left, visual right.

Instead they ask what object or idea can communicate the proposition fastest.

BioForge uses robotic manufacturing/cleanroom subject matter to establish technical credibility. CargoKite uses the vessel itself. Illoca uses the physical language of architectural drafting. Dribbble’s current best-web curation explicitly praises product-centric and domain-specific approaches because they make complex ideas understandable faster than generic explanation. citeturn21search4turn17search0turn17search1turn17search2

This produces several hero archetypes that are genuinely useful to us:

| Hero model | Appropriate when | Core principle |
|---|---|---|
| **Full-bleed photographic world** | Place, hospitality, travel, physical activity or human context is the product | Let the actual environment carry emotion. |
| **Central protagonist** | One physical/digital object embodies the product | Give it spatial dominance and let copy orbit it. |
| **Type + protagonist interlock** | Brand identity and product media can occupy one field | Typography becomes architecture; media may cross or occlude it. |
| **Functional 3D hero** | Shape, movement or spatial mechanism materially helps explain the product | 3D is explanatory, not ornamental. |
| **Live interface hero** | Product behavior itself is the proof | Demonstrate the workflow instead of showing a device mockup. |
| **Diagram/data hero** | Relationship, system, network or mathematics is the product | Let information itself become graphic form. |
| **Editorial image/type hero** | Authorship, culture, fashion, architecture or portfolio identity is central | Scale, crop and negative space become the expressive mechanism. |

This fits our existing Valtum doctrine rather than replacing it. The hero and header remain **one visual state**, with the header participating in the protagonist’s world rather than floating in a separate capsule. fileciteturn0file0

### Imagery: the strongest visual is usually the most specific one

Your correction about “cinematic” imagery is strongly supported by the work I studied.

BioForge’s large visuals focus on robotics, automation and cleanroom systems because those subjects explain the operation. CargoKite uses ships because ships are the technology. Illoca’s graphical imagery references actual architectural process. Auralis intentionally uses natural photography to move away from data-heavy generic AI imagery. citeturn17search0turn17search1turn17search2turn22search0

The resulting rule is stronger than “use high-quality photography”:

> **Every major image must answer why this particular image belongs to this particular sentence, section or state.**

An image can justify itself by doing one or more of these jobs:

**Explain** — show the thing being discussed.

**Prove** — show real evidence that a claim is true.

**Locate** — establish environment, scale or context.

**Humanize** — connect an abstract product to the person affected by it.

**Demonstrate** — show behavior or transformation.

**Create continuity** — carry a subject from one scene into another.

This extends the imagery doctrine already established by our earlier research. fileciteturn0file1

Accordingly, I would reject an HD photograph with dramatic lighting if a completely different company could use the same image without anyone noticing.

That means:

**KT Courier:** an actual delivery, route, driver, parcel, warehouse or proof-of-delivery scene beats generic “fast truck at night.”

**Dubai Booking:** actual people experiencing an actual bookable destination beats a dark supercar or anonymous hotel hallway.

**Math Intellect:** a meaningful graph, geometric manipulation or live relationship beats a child looking thoughtfully at a laptop.

**Personality Assessor:** real assessment/result material or restrained human context beats an AI-generated glowing head.

**Aegis:** authentic camera evidence, location, event context and operator information beat random server rooms and neon grids.

**Valtum:** actual project artifacts and interfaces beat generic studio laptops.

Those choices follow directly from the domain-specific imagery logic visible in the strongest work and our earlier product-specific research. citeturn17search0turn17search2 fileciteturn0file0

### Typography: there is no “premium font,” only the right typographic behavior

BioForge describes its typography as sharp and optimized for scannability and authority. Auralis deliberately combines serif and sans typography to create a more human/natural identity distinct from data-heavy AI competitors. Dribbble’s current agency curation repeatedly cites bold or precise typography as part of hierarchy rather than as an independent decorative trend. citeturn17search0turn22search0turn21search2

The important pattern is not one specific family. It is **typographic role separation**.

For our work, I now want to treat typography as at least five systems:

| Role | Requirement |
|---|---|
| **Spatial/display** | Controls composition, scale, line breaks and media relationships. |
| **Editorial heading** | Creates chapter rhythm and recognizable voice. |
| **Reading text** | Comfortable width, line height, contrast and long-form consistency. |
| **Interface text** | Highly legible labels, states and actions at small sizes. |
| **Numerical/data** | Stable numerals, tabular figures where necessary, strong differentiation for operational information. |

A single family can serve several roles where its variable axes and glyph design support them; other projects may need a display/utility pairing. The final choice should always be judged in **real strings and real layouts**, not from a specimen showing “Aa Bb Cc.”

There is one important evidence boundary here: **many Behance and Dribbble case studies do not disclose their exact typefaces or implementation font files**. Some disclose broad type direction, tools or styling; some do not. I will therefore not reverse-identify a commercial font from a screenshot and present that guess as fact. For our actual sites, final font choice should be confirmed through the project’s licensed assets or, when researching a shipped reference, through its live CSS/font resources.

That is more professional than pretending every geometric grotesk is “Neue Montreal.”

### Color: hierarchy before palette fashion

The selected work demonstrates that there is no universal premium palette.

BioForge’s Dribbble shot exposes a palette spanning near-black, blue-gray, muted blue, gray and several secondary tones, while its creators describe the main emotional strategy as cool grays and surgical blues. citeturn17search0 Auralis uses muted greens and natural photography to distinguish itself from conventional AI products. citeturn22search0 Autism Care Centre uses softer colors to reduce cognitive load, while Cerebra uses a much darker enterprise-security system; both appear in the same Dribbble Select UI/UX curation because their palettes solve different problems. citeturn21search0

The useful palette model is therefore:

> **base field**  
> + **primary foreground hierarchy**  
> + **identity accent(s)**  
> + **semantic/state colors where needed**  
> + **media colors**

Rather than:

> background #1  
> gradient #2  
> glass card #3  
> neon button #4.

Dribbble’s own curated material includes projects using gradients. That proves only that gradients can belong to some visual systems; it does **not** make them a requirement for high-end UI. Our own no-gradient rule therefore remains intact. We take the underlying hierarchy and color discipline, not a surface treatment that conflicts with our system. citeturn21search0turn21search2

### Cards: top-tier design does not mean putting everything in rounded rectangles

This is one of the clearest Behance/Dribbble lessons.

Data-heavy fintech and enterprise interfaces frequently use modular cards because **portfolio values, charts, status summaries and discrete metrics really are separate information units**. Dribbble specifically praises structured cards in crypto/finance interfaces because they make dense information easier to scan. citeturn21search0

But a brand story, hero photograph, project case study, editorial narrative or immersive visual does **not** automatically become more usable when it is surrounded by a 24-pixel radius rectangle.

The correct rule is:

> **Use a card when the content behaves like a card.**

A card is justified when the item is:

- independently selectable,
- repeatable in a collection,
- comparable to sibling items,
- capable of having its own status/action,
- or intentionally isolated as a unit of information.

A card is weak when it is merely:

> “We did not know how to compose these elements, so we put each section inside a rounded box.”

Carbon’s component guidance reinforces the distinction: compact contained lists are appropriate for grouped items and inline actions in restricted spaces, whereas complex multi-column information should graduate to a real data table instead of accumulating more columns inside a pseudo-card. citeturn19search0turn19search3

For our sites this means:

**Valtum:** project worlds should usually be editorial compositions, not five equal rounded portfolio cards.

**KT:** an individual active delivery can be a card; a 200-delivery operational dataset should often be a list/table.

**Dubai:** an experience listing can be a card because it is a comparable purchasable unit.

**Math:** a course/lesson can be a card; the interactive mathematical canvas should not be trapped inside card styling without reason.

**Personality:** an insight may be a contained unit; an entire result narrative should not become a wall of tiles.

**Aegis:** incident summaries can be cards/list rows; audit history and large event collections need operational list/table structures.

### Image scrollers and horizontal experiences

The best carousel research strengthened a principle we already suspected: **the beautiful part of a scroller is not “items moving sideways.” It is the composition and orientation system around the movement.**

CargoKite explicitly relates horizontal progression to the journey of its ship. citeturn17search1 A highly viewed Dribbble horizontal-scroll study changes card scale, position and opacity as items progress and uses position indicators so users understand their place in the sequence. citeturn16search5 A separate RALLY carousel exploration describes a draggable/fluid desktop/tablet system while providing a distinct mobile version rather than forcing the identical interaction onto all screens. citeturn16search15 Another prominent Dribbble/Framer exploration uses a **carousel-to-list transition**, showing that the same content can legitimately change presentation mode rather than remaining forever trapped in a carousel. citeturn16search12

From those examples, I am locking the following scroller anatomy:

**A strong image scroller has:**

**A reason to move.** Collection, chronology, physical travel, progress or comparison.

**A dominant active state.** The user should know what is currently being inspected.

**Orientation.** Counter, progress, neighboring item, label, pagination or another signal.

**Context.** Do not display one mysterious image with no relationship to the surrounding content unless deliberate suspense is the goal.

**Direct control.** Drag/swipe/arrows/tap/keyboard depending medium.

**A coherent stop state.** The layout should still look composed when motion stops.

**A reverse path.** Moving backward must be as intentional as moving forward.

**A mobile transformation.** Native swipe, vertical stack, list or another suitable presentation may replace desktop choreography.

That gives us several distinct future patterns rather than one generic Valtum carousel:

| Pattern | Best use |
|---|---|
| **Editorial rail** | Valtum projects, visual portfolios |
| **State rail** | KT delivery progression, selected Aegis narratives |
| **Native media gallery** | Dubai experience detail |
| **Carousel → detail expansion** | Work/booking browsing |
| **Carousel → list transformation** | Collection exploration where the user later needs density |
| **Spatial journey** | Only when content genuinely represents movement/space |
| **Interactive demonstration sequence** | Math; frames may be states of a live concept rather than photographs |

This improves on our earlier image-scroller doctrine while remaining compatible with the live behavior we observed in Osmo and Jasmine. fileciteturn0file2

## Mobile product grammar

The mobile research changes one important misconception: **excellent mobile design is not simply an attractive smaller artboard. It is priority compression.**

Behance’s current hand-picked UI/UX collection includes dedicated finance apps, language-learning apps, logistics/mobile products, CRM and other app systems rather than treating mobile as a secondary deliverable. citeturn20search0turn20search4 Dribbble’s current UI/UX Select similarly includes mobile budgeting, crypto products and other task-oriented interfaces alongside web work. citeturn21search0

The most sophisticated cross-platform work treats different devices as **different contexts for the same underlying product**, not identical canvases at different widths. That aligns directly with Apple’s current principle of approaching every supported platform intentionally and preserving a person’s context as interfaces adapt. citeturn18search2

### What top mobile work repeatedly gets right

**The primary task gets more space.**

Desktop can display context, tools, side navigation, supporting charts and metadata simultaneously. Mobile often needs one dominant decision at a time. This is why simply turning a three-column dashboard into three tiny columns is fundamentally wrong.

**Navigation moves with reach and frequency.**

Apple’s current search guidance explicitly allows search as a tab, bottom toolbar, top toolbar or inline field depending on whether search is global, locally contextual or a frequent mobile action; it specifically notes that bottom placement can be beneficial when search is a priority because it is easier to reach. citeturn18search3

That is useful for Dubai Booking. Search is not a decorative control—it may be one of the product’s primary mobile actions.

**Lists become powerful.**

Apple recommends lists/tables for text-heavy hierarchy and browsing, while large image collections may be better represented as collections; complex multicolumn data can remain tabular where necessary. citeturn18search0

That means “cards everywhere” is even less appropriate on mobile. Often the best mobile interface is an excellent row.

**Onboarding gets shorter, not more cinematic.**
