Apple’s onboarding guidance recommends that products ideally explain themselves through use; when onboarding is necessary, it should be fast, focused and optional, with contextual teaching preferred over forcing users to memorize a feature tour. citeturn18search1

So Personality Assessor or Math Intellect should not begin with five gorgeous full-screen slides explaining controls that the product could teach naturally.

**Feedback must be immediate.**

Apple’s current design principles emphasize clear feedback, user agency and recovery from mistakes. citeturn18search2 Mobile particularly magnifies this: pressed, selected, saving, loading, success and error states need to be unmistakable because the user has less surrounding context.

### The mobile adaptation rule for expressive websites

For each desktop scene, I will now explicitly answer seven questions before Codex receives it:

> **What is the primary meaning?**  
> **What visual object carries that meaning?**  
> **What can disappear?**  
> **What must remain visible?**  
> **What becomes touch-controlled?**  
> **What changes crop or orientation?**  
> **What becomes normal document flow instead of scroll choreography?**

That is how a sophisticated desktop hero survives mobile without becoming an ordinary stacked template.

For example:

**Valtum desktop:** massive spatial typography and project artifact crossing type.

**Valtum mobile:** one intentional type/media intersection remains; secondary depth planes are reduced rather than shrunk.

**KT desktop:** route field + multiple operational states.

**KT mobile:** current delivery state and next required action dominate; full route context is accessed when needed.

**Dubai desktop:** full-width experience photograph with integrated discovery.

**Dubai mobile:** portrait art-directed experience photograph, large accessible search control, then native discovery/gallery.

**Math desktop:** equation, graph and controls may coexist.

**Math mobile:** the manipulable relationship gets the viewport; supporting explanation follows it.

**Personality desktop:** profile visualization can coexist with explanatory type.

**Personality mobile:** one dimension or interpretation owns the moment.

**Aegis desktop:** map, evidence, timeline and related signals can form a command composition.

**Aegis mobile:** incident → evidence → immediate action, with deeper context one tap away.

This is also consistent with the mobile/accessibility doctrine already established in the previous final research: mobile is **recomposition, not reduction**. fileciteturn0file0

## The UX pattern library a top-tier designer must command

Behance and Dribbble make visual excellence highly visible, but the difference between a great **shot** and a great **product** is often knowledge of patterns the screenshot does not advertise.

Apple’s design principles explicitly frame familiarity, agency, feedback, consistency, recovery, flexibility and simplicity as foundations of successful product design. citeturn18search2 Carbon’s design system then gives concrete operational forms to many of those principles across tables, filtering, search and list structures. citeturn19search0turn19search1turn19search3

This is the minimum pattern vocabulary I want active when we design the six sites:

| Pattern | What a top designer understands | Relevant Valtum use |
|---|---|---|
| **Progressive disclosure** | Show the decision-critical layer first; reveal supporting complexity only when useful. Carbon’s expandable tables explicitly use this to fit supplementary information without making every row permanently dense. citeturn19search3 | KT delivery detail, Aegis incidents, Personality results |
| **List → detail / master → detail** | Browsing and inspection are different tasks; keep collection context while exposing detail where screen size permits. Apple cites split-view list/table patterns for hierarchical navigation. citeturn18search0 | KT, Dubai, Aegis |
| **Search by scope** | Search can be global or local; users should understand what is being searched and be allowed to refine a broad scope. citeturn18search3 | Dubai, KT, Aegis |
| **Filtering** | Use single, multi-category, instant or batch filtering according to the decision and response cost; Carbon specifically recommends batch filtering when multiple selections or slow data return make instant refresh disruptive. citeturn19search1 | Dubai discovery, KT operations, Aegis event history |
| **Visible applied filters** | A closed filter control should still communicate that filters are active and offer a way to clear them. citeturn19search1 | Dubai/Aegis/KT |
| **Sortable operational table** | Dense comparable data deserves columns, sorting, selection and batch actions rather than decorative metric cards. citeturn19search3turn18search0 | KT and Aegis |
| **Contained list** | Small related groups with inline actions can remain compact; when columns proliferate, graduate to a data table. citeturn19search0 | Notifications, delivery events, assessment insights |
| **Contextual onboarding** | Teach through interaction and context rather than front-loading an instructional slideshow. citeturn18search1 | Math, Personality, Aegis |
| **Reversible action** | Give users confidence by making mistakes recoverable and allowing them to return to prior states. citeturn18search2 | Cart/booking, assessment answers, operational actions where safe |
| **Clear system feedback** | Every interaction needs perceptible feedback about what changed or what is happening. citeturn18search2 | All five products |
| **Search results states** | Search requires result context, useful categorization, no-results messaging and appropriate descriptions, not merely an input field. citeturn19search4turn19search2 | Dubai, KT, Aegis |
| **Selection + batch action** | When actions apply to multiple records, selection should visibly shift the interface into an action mode. citeturn19search3 | KT dispatch/admin, Aegis event management |
| **Skeleton / delayed-detail state** | Carbon recommends skeleton states for expanded information that requires additional loading rather than leaving the user guessing. citeturn19search3 | Data-heavy products |
| **Single-focus step flow** | Reduce simultaneous decisions when the task is sequential; progress and back behavior should remain clear. Apple’s onboarding guidance similarly favors focused learning rather than large memorization burdens. citeturn18search1 | Personality assessment, booking, learning |
| **Collection vs table** | Use image-rich collection treatments when visual recognition is primary; use rows/tables when textual comparison and operational scanning dominate. citeturn18search0 | Dubai vs KT/Aegis |
| **Multiple input parity** | Do not design an interaction that makes sense only for a desktop pointer. Apple’s principles explicitly recommend considering touch, keyboard and other input methods. citeturn18search2 | Every expressive interaction |

WCAG 2.2 adds another non-negotiable product layer: focus must not be obscured, dragging interactions need alternatives where dragging is not essential, and pointer targets have minimum-size requirements. citeturn18search4

So a Dribbble-perfect draggable carousel is not production-complete until it has keyboard/direct controls and an appropriate touch implementation.

### A dashboard pattern particularly important to us

The Behance/Dribbble dashboard work reinforces something I want to formalize:

> **A dashboard is not a gallery of analytics cards. It is an answer to “what requires my attention?”**

Dribbble’s enterprise examples repeatedly describe good dashboard design in terms of workflow clarity, scanability, controlled density and prioritization; its current trendsetter analysis specifically praises interfaces that make large data sets manageable rather than merely visually impressive. citeturn21search2

Therefore the first hierarchy question for KT and Aegis should be:

> **What should the operator notice in three seconds?**

Then:

> What changed?  
> What is abnormal?  
> What action is required?  
> What evidence supports it?  
> What is currently healthy and therefore does not need attention?

This is superior to beginning with:

> four KPI cards at the top because dashboards have four KPI cards.

### A search/filter pattern particularly important to Dubai

Apple recommends beginning from a broader search scope and enabling refinement through scopes/tokens where useful. citeturn18search3 Carbon distinguishes instant filters from batch filters based on the complexity of selection and response cost. citeturn19search1

For Dubai Booking this means we should not blindly duplicate desktop e-commerce filtering.

A mobile user might begin with:

**What do you want to do?**  
→ date  
→ party size  
→ category/area/price as needed.

Deeper filters can appear in a sheet and be applied together if there are several categories, while very lightweight filters can update immediately.

That is product UX, not visual decoration.

## How this changes each Valtum website

The Behance/Dribbble research does **not** replace the six identities we already established. It makes them more precise. fileciteturn0file0

### Valtum Studio

Valtum should absorb Behance’s **case-study intelligence**, not Behance’s static-poster format.

Behance’s strongest projects are highly authored presentations; Illoca, for instance, carries its architectural-paper concept from identity into web, while Behance’s curated UI/UX ecosystem regularly presents projects as coherent systems rather than lone screens. citeturn17search2turn20search0

For Valtum, that translates into:

**Hero:** one integrated header/hero composition. Large spatial typography and actual project artifacts occupy the same field.

**Selected work:** not five equal card tiles. Each project receives enough visual territory to establish its own world.

**Image rail:** an editorial project rail on desktop is still justified, but it needs active project identity, orientation and a strong mobile replacement rather than horizontal scroll for its own sake.

**Project opening:** the selected homepage artifact should visually or conceptually carry into the case study.

**Case study:** problem → context → constraints → role → concept → key decisions → interface/product system → implementation → evidence → reflection.

**Typography:** Valtum gets the most expressive spatial typography in the ecosystem because authorship itself is part of the product.

**Mobile:** retain one strong type/media relationship; simplify depth rather than reverting to “headline, paragraph, card, card, card.”

This extends the Jasmine case-study/editorial lessons from our live research while adding Behance’s stronger concept-to-presentation discipline. fileciteturn0file2

### KT Courier

Behance/Dribbble reinforce that operational software feels advanced when it is **legible**, not when it looks futuristic. Current agency curation praises logistics, warehouse and enterprise work for structured layouts, restrained interface treatment and workflow organization. citeturn21search2

KT should therefore use:

**Marketing hero:** one real delivery moving through a system.

**Visual motif:** route/progression may legitimately move horizontally because delivery has sequence and physical movement.

**Cards:** individual orders, exceptions or courier summaries can be modular units.

**Tables/lists:** operational collections should become real lists/tables once comparison across many records matters. citeturn19search3turn18search0

**Dashboard priority:** exceptions and interventions first; vanity metrics second.

**Delivery detail:** location + state + last event + next expected event + evidence + intervention.

**Mobile:** “What do I need to do right now?” dominates. A driver should not operate a shrunken desktop dispatch dashboard.

**Imagery:** real package/driver/location/route evidence—not generic logistics cinematography.

The design character becomes:

> **movement made accountable.**

### Dubai Booking Marketplace

The Behance/Dribbble research strengthens the idea that Dubai should be our most **image-led consumer site**, but the experience imagery must correspond to the thing the user can actually buy.

The full-background hero remains especially appropriate here because destination/activity imagery is itself product information. The visual lesson from current design curation is not “make travel dark and luxurious”; Dribbble’s agency analysis explicitly warns that premium travel experiences still need controlled, usable navigation rather than aesthetics consuming the interaction. citeturn21search2

The design should become:

**Hero:** full-viewport, human-relevant Dubai experience photograph.

**Header:** integrated directly into the image field.

**Discovery:** What / When / Guests or the product’s verified equivalent.

**Experience cards:** cards are justified because experiences are comparable purchasable objects.

**Image scroller:** native, direct gallery on detail pages; no excessive inertial effect between the user and a photo they are trying to inspect.

**Search:** prominent and context-aware; mobile placement can favor reach where appropriate. citeturn18search3

**Filters:** meaningful categories; instant for simple choices, batch when several attributes are being combined. citeturn19search1

**Detail:** visual desire first, then rapid movement into practical decision information.

**Checkout:** almost no ornamental motion.

The site’s premium feeling should come from:

> exceptional photography + crop + typography + spacing + confident transactional UX.

Not:

> black shadows + gold text + gradient button.

### Math Intellect

The greatest Behance/Dribbble lesson for Math is **not to imitate education-app presentation boards**.

The product’s most distinctive visual material is mathematics itself.

Therefore:

**Hero:** a manipulable mathematical relationship.

**Cards:** use them for lessons, curricula, saved work and progression where those are genuinely discrete units.

**Learning canvas:** do not turn it into a decorative rounded card if the mathematics needs spatial room.

**Motion:** always causal. Input changes → mathematical state changes.

**Onboarding:** teach the manipulation interactively rather than showing a long tutorial. Apple explicitly recommends teaching through interaction where practical. citeturn18search1

**Mobile:** give the mathematical object sufficient space and move secondary explanation into normal flow.

**Progress:** communicate actual learning progression rather than generic gamification decoration.

**Typography:** notation authority matters more than fashionable display type inside the learning surface.

The site should feel top-tier because:

> **the interface makes an abstract thing unusually clear.**

That is a stronger competitive position than “our education website has Awwwards animations.”

### Personality Assessor

The Behance/Dribbble agency work around healthcare and wellness repeatedly emphasizes reduced cognitive load, softer hierarchy and guided interaction when the emotional context calls for it. Dribbble’s Autism Care Centre selection specifically praises a calm UI, structured navigation and reduced cognitive load rather than maximal visual density. citeturn21search0

Personality should therefore become the most psychologically calm site in the ecosystem.

**Hero:** multidimensional profile, not person-in-a-box classification.

**Assessment:** one question owns the state.

**Progress:** persistent but quiet.

**Selection:** immediate and unmistakable.

**Back:** safe.

**Result hierarchy:** overview → dimensions → relationships → interpretation → methodology/limitations.

**Cards:** insights can be contained, but do not create a Pinterest grid of personality fragments.

**Color:** different dimensions can have controlled semantic distinction without creating a childish rainbow.

**Typography:** warm and human; enough personality for the brand but extremely comfortable at reading lengths.

**Motion:** small, responsive and reassuring rather than spectacular.

The Behance lesson here is particularly important:

> **Presentation can look sophisticated without making the underlying subject feel cold or computational.**

### Aegis Senital

Aegis benefits strongly from the security and enterprise UI patterns in Dribbble’s curation, but we need to filter out reference-specific surface treatments.

Cerebra, for example, uses a dark security-focused visual language, dashboard previews and precise typography; Dribbble’s own description attributes its strength to the clear modular narrative around complex infrastructure, not merely the dark surface. citeturn21search0 Ryven similarly receives praise because its dark mode maintains controlled contrast, spacing and operational hierarchy rather than becoming visually exhausting. citeturn21search2

So we take:

**clear evidence hierarchy**  
**structured operational density**  
**strong small typography**  
**meaningful status color**  
**modular incident views**  
**precise state changes**

and reject:

**cyberpunk neon**  
**random glowing maps**  
**monospace everywhere**  
**decorative detection rectangles**  
**gradient-based “AI security” aesthetic**.

Operationally:

**Primary state:** events requiring attention.

**List:** incident/event queue with status, location, time, severity/evidence and ownership.

**Detail:** evidence + location + related events + timeline + action.

**Table:** audit/history where multi-record comparison matters.

**Mobile:** current incident, evidence and next action—not a squeezed wall of dashboard cards.

**Marketing hero:** one event becoming intelligible.

Aegis’s premium quality therefore comes from:

> **calm situational authority.**

That is much stronger than “futuristic security.”

## Final design doctrine now added to the Valtum brain

The most valuable result from Behance and Dribbble is not a list of trendy visual motifs.

It is the ability to separate **presentation quality**, **art direction**, **interface quality**, and **production quality**.

A Behance project can be extraordinary at the first two while saying little about the latter two. A Dribbble shot can reveal brilliant component or motion thinking while not showing the full product. Dribbble itself increasingly frames its strongest work as the combination of visual execution, user-centered thinking, usability and business value rather than aesthetic impact alone. citeturn21search0turn21search3turn21search4

That creates a final four-layer evaluation model for every reference we use:

| Layer | Question |
|---|---|
| **Concept** | Why does this visual world belong to this product? |
| **Art direction** | How do type, imagery, color, scale and composition express that concept? |
| **UX system** | Does the user understand what to do, what changed and what matters? |
| **Production reality** | Does it survive responsive layouts, real content, states, touch, keyboard, loading and failure? |

The last layer is precisely why our earlier live-browser research remains essential alongside Behance/Dribbble. fileciteturn0file2

### Patterns I am explicitly locking

**The header remains part of the hero.** No detached floating pill unless a particular later state genuinely calls for it. fileciteturn0file0

**Hero design starts with the protagonist.** We do not begin with “left or right image?”

**Meaningful media beats fashionable media.** A sharp relevant photograph is more valuable than an expensive-looking irrelevant one.

**3D has to explain shape, space, behavior or identity.** CargoKite is a good example because the 3D vessel is the actual subject. citeturn17search1

**Typography has multiple jobs.** Spatial, editorial, reading, interface and numerical roles can require different behavior.

**We never guess a font and call it verified.** Exact type families will come from disclosure, live technical inspection or our own selected/licensed system.

**Color is contextual.** Healthcare softness, biotech precision, consumer photography and operational darkness can all be premium when they serve the product. citeturn17search0turn21search0turn22search0

**Our no-gradient rule remains.** A reference using gradients can still teach hierarchy, composition or motion; the gradient itself simply does not transfer.

**Cards have semantics.** We will not “cardify” six websites.

**Data-heavy information earns tables and lists.** Carbon and Apple both support row/table structures for scanability and complex comparable data. citeturn19search3turn18search0

**Horizontal movement requires a narrative reason.** CargoKite works because movement corresponds to the subject. citeturn17search1

**Image scrollers require orientation.** Active state, neighboring context, counter/progress or another navigational signal.

**Desktop scrollers do not automatically survive mobile.** Mobile can become swipe, vertical editorial sequence or list.

**Onboarding should be taught through use whenever possible.** citeturn18search1

**Search is architecture.** Its placement and scope should reflect what is being searched and how important discovery is. citeturn18search3

**Filtering is a decision system, not a row of chips.** Instant versus batch behavior should be selected according to task complexity and response cost. citeturn19search1

**Every important action has feedback and recovery.** citeturn18search2

**Mobile receives priority redesign.** It does not inherit desktop hierarchy mechanically.

**Accessibility is a design-state problem, not a final QA checkbox.** WCAG 2.2’s focus, drag and target requirements directly affect custom interaction concepts. citeturn18search4

**Motion remains semantic.** Reveal, carry, exchange, pin, rail, inspect, resolve and route continue to be our motion vocabulary. fileciteturn0file0

### Patterns I am explicitly rejecting

A future Valtum/Codex brief should fail review if it relies on any of the following without a product-specific reason:

> generic split hero  
> floating pill navbar by default  
> dark = premium  
> beige = design studio  
> purple/blue = AI  
> black/gold = Dubai luxury  
> neon = security  
> random 3D blob = innovation  
> laptop mockup = product proof  
> glass card = hierarchy  
> 24-pixel radius everywhere = contemporary  
> every section inside a card  
> four KPI cards because “dashboard”  
> horizontal scroll because Awwwards  
> carousel with no orientation  
> hover-only information  
> giant typography with no compositional job  
> device frames used as a substitute for mobile design  
> a desktop page shrunk to 390 pixels  
> static Behance boards treated as production UX  
> loaders with fake waiting  
> motion that only works forward  
> “cinematic” stock photography unrelated to the content  
> animation whose only justification is “premium.”

Those rejections follow from the difference between the product-driven systems repeatedly praised in current curated work and purely decorative treatment; Apple’s principles make the same distinction when they state that delight should support the product rather than interfere with its purpose. citeturn21search4turn18search2

### The final top-tier studio model

The design brain I will carry into Valtum and all five portfolio products is now:

> **Understand the product deeply enough to discover a visual idea that belongs only to it.**

> **Choose a protagonist that communicates that idea before choosing a conventional layout.**

> **Build composition around content rather than inserting content into components.**

> **Use typography to create geometry and hierarchy.**

> **Use imagery because of what it says, not because of how expensive it looks.**

> **Use cards only when the information is genuinely modular.**

> **Use tables and lists when the work is operational.**

> **Use motion to explain change, continuity, hierarchy, progress or space.**

> **Use horizontal movement only when the information itself has a horizontal logic.**

> **Treat mobile as another composition with a different attention budget.**

> **Design loading, empty, error, selected, disabled, success, filter, search, reverse, keyboard and reduced-motion states as part of the product.**

> **Let brand survive inside the product rather than stopping after the hero.**

> **Make the portfolio presentation itself authored, while never confusing presentation theater with actual application UX.**

> **When a reference uses something powerful, transfer its reasoning—not its appearance.**

That last rule remains our strongest anti-copy test:

> **If Behance, Dribbble, Awwwards and every reference site disappeared tomorrow, would this decision still make sense because of Valtum, KT Courier, Dubai Booking, Math Intellect, Personality Assessor or Aegis Senital itself?**

If the answer is **yes**, we have learned from top-tier designers.

If the answer is **no**, we have copied them.

The Behance/Dribbble research therefore does not change our goal into making six sites that resemble award galleries. It does something more useful: it gives us a considerably deeper vocabulary for **how elite studios convert product truth into art direction, visual systems, responsive product interfaces, interaction patterns and polished presentation**—while our live-browser and production research keeps that vocabulary grounded in experiences that actually have to work. citeturn20search0turn21search2turn21search3 fileciteturn0file2

---

# SOURCE REPORT — Creative Frontend Engineering, Mobile, Typography & Color Intelligence

**Original local source:** `deep-research-report (3).md`

# Creative Frontend Engineering, Mobile Systems, Typography, and Color Intelligence for the Valtum Ecosystem

## Research verdict: the missing layer was not more visual inspiration, but implementation intelligence

The previous research gave us a strong **designer brain**: concept development, art direction, hero composition, semantic imagery, typography as architecture, mobile recomposition, interaction patterns, cards, image scrollers, dashboards, and the distinction between presentation quality and production UX. fileciteturn0file0 The two pre-coding investigations had also already established the ecosystem rules: the header belongs to the hero, motion must communicate state, horizontal movement must have semantic justification, responsive images need art direction, reduced motion needs an alternate composition, and Codex should receive explicit instructions rather than vague directions such as “make it premium.” fileciteturn0file1 fileciteturn0file2

This pass closes a different gap:

> **How do studios actually engineer these experiences so that the visual idea survives the browser?**

The strongest conclusion is that there is **no secret “Awwwards technology stack.”** Top-tier sites are produced by combining ordinary semantic web technology with highly specialized systems only where the concept requires them. The quality comes from **architecture, asset preparation, synchronization, performance discipline, responsive state design, and custom tooling**, not from installing as many animation libraries as possible. This is visible across Lusion, Dogstudio, Unseen Studio, Basement, GSAP's own tooling, Three.js, and the modern browser platform. citeturn16search5turn3search4turn16search14turn3search11turn4search0

There is also an important evidence boundary around **Oryzo**. Lusion publicly identifies Oryzo as a self-initiated experience involving concept, web design, web development, 3D, WebGL, and animation. citeturn1view0turn6view0 But Lusion's public Oryzo repository currently exposes the coaster 3D files and explicitly says the code is “coming soon”; therefore I will **not fabricate an exact Oryzo stack** by claiming it uses React, Lenis, GSAP, a particular shader library, or another technology that Lusion has not disclosed for this project. citeturn2view0

That boundary is useful rather than limiting. Lusion's other published work gives us direct evidence of its engineering philosophy. In one experiment it states that Three.js was essentially the only dependency and that the world was generated procedurally without imported models or textures; its Worldcoin Globe work describes compressing geographic/dot information and reconstructing/rendering it efficiently on the GPU; and Synthetic Human describes Houdini-based preparation of high-quality assets for realtime web rendering. citeturn16search5turn16search10turn16search6

So the correct lesson from Oryzo is not:

> “Discover Oryzo's npm packages and copy them.”

It is:

> **The design concept dictates a custom visual system, and engineering is organized around making that system feasible in real time.**

That principle is much more valuable for Codex.

### What elite studios appear to build behind the page

The common architecture is roughly:

**semantic document/application layer**  
→ **layout and responsive system**  
→ **interaction/state layer**  
→ **motion orchestration**  
→ **optional WebGL/canvas renderer**  
→ **asset pipeline**  
→ **input/accessibility adaptations**  
→ **performance instrumentation**

Dogstudio's published work gives especially strong evidence for this layered approach. On Meebits it describes integration across React/Nuxt and a stack-agnostic WebGL experience rather than forcing commerce and visual rendering into one technical layer; another case describes an internal WebGL framework tightly connected with the Nuxt frontend. Its Zoox case also describes wireframes, motion and visual behavior being developed together while realtime WebGL helped handle a large animated visual system across devices. citeturn3search3turn3search4turn3search7

Unseen Studio makes this even more explicit through its publicly exposed FEB toolkit. Its system includes grid and accessibility utilities; UI components such as accordions, modals, cursors, marquees, carousels and image sequences; utilities such as `FocusManager`, `SmoothScroll` and global event handling; GSAP; page-transition infrastructure; and specialized WebGL modules. citeturn16search14

That is extremely important for our future workflow:

> **A top-tier studio does not write an unrelated animation script for every section. It accumulates a controlled internal language of scenes, transitions, media behaviors and infrastructure.**

That is precisely what Valtum needs.

---

## What the actual frontend architecture should look like

### Start with the DOM, not Canvas

Even on our most expressive sites, the document itself should remain a real document.

Headlines are headings.

Navigation is navigation.

Buttons are buttons.

Forms are forms.

Project descriptions are real text.

Booking controls remain semantic controls.

Operational tables remain structured data.

Canvas/WebGL is **an enhancement layer**, unless the subject itself truly cannot exist meaningfully without realtime graphics.

That distinction gives us resilience:

> HTML/CSS establishes meaning.  
> JavaScript establishes state.  
> GSAP or native animation establishes choreography.  
> WebGL establishes spatial/media behavior only where justified.

This also prevents the catastrophic creative-site failure mode where a renderer error leaves the visitor staring at a blank viewport.

### The internal architecture I want Codex to use

When the actual repositories are audited, framework naming can adapt, but the conceptual structure should resemble this:

```text
src/
  app/
    routes/
    layouts/
    metadata/

  components/
    navigation/
    buttons/
    forms/
    media/
    data/
    overlays/

  experience/
    scenes/
    transitions/
    motion/
    scroll/
    pointer/
    galleries/
    webgl/

  design-system/
    tokens/
    typography/
    color/
    spacing/
    radius/
    motion/
    breakpoints/

  lib/
    assets/
    performance/
    accessibility/
    analytics/
    feature-detection/

  content/
    projects/
    copy/
    metadata/
```

The important decision is not the folder names themselves. It is the separation of responsibilities.

A Valtum project hero should not contain:

```tsx
useEffect(() => {
  // 240 lines of GSAP
  // 80 lines of scroll math
  // WebGL initialization
  // menu state
  // image loading
  // resize logic
  // route transition
}, []);
```

That is how an exceptional concept becomes an unmaintainable demo.

Instead, Codex should receive the scene as a composition whose state and behavior are independently describable.

### Scenes should behave as state systems

For an authored sequence I want us to think:

```text
scene-enter
    ↓
state-a
    ↓
a-to-b
    ↓
state-b
    ↓
b-to-c
    ↓
state-c
    ↓
scene-exit
```

rather than:

```text
scroll happened
→ play entrance animation
→ play another animation
→ hide things
```

This distinction is why **reverse scrolling** becomes natural instead of being an afterthought.

GSAP ScrollTrigger provides precisely the kinds of primitives such a system requires: scrubbed progress, pinning, forward/back callbacks, snapping, responsive matching, velocity awareness, refresh behavior and fast-scroll handling. Its documentation also explicitly cautions against transforming the pinned element itself because that interferes with position calculations; the stable stage should generally be pinned while children change within it. citeturn4search0

Our Codex vocabulary therefore becomes:

```text
STAGE
    stable spatial container

STATE
    complete meaningful arrangement

TIMELINE
    reversible relationship between states

PROTAGONIST
    object that persists or changes meaning

SUPPORTING LAYERS
    text, media, labels, background

EXIT
    handoff into next normal/interactive state
```

That is significantly more exact than “add scroll animations.”

### React animation cleanup must be architectural

When React is used, animation instances cannot be allowed to leak across mounts, route changes or responsive reconstruction. GSAP's `context()` is specifically designed to collect animations and ScrollTriggers so they can be reverted together, and GSAP recommends its React integration for lifecycle cleanup. citeturn18search10

So our implementation doctrine becomes:

```tsx
useGSAP(
  () => {
    // Create scene timeline and triggers.
    // Everything is scoped to this scene.
  },
  {
    scope: sceneRef,
    dependencies: [/* meaningful scene inputs */],
    revertOnUpdate: true,
  }
);
```

Not hundreds of unscoped document selectors.

### Responsive scenes should be reconstructed, not patched

Desktop and mobile scenes may share content but should not necessarily share one animation timeline.

For example:

```text
Valtum selected work
desktop:
vertical scroll → pinned lateral editorial movement

mobile:
normal vertical document → large project chapters
```

I do **not** want:

```text
same 2400px horizontal transform
scaled down to phone width
```

This is where responsive JavaScript/state conditions and CSS container/media queries have different jobs. Container queries allow components to react to the space actually available to them rather than relying exclusively on the global viewport. citeturn18search6turn18search15

This will be particularly useful for reusable KT delivery units, booking results, Aegis incident modules, result visualizations and case-study blocks.

---

## Motion, WebGL, image sequences, and rendering engineering

### WebGL is one layer, not the website

The strongest agency engineering repeatedly separates **visual rendering** from the application shell.

That means a scene can conceptually look like:

```text
DOM
 ├── nav
 ├── headline
 ├── accessible description
 ├── CTA
 └── progress/status

WebGL canvas
 ├── protagonist
 ├── lighting
 ├── particles/environment
 └── transition material

state controller
 └── synchronizes both
```

The visual result can feel fully integrated even though the DOM and GPU layers remain technically separate.

That is exactly the kind of architecture Dogstudio's stack-agnostic WebGL integrations and Unseen's toolkit suggest. citeturn3search3turn16search14

### We should use a three-level rendering ladder

I am adding this directly to the Valtum engineering brain.

#### DOM/CSS

Default.

Use for:

text  
cards  
tables  
images  
masks  
sticky regions  
native galleries  
most hover effects  
basic transforms  
UI state.

#### DOM + orchestrated animation

Use GSAP/native Web Animations/View Transitions when relationships across several elements require sequencing.

Examples:

Valtum project carry.

KT route progression.

Personality result reveal.

Aegis evidence transformation.

#### Realtime rendering

Use Three.js/WebGL/WebGPU only when one of these is true:

- shape needs to rotate/manipulate in space;
- a large field of particles/data needs GPU rendering;
- a shader is part of the visual concept;
- camera/parallax/perspective materially explains the subject;
- interaction needs a realtime visual simulation;
- DOM equivalents would be substantially worse.

That keeps 3D **special**.

### Top-tier 3D engineering begins before the browser

This is one of the biggest technical lessons from the agency research.

Unseen's BlueYard case describes creating particle animation offline in Houdini, exporting it into compressed image data, then decoding/mapping that data into the realtime WebGL system. citeturn16search16 Lusion's Synthetic Human similarly emphasizes optimizing 3D assets through a Houdini workflow for realtime web use. citeturn16search6

That means:

> **Do not ask the visitor's phone to calculate something that the studio could calculate once during production.**

This is a major senior-frontend distinction.

For a complicated scene we should ask:

Can simulation be baked?

Can animation data be compressed?

Can texture information encode state?

Can we reduce geometry?

Can repeated objects be instanced?

Can the “3D” moment actually be rendered video?

Can only the interactive part remain realtime?

Unseen's Nikolas Type project is particularly instructive because it combines realtime WebGL with pre-rendered 3D media rather than insisting everything be realtime. citeturn16search15

That hybrid philosophy is excellent for Valtum.

### Use the correct 3D transmission pipeline

Three.js supports glTF with compressed geometry/texture workflows; `GLTFLoader` can integrate Draco, KTX2 and Meshopt decoders. citeturn18search5

Draco can substantially reduce mesh-transfer size, but decompression introduces client-side work, so it is a **tradeoff**, not a checkbox. citeturn18search0 KTX2/Basis provides GPU-compressed textures that can be transcoded into formats appropriate for the device's GPU. citeturn18search1 And for many repeated copies of the same geometry/material, Three.js `InstancedMesh` reduces draw calls through instanced rendering. citeturn18search2

Therefore, for future Codex briefs:

> **Do not ship raw production Blender/Houdini geometry directly because it “works.”**

We will explicitly determine:

```text
source asset
→ optimize geometry
→ clean materials
→ resize textures
→ compress textures
→ choose glTF
→ optional Draco/Meshopt
→ inspect GPU memory
→ inspect decode time
→ mobile fallback
```

Incidentally, Oryzo's public repository currently contains OBJ coaster assets, but because the runtime source is unpublished, we cannot infer that the production site necessarily loads those exact raw files at runtime. citeturn2view0

### Particle systems should not mean thousands of React objects

The entire value of GPU rendering disappears when every particle becomes a high-level UI component.

Large repeated systems should be expressed through buffers, instances, textures or shader data.

Lusion's Worldcoin work is a particularly strong example of thinking about **data representation** as part of visual engineering: geographic/dot information was compressed into a compact representation and decoded for rendering rather than transported as a naive hierarchy of individual DOM-like objects. citeturn16search10

This changes how I will specify Aegis maps, Math diagrams or any Valtum visual field.

The instruction will not be:

> “Add 10,000 floating particles.”

It will be:

> “This field represents X. Use a single GPU-friendly repeated representation; no per-particle React component/state. Cap visual complexity according to capability and provide a static/fewer-points state below the defined tier.”

### Smooth scrolling is not a prerequisite for premium

Lenis is intentionally lightweight, works around native scrolling, and can synchronize scrolling with animation systems such as GSAP, but its own documentation contains real caveats around nested scrolling, anchors, snap behavior and certain touch/iOS configurations. citeturn5search0turn5search1

So our rule is now very precise:

| Surface | Scrolling choice |
|---|---|
| Valtum editorial marketing | restrained smooth scroll may be appropriate |
| Valtum long case study | mostly native semantics; smoothing only where beneficial |
| KT marketing story | potentially light smoothing |
| KT operations | **native** |
| Dubai editorial home | optional light desktop smoothing |
| Dubai search/detail/checkout | **native** |
| Math marketing | optional |
| Math learning workspace | **native/direct** |
| Personality marketing | light/optional |
| Personality assessment | **native/direct** |
| Aegis marketing | optional |
| Aegis command interface | **native/direct** |

There is no reason to make a courier dispatcher, assessment taker or security operator feel that their scroll input is attached to a rubber band.

### Horizontal experiences get three implementation strategies

#### Scroll-driven editorial rail

Desktop only where vertical progress is intentionally mapped into lateral narrative movement.

Use ScrollTrigger.

Stage stays fixed.

Track moves.

Progress/navigation updates from the same timeline.

Reverse uses the same timeline.

#### Native touch rail

Use CSS overflow and scroll snapping for mobile image/product galleries. `scroll-snap-type` is broadly supported and allows a browser-native snapping container rather than requiring a custom inertial physics implementation. citeturn23search14

#### Normal vertical recomposition

Where the horizontal idea was purely compositional rather than functionally valuable on mobile, eliminate it.

This is not a downgrade.

It is good responsive art direction.

### Shared-element transitions have become considerably more viable

The browser View Transition API now supports transitions between DOM states, including SPA and MPA scenarios, with recent browser support improving materially; MDN classifies the core `ViewTransition` interface as Baseline 2025, while newer transition-type functionality reached Baseline 2026. citeturn23search4turn23search3turn23search5

That means our future shared-project transitions can use a hierarchy like:

```text
native View Transition
when sufficient and supported

        ↓

GSAP Flip
when we need more precise layout-state choreography

        ↓

simple opacity/state transition
for reduced motion or unsupported environments
```

GSAP Flip's core model is particularly useful: capture an element's current state, change layout/DOM state, then animate from the previous state into the new one. citeturn13search0

That is exactly the mental model for:

> Valtum homepage project image → case-study opening image.

### Page transition does not mean blocking navigation

The page should change promptly.

The animation explains continuity.

It does not hold the visitor hostage.

That will be a hard Codex acceptance rule.

---

## Mobile needs to feel designed for the phone, not merely responsive

The user requirement here is especially important:

> On mobile, these sites should feel as though a design team intentionally designed a mobile product, not as though CSS eventually reached a breakpoint.

Apple's current design principles explicitly emphasize approaching each platform intentionally, preserving people's context as interfaces adapt, and supporting different input methods. citeturn24search6 Apple's current accessibility guidance likewise recommends controls large enough to interact with comfortably, simple gestures for frequent actions, alternatives to gestures, support for larger text, and adaptations for reduced motion. citeturn24search0turn24search1

Current Apple Design Award selections reinforce the same principle from actual products. Primary is praised for a minimal interface that gets out of the way of its news content; Katha Room derives its UI from the visual language of its cultural content; Tide Guide uses full-screen information graphics and a visual system related directly to weather/ocean context. citeturn24search2turn24search3 Earlier winners such as Feather and iA Writer are praised for interfaces shaped around their actual platform interactions rather than generalized desktop conventions. citeturn24search5

The design principle is:

> **Mobile is not a width. Mobile is another attention, input, posture and context model.**

### The phone gets a different hierarchy

Desktop can comfortably show:

```text
primary object
+ context
+ navigation
+ metadata
+ secondary action
+ comparison
```

Mobile often needs:

```text
primary object
→ primary decision
→ immediate feedback
→ deeper context
```

This means mobile design should be performed **scene by scene**, not page by page.

For every desktop scene, I will ask Codex to implement a mobile specification answering:

**What owns the viewport?**

**What information moves below the fold?**

**What disappears?**

**What is converted from hover to tap?**

**What turns from pinning into document flow?**

**Which image source changes?**

**What action becomes thumb-priority?**

**What metadata becomes collapsible?**

**Does the user still understand the same concept?**

### Mobile hero engineering

We should prefer `svh`/`dvh` variants deliberately rather than blindly using old `100vh`. Modern CSS provides small, large and dynamic viewport units because mobile browser UI changes the actually visible viewport as browser chrome expands or collapses. citeturn23search15

So a hero specification might say:

```css
.hero {
  min-height: 100svh;
}

@supports (height: 100dvh) {
  .hero--interactive {
    min-height: 100dvh;
  }
}
```

The choice depends on whether we need stability or continuous accommodation of the browser UI.

### Hover must never be fundamental on mobile

CSS can explicitly distinguish whether the primary input mechanism can conveniently hover. citeturn23search12

Therefore our pattern should be:

```css
/* Essential state exists without hover. */

@media (hover: hover) and (pointer: fine) {
  /* Add rich hover behavior only here. */
}
```

That changes how we implement our reference-inspired effects.

For example:

**Valtum desktop:** hover project image → media shifts/crop reacts/details appear.

**Mobile:** project title and essential information are already visible; tap opens.

Not:

> long-press and hope the browser simulates hover.

### Touch target standard

Apple's current guidance uses 44×44 pt as its default iOS/iPadOS control size and recommends adequate spacing around interactive elements. citeturn24search0

Our web implementation should therefore generally target approximately:

> **44–48 CSS px for important touch controls**, where density permits.

That will apply to:

menu trigger  
carousel navigation  
filter controls  
calendar navigation  
assessment answers  
Aegis incident actions  
Math manipulation handles.

### Responsive media needs different files, not only CSS

A desktop image can be compositionally perfect and mobile-useless.

The `<picture>`/responsive-image system exists specifically to allow art-directed alternatives, and current image guidance recommends device-appropriate sources rather than forcing a small device to download or crop unnecessarily large desktop imagery. citeturn15search0turn15search2

So Codex will eventually receive:

```text
dubai-hero-desktop.avif
focal point: subject x=72%, y=48%

dubai-hero-mobile.avif
focal point: subject x=51%, y=45%
```

rather than:

```text
hero.jpg
object-fit: cover
```

### Mobile design direction across the six sites

| Product | Desktop composition | Purpose-built mobile composition |
|---|---|---|
| **Valtum** | Spatial type, multiple media planes, authored project movement | Preserve one strong media/type intersection; project work becomes large vertical chapters; remove unnecessary depth |
| **KT Courier** | Route context + deliveries + operations | Current delivery/exception/action dominates; map becomes contextual, not permanent |
| **Dubai Booking** | Full photographic field + rich discovery | Portrait experience art direction, large search/discovery control, natural touch gallery, recoverable booking CTA |
| **Math Intellect** | Equation, graph, control and explanation can coexist | Manipulable concept owns viewport; controls sit within thumb reach; explanation follows |
| **Personality** | Profile + narrative interpretation coexist | One question/one dimension at a time; calm progress; result unfolds sequentially |
| **Aegis** | Incident queue + map + evidence + timeline | Incident → evidence → action first; map/context opens when needed |

That extends the “mobile is priority compression” principle from our Behance/Dribbble research into actual implementation architecture. fileciteturn0file0

### Mobile should sometimes be better than desktop

There are interactions where touch is actually the more direct medium.

Dubai gallery:

> finger directly moves imagery.

Math:

> finger directly moves a point, slider or geometric element.

Personality:

> large answer regions can be faster than desktop radio-card interfaces.

KT driver workflows:

> camera/proof/status actions can be organized around the task happening physically.

The goal is not desktop parity.

It is **contextual excellence**.

---

## Typography engineering: what elite product typography is actually doing

The font research changes our approach from:

> “find a beautiful font”

to:

> **build a typographic operating system.**

### Apple demonstrates role-aware typography

Apple's current SF family is not one static sans used identically everywhere. SF Pro includes nine weights, variable optical sizes and four widths; SF Compact is optimized for smaller sizes and narrower columns; SF Mono exists for aligned technical/code contexts; New York acts as a serif companion and also includes optical-size behavior. citeturn21search3

That is the real lesson from Apple typography:

> **One brand can alter glyph behavior according to role, scale and environment without losing identity.**

Not:

> “Use San Francisco because Apple looks premium.”

### Google's typography evolution is even more instructive

Google's own design documentation explains that the original geometric Google Sans was effective for branding/display but was not sufficiently legible for smaller interface text. Google Sans Text was therefore made taller, somewhat more condensed and less circular, with greater spacing and altered numeral behavior for small sizes. Google Sans Flex subsequently expanded the system with weight, width, optical size, slant, grade and roundedness axes. citeturn21search6

That is an enormously important design lesson for Valtum:

> **The font behavior that creates an exceptional 100 px hero can be the wrong behavior at 13 px in a dashboard.**

We should never force brand display typography into operational text merely to maintain “consistency.”

### Variable fonts are especially valuable to our ecosystem

The W3C's current CSS Fonts specification supports variable axes through normal font properties and specifically recommends using semantic CSS properties such as weight, width/style and optical sizing where they map to standard axes, rather than relying on raw low-level variation settings for everything. Optical sizing exists so a variable font can adjust glyph construction according to display size. citeturn19search0

GitHub's Mona Sans illustrates this particularly well: its public variable family exposes weight, width, optical size and italic behavior, with smaller optical sizes adjusted for text and larger ones suited to display. citeturn21search0

This makes it a useful **technical reference**, even when we do not select Mona Sans visually.

### Fonts I now regard as useful reference systems

| Typeface/system | What it teaches us | Likely role for us |
|---|---|---|
| **SF Pro / SF Compact** | Optical sizing, width, scale-specific utility | Design principle, not Valtum web identity citeturn21search3 |
| **Google Sans Flex** | Brand expression and legibility can coexist through variable axes | Reference for variable type architecture citeturn21search6 |
| **Mona Sans** | Wide variable design space, strong web/product versatility | Strong open-source utility/data candidate citeturn21search0 |
| **Hubot Sans** | More geometric/technical sibling designed for header/pull-quote expression | Potential display companion in technical projects citeturn21search4 |
| **Geist Sans / Mono** | Highly functional digital/dev typography and easy web implementation | Useful utility reference; too familiar to automatically become Valtum's flagship identity citeturn21search2 |
| **PP Fragment** | One coordinated family spans Sans, Glare, Serif and Text personalities | Excellent reference for authored editorial systems; commercial license required citeturn22search0turn22search2 |
| **PP Monument** | Multiple widths and weights create strong spatial display control | Strong Valtum display candidate to prototype, license permitting citeturn22search7 |
| **PP Museum** | Variable humanist sans character | Interesting Personality/Dubai reading candidate to prototype citeturn22search6 |
| **STIX Two Math** | Purpose-built OpenType mathematics alongside a corresponding text family | High-confidence math typography candidate citeturn20search0turn20search2 |

This is **not a final font lock**. Actual selection should be done in real Valtum strings, screenshots and interface states, after licensing is confirmed.

### Oryzo's exact font remains intentionally unclaimed

The public Oryzo/Lusion material proves its design/development/WebGL authorship but does not provide a verified font declaration or released application source. citeturn1view0turn2view0

So I will not perform the common bad research behavior:

> screenshot looks like Neue Montreal → claim Oryzo uses Neue Montreal.

Until technically verified from a disclosed stylesheet or official source, it remains unknown.

### My current font direction for each site

#### Valtum Studio

Needs **two personalities that feel related**:

**Spatial/display:** variable grotesk with dramatic width control.

**Utility/editorial text:** calmer face with excellent reading rhythm.

A promising research direction is something in the **PP Monument / PP Museum / PP Fragment** level of authorship rather than a default startup font. PP Monument's expanded width/weight system makes it particularly relevant to the spatial typography we want. citeturn22search7

I would not use Geist as the main Valtum identity simply because it is technically excellent; its widespread association with modern developer/product interfaces would make it harder for Valtum to develop a distinct studio voice. Geist remains useful as a reference for functional UI typography. citeturn21search2

#### KT Courier

A variable workhorse is ideal.

Requirements:

tabular figures  
clear `1/I/l` differentiation  
strong numerals  
condensed capability  
excellent 12–16 px rendering  
heavier states without closing counters.

Mona Sans is a credible candidate because it provides width/weight/optical-size control and stylistic alternatives. citeturn21search0

For timestamps, order IDs and tightly aligned operational metadata, a mono face can appear **selectively**.

Not the entire UI.

#### Dubai Booking

Needs more editorial warmth than KT/Aegis, while transactional UI must remain neutral and extremely readable.

A family such as PP Fragment is conceptually interesting because the same family can move between Sans/Text and more expressive Serif/Glare expressions without becoming typographically unrelated. citeturn22search0turn22search2turn22search3

Possible structure:

> expressive destination/editorial headings  
> restrained booking UI  
> tabular pricing/date numerals.

#### Math Intellect

Math notation deserves its own actual mathematical type system.

STIX Two Math exists specifically for scientific/technical mathematical typesetting, includes an OpenType MATH table and a large mathematical character repertoire, while STIX Two Text provides corresponding text faces. citeturn20search0turn20search2

KaTeX similarly ships with a dedicated web-font system for its rendered mathematical notation. citeturn19search1

So:

> **brand UI font ≠ equation font by necessity.**

That distinction will materially improve Math Intellect.

#### Personality Assessor

Needs a warmer, less mechanical typographic voice.

The key qualities are:

open forms  
comfortable reading  
moderate contrast  
gentle but not childish personality  
enough differentiation for data/result labels.

A humanist variable family such as PP Museum belongs on the prototype shortlist. citeturn22search6

#### Aegis Senital

Needs dense precision.

Aegis should use:

**compact UI sans** for command surfaces.

**tabular numerals** for times/values.

**monospace only where semantic** — device IDs, technical identifiers, perhaps event hashes.

No “everything monospace because cyber-security.”

### The coding of typography matters almost as much as the family

A final Codex specification should ultimately contain something closer to:

```css
:root {
  --font-display: "Valtum Display", sans-serif;
  --font-text: "Valtum Text", sans-serif;

  --type-hero-size: clamp(4.6rem, 9vw, 10rem);
  --type-hero-leading: 0.88;
  --type-hero-tracking: -0.045em;

  --type-body-size: clamp(1rem, 1.15vw, 1.125rem);
  --type-body-leading: 1.52;
}
```

With a variable font:

```css
.hero-title {
  font-family: var(--font-display);
  font-weight: 620;
  font-stretch: 112%;
  font-optical-sizing: auto;
}
```

And operational values:

```css
.metric,
.timestamp,
.price {
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
}
```

The W3C Fonts specification explicitly recommends using higher-level font properties for standard variable axes and supports optical sizing and advanced numeral/features systems. citeturn19search0

### Font loading will be part of performance engineering

The selected font must be:

licensed correctly  
served as appropriate WOFF2/variable assets  
subset if genuinely justified  
preloaded only when initial rendering requires it  
paired with a metrically sensible fallback  
protected against catastrophic layout shifts.

Current Next.js font tooling, for example, self-hosts fonts and is specifically designed to avoid external requests while helping prevent layout shifts; its documentation recommends variable fonts where appropriate. citeturn14search0

We will therefore not finish a beautiful typography system and then import six families from third-party CDNs indiscriminately.

---

## Color-system engineering and the specially designed green direction

The most important color conclusion is:

> **We should design colors as a system of perceptual and semantic relationships, not select six attractive hex codes from a moodboard.**

Modern CSS Color supports OKLab/OKLCH, which were designed around greater perceptual uniformity than traditional RGB/HSL-style manipulation and can also participate in modern wide-gamut color workflows. citeturn11search1 Systems such as Radix Colors likewise organize colors into role-oriented scales spanning backgrounds, interactive components, borders, solid fills and text rather than treating one brand hex as the whole palette. citeturn11search0turn11search3

That is the approach I want Valtum to use.

### The proposed Valtum Green

I generated a candidate green in OKLCH rather than choosing a stock framework green.

The current strongest **research candidate** is:

> **Valtum Green — `#83D670`**  
> approximately `oklch(80% 0.16 140)`

This is intentionally:

bright without being neon;

botanical without looking like an eco startup;

high enough in luminance to work as a large energetic field;

distinct from generic emerald-fintech greens;

and flat, because we do **not** need a gradient to make it interesting.

Against a near-black green-tinted ink (`#071008`), this candidate computes to approximately **10.9:1 contrast**, making dark foreground content highly legible when the green is used as a solid background. That numerical value is a direct calculation from the proposed sRGB values; final UI use would still be verified in context.

### Candidate Valtum green scale

This is a **designed research scale**, not a final locked brand palette:

| Role | Candidate | Intended use |
|---|---:|---|
| Green wash | `#F1FCF1` | very light section tint |
| Light surface | `#E1F5E0` | selected subtle surface |
| Pale emphasis | `#C7EDC7` | diagrams/secondary indicators |
| Soft green | `#AAE2AB` | supporting illustration/state |
| Mid green | `#85D088` | secondary accent |
| Functional green | `#61B966` | controls/status where appropriate |
| Strong green | `#409D48` | dark-on-light secondary |
| Deep green | `#298033` | pressed/strong state |
| **Valtum signature** | **`#83D670`** | primary authored accent |
| Signature hover | `#70C35D` | active/pressed variation |
| Forest text | `#174C1D` | high-contrast dark green text |
| Deep forest | `#09260C` | dark field |
| Valtum ink | `#071008` | primary near-black |

The important thing is the **role structure**.

A developer does not receive:

```css
green: #83d670;
```

and then make every green object the same color.

They receive:

```css
--color-accent:
--color-accent-hover:
--color-accent-pressed:
--color-accent-subtle:
--color-accent-text:
--color-focus:
```

Radix's role-oriented color scales provide a useful precedent for this sort of systematic mapping. citeturn11search0turn11search6

### Green is not automatically the color of all six websites

This is critical.

The Valtum ecosystem should have shared **color intelligence**, not six green skins.

#### Valtum Studio

Research direction:

```text
Valtum Ink       #071008
Clean field      near-white neutral
Valtum Green     #83D670
Forest           #174C1D
Project colors   enter through actual portfolio media
```

The green can become recognizable because it is used deliberately, not because the page is green everywhere.

Possible moments:

logo accent  
selection indicator  
active project index  
interactive focus  
one hero artifact  
CTA background  
case-study metadata.

#### KT Courier

The green should feel operational.

A possible structure:

```text
carbon neutral
route green
warning amber
critical red
cool informational secondary
```

Green means:

movement healthy  
delivery completed  
route verified  
system active.

It should not mean every clickable thing.

#### Dubai Booking Marketplace

The hero photographs should provide most of the chromatic richness.

A restrained interface system can combine:

warm clean field  
deep date-palm/mineral green  
sunlit terracotta or desert accent  
cool water/sky support  
strong dark text.

No black-and-gold “Dubai luxury” stereotype.

No overlay gradient needed simply to make a photograph feel expensive.

#### Math Intellect

Color can carry **mathematical semantics**.

For example:

```text
green    selected/proven/current object
blue     variable/relationship A
coral    relationship B / conflict / correction
neutral  construction/reference
```

But the same mathematical variable must preserve its identity across graph, equation and explanation.

Color therefore becomes an explanatory system.

#### Personality Assessor

A multidimensional system benefits from several restrained accents rather than one corporate green.

Possible family:

muted leaf  
plum  
warm apricot  
controlled blue  
neutral ink.

But colors should label dimensions alongside text/icon/shape so the result does not become inaccessible or psychologically simplistic. Apple's accessibility guidance explicitly recommends not relying on color alone to distinguish state or meaning. citeturn24search0

#### Aegis Senital

Green becomes **semantic evidence**, not atmosphere.

```text
verify / healthy → green
warning          → amber
critical         → red/coral
informational    → neutral/cool
inactive         → gray
```

The background can legitimately be dark where video evidence requires it.

But if every border, map marker and text label glows green, the actual verified/safe state loses meaning.

### Color coding should use modern perceptual methods

For production, I want the master palette stored conceptually in OKLCH and exported with sRGB-safe values where required.

For example:

```css
:root {
  --green-brand: oklch(80% 0.16 140);
  --green-brand-srgb: #83d670;
}
```

This lets designers make lighter/darker variations by changing luminance and chroma deliberately rather than performing naive HSL adjustments that may create perceptually uneven steps. The underlying rationale comes from OKLab/OKLCH's more perceptually uniform construction in CSS Color. citeturn11search1

### No gradients remains a strength, not a handicap

Nothing in this research suggests we need to reverse that rule.

Depth can come from:

composition  
image content  
solid color field changes  
type contrast  
scale  
occlusion  
texture  
light inside real media  
motion  
spacing  
material rendering.

A strong solid green beside black and a sharp image can be much more recognizable than another blue-purple gradient.

---

## The final engineering brain I will give Codex

This research changes the level of specificity at which I can direct implementation.

I now want every important scene to be specified across **five simultaneous layers**.

### Visual layer

What does it look like at rest?

Example:

> Hero occupies `100svh`. Header has no separate surface. Wordmark is aligned to the same 32 px desktop inset as the hero type. Display title begins 19% below the top edge and crosses behind the supplied product frame. The frame's visual center is approximately 64% across the viewport.

### Content layer

What exactly does it say?

Example:

```text
Headline:
Digital products, designed to work in the real world.

Primary CTA:
View selected work

Secondary navigation:
Work
Studio
Contact
```

Not:

> “Add an interesting headline.”

### Behavioral layer

What changes?

Example:

```text
0.00–0.18
hero fully stable

0.18–0.42
supporting copy leaves upward
protagonist remains

0.34–0.62
project artifact grows and shifts toward project-stage position

0.48–0.72
next state typography enters while old artifact remains visible

0.72–1.00
handoff completes
```

And the same progress reverses identically.

### Responsive/input layer

What changes by context?

Example:

```text
desktop pointer:
hover crop/pointer response enabled

tablet:
no custom cursor
reduced depth

mobile:
hero becomes 100svh
secondary artifact removed
one type/media crossing retained
scroll choreography converted to normal vertical sequence

reduced motion:
no camera/depth travel
destination composition appears directly with brief opacity transition
```

CSS hover enhancements should be capability-gated rather than assumed on all devices. citeturn23search12

### Engineering layer

What implementation system owns it?

Example:

```text
semantic hero layout:
React/HTML/CSS

scene progress:
GSAP ScrollTrigger

smooth scroll:
none on mobile
optional Lenis on large marketing viewports

shared transition:
View Transition API when available
GSAP Flip where additional control is required

3D:
not justified for this scene

hero image:
eager
responsive sources
desktop/mobile art direction
```

This is the difference between a creative direction and a production instruction.

### The component system should not erase project identity

We **will** share engineering primitives:

```text
Scene
Media
Picture
Video
ProjectLink
Button
SplitText-like typography utility
EditorialRail
TouchGallery
Modal
FocusTrap
TransitionLink
OperationalList
DataTable
Status
ChartShell
```

But we should **not** share an all-purpose visual template called:

```text
PremiumHero
PremiumCard
PremiumSection
```

That would recreate exactly the generic design system we have spent all this research trying to avoid.

### The internal motion tokens should be reusable

A possible structure:

```ts
export const motion = {
  micro: {
    duration: 0.16,
  },

  ui: {
    duration: 0.32,
  },

  structural: {
    duration: 0.64,
  },

  route: {
    duration: 0.78,
  },
};
```

But complex narrative timing should be state/progress-driven rather than forcing every transition into one duration token.

### The renderer should be capability-aware

For a heavy 3D scene I want a system closer to:

```text
Tier A
semantic DOM + static media

Tier B
DOM + orchestrated motion

Tier C
DOM + full realtime renderer
```

The user always receives the story.

Only the rendering richness changes.

That is progressive enhancement applied to creative direction.

### Loading becomes honest

Three.js exposes `LoadingManager` precisely so groups of asset loads can be observed and coordinated. citeturn18search13

If we ever build a branded loader, its progress should correspond to actual critical readiness where possible.

We should not do:

```js
setInterval(() => {
  fakeProgress += Math.random() * 8;
}, 100);
```

just because a reference site had a counter.

The earlier Outfit study already taught us to transfer **brand continuity through the loader**, not copy its numeric behavior. fileciteturn0file1

### Images get production specifications

For every important image, Codex will eventually get:

```text
asset
purpose
desktop crop
tablet crop
mobile source
focal point
aspect ratio
object-position
alt text
loading strategy
quality
responsive widths
transition participation
fallback
```

Next.js's current image tooling, for example, supports responsive `sizes`, explicit dimensions to prevent layout shift, priority/preloading choices and art-directed `<picture>`-style approaches for different mobile/desktop sources. citeturn14search1turn14search3 Web performance guidance similarly recommends serving responsive image sources and prioritizing genuinely critical first-viewport media rather than indiscriminately preloading everything. citeturn15search0turn15search2

### Fonts get production specifications

Likewise:

```text
family
license/source
file
format
weight range
width range
optical sizing
features
fallback
preload status
hero/body/interface role
tabular numeral behavior
mobile size
line-height
line breaks
```

Not:

> “Use a modern sans.”

### Mobile gets its own acceptance test

A page fails if:

> desktop looks exceptional and mobile merely works.

The target becomes:

> **Someone seeing only the 390 px version should believe that was one of the primary design canvases.**

That means:

media art direction  
thumb ergonomics  
native touch movement  
mobile information hierarchy  
portrait composition  
font rescaling/re-breaking  
correct viewport units  
direct controls  
no hover dependency  
no desktop motion squeezed into a phone.

Apple's explicit recommendation to approach each platform intentionally and preserve context across adaptations strongly reinforces this standard. citeturn24search6

### The six products now have both a design brain and a frontend brain

| Product | Design identity | Engineering identity |
|---|---|---|
| **Valtum** | Authored editorial studio | semantic DOM + sophisticated route/media transitions + selective scroll choreography |
| **KT Courier** | Movement made accountable | state-driven operational UI; maps/lists/tables; minimal ornamental runtime |
| **Dubai Booking** | Experience → confidence → transaction | responsive photography + native galleries + resilient search/filter/booking UX |
| **Math Intellect** | Understanding made manipulable | realtime mathematical state/data visualization; direct touch input; specialized math typography |
| **Personality** | Humane multidimensional interpretation | calm step-state architecture; accessible forms; progressive result disclosure |
| **Aegis** | Evidence → context → action | data-heavy operational hierarchy; selective realtime/map/video systems; native responsiveness |

### The final rule I am adding to the Valtum doctrine

Before approving any ambitious visual implementation, I will now ask:

> **Does this need to exist in DOM, CSS, video, Canvas, WebGL, or a hybrid—and why?**

Then:

> **What is the cheapest medium that preserves the idea completely?**

Then:

> **What should be precomputed rather than calculated on the visitor's device?**

Then:

> **What is the mobile composition rather than the mobile fallback?**

Then:

> **Which input methods can operate it?**

Then:

> **What is its reduced-motion state?**

Then:

> **What survives when JavaScript, WebGL, media or network conditions are imperfect?**

Then:

> **What exact asset, crop, string, font, color, position and state will Codex implement?**

That moves us from being able to **recognize** top-tier frontend work to being able to **architect** it.

The research now gives us an unusually complete combination of three perspectives:

> **the design-studio brain** from Behance, Dribbble, live creative sites and agency case studies; fileciteturn0file0

> **the experience-director brain** from the earlier Valtum/Oryzo/White Desert/Lando/Outfit/Inversa/Jasmine/Osmo investigations; fileciteturn0file1 fileciteturn0file2

> **and now the senior frontend-architecture brain** that understands how semantic DOM, responsive composition, GSAP timelines, native View Transitions, selective smooth scrolling, 3D asset preparation, GPU rendering, responsive media, variable fonts, mobile input, and perceptual color systems can be assembled without turning the product into a technology demonstration. citeturn4search0turn23search5turn18search5turn19search0turn11search1

The resulting engineering doctrine is:

> **HTML carries meaning.**

> **CSS establishes composition.**

> **JavaScript manages state.**

> **Motion explains relationships.**

> **WebGL is reserved for visual problems that genuinely deserve realtime rendering.**

> **Heavy visual computation is preprocessed whenever possible.**

> **Mobile is independently composed.**

> **Typography is a role-based system, not one pretty font.**

> **Color is perceptual and semantic, with a deliberately authored Valtum Green rather than a generic framework palette.**

> **Every enhancement has a simpler coherent state beneath it.**

> **And Codex will receive exact engineering decisions—not an instruction to somehow “make it look like Oryzo.”**
---
